Its adversarial review (2026-09-29; the live daemon's one standing loop, 512 tries, was an empty repository beside eight committed ones, which this branch leaves out): a git killed by a signal is transient and never "no HEAD" (read so, it passed the main/master refusal); a reftable ref write that failed on a full disk is transient; a tracked file replaced by a repository with no commit is left out as an untracked one is; a salvage ref's branch part is cut to 200 characters; salvage ignores a repository the daemon's environment names (`GIT_DIR` and the rest), which `daemon start` no longer passes on; a held retry baseline names what it left out; and a submit that could not read the checkout's top level leaves no job directory behind. Its independent review (2026-09-29, of ceacf18b): a job that ends before its next attempt finalizes (a cancel while it waits to retry, a retry whose preparation fails or is refused, a retry cancelled before launch or quarantined) names the last attempt, what its salvage could not save and any held start snapshot, which it records as that attempt's artifact when no retry was reserved, and never says only `cancelled before launch`; salvage's git and the daemon drop `GIT_LITERAL_PATHSPECS` and the other pathspec variables; any failure of a snapshot's seeding step, a git killed by SIGSEGV on a corrupt index included, reads the baseline without the seed in a fresh directory; and a launch-time main/master check that got no answer from git queues the job again rather than failing it.
- C-26.8 (2026-09-27, Max): an `ultracode` effort level for Claude conversations, and it is the default. The Claude CLI takes ultracode as its `ultracode` settings key, not as an `--effort` value, so an ultracode turn runs `--effort xhigh` with that setting; the default comes from the new `conversations.default_effort` policy key.
- C-26.8 (2026-09-28, review of 0eac67b4): the served effort is the provider's `get_settings` report, not the request; a policy default the account's catalog lacks goes ahead instead of failing the turn; the composer's efforts and default follow the picked model.
- C-26.9 (2026-09-28): tool approvals and question approvals (AskUserQuestion) wait without a time limit by default. `conversations.approval_wait_s` is null (no limit), or a positive finite number of seconds that limits only tool approvals; questions never time out. The former 3600-second default protected turn capacity that became uncapped by default on 2026-09-27.
- C-26.9 (2026-09-28): a question approval (AskUserQuestion) never times out; only a tool approval stops its turn after `conversations.approval_wait_s`.
- C-8.4, C-5.10: port #109 onto #76’s archive batches: retain interrupted sizing/archive progress, retry advancing deadlines promptly, rearm idle/cancelled passes last, prune service notices first, and prioritize orphan retention leases (2026-10-05).
- C-18.1: `status.json` judges a lane a probe holds as admission does, not dispatchable while the lease is held, counts probe leases toward the fleet cap when there is one, and adds `probe_state`, `probe_holder` and `probe_held`, reading the leases with the snapshot's rows; reset credits and alerts still judge the snapshot before the leases are laid (2026-09-30, the timer's snapshot read no probe leases).
- C-18.1, C-18.3: a busy Codex lane's usage is read beside its attempts, holding no lease, and only usage facts and an identity mismatch are published from it (2026-09-30, codex-4 held at one attempt by an unread lane and an unsettled reset-credit override; `docs/decisions/2026-09-30-busy-lane-usage-read.md`).
- C-11.2: a route evaluation reads the active attempts and the jobs with a parent or an active attempt, not every attempt and job the store keeps (2026-10-02: 2,722 attempts, 23 MB, and 2,625 jobs were read on every evaluation against 38 and 26 that could matter).
- C-15.8, C-18.1, C-18.4, C-23.26, C-16.2, C-17.1, C-11.8: an alert reaches a person, and no notice is parked for no one (2026-10-03). Timer alerts went to `alerts.operator_session`, which was never set, so `ping` parked them for the literal session `operator`, which no session holds and nothing listed: 1,637 alerts from 2026-09-19 to 2026-10-03, none read. There is now no default inbox; `status`, `status.json` and the menu show every alert in force; `doctor --live` pings without text; and `notices`, v1's spelling, lists every inbox, acknowledges one, or withdraws its undelivered service notices with a record of what went.
- C-9.10: Claude limit-reset cards and promotional credits are read per account with that account's own Claude Code login, shown by `status`, `status.json` and `subfleet cards`, and warned on five days before they are lost; nothing redeems or claims (2026-10-05: the card granted with Opus 5.5 on 2026-09-22 is usable until 2026-10-22 and, per help article 17007452, lost on cancellation or downgrade; `readings` holds no card or credit field; `docs/reports/2026-10-05-claude-reset-cards.md`).
- C-9.10, after the review of #132 (2026-10-05, its five P3 follow-ups): an `ok` read whose cards block is missing or ineligible no longer erases the cards the last listing read saw, so an unused card stays warned on; it is recorded beside them as `cards_unlisted`, the kept cards are judged lost from the read that last listed them, and their alerts say so. A login folder that answers as another account drops what it kept of the old one when the profile names the new one, so a failed claim-status read can no longer date the new account's cloud credit from the old claim. The heal turn's output is read as UTF-8 with bad bytes replaced, and its process group is killed however the turn ends. `subfleet cards --help` no longer says "read only": `--refresh` may spend a heal turn. `lapsed` stays "`claude_free` or `canceled`", either on its own, now with its grounds.
- C-9.10, after the reviews of #133 (2026-10-05): a login whose only card ended unused no longer reads "reset card ended unused (…); no reset card": "no reset card" is said only when the cards block lists none. The `card-lost` and `credit-lost` lines of `status` and `subfleet cards` name the card or credit; they had read `grant`, which those warnings do not carry, and named nothing. A card or credit recorded at a lapse whose end, as last seen, had already passed is recorded `expired`, not `lapse`, so its alert no longer says it was lost with its plan (a card listed unused that ended while the reads between listed no cards, or failed, and then the plan lapsed).
- C-23.31: a lane run is recognised by the `entrypoint` Claude Code stamps on its transcript entries, not by `promptSource` (2026-10-03). The desktop app now sends its prompts as `promptSource: sdk`, so v1's rule (one or two `sdk` prompts) read desktop sessions that one or two messages started as lane runs: 5,884 of one machine's 8,202 desktop and terminal sessions, 19 of them holding pending notices, were left out of `sessions` listings, nudges, cold revives and handoff. The same rule read 3,238 `claude -p` runs (probes, one-shots, resumed lanes) as sessions. The prompt rule now reads only prompts that name no entrypoint.
- C-5.11, C-5.12: the control loop gives a worker only the live attempts a pass could act on; a quiet running attempt costs one `stat` and its share of one statement a tick, not a pass that reads 8 KB of evidence and two receipts (2026-10-02, the compute-contention study's daemon profile).

## 1. Identifiers and vocabulary

- **C-1.1** A job id is `YYYYMMDD-HHMMSS-<slug>` in local time, the v1 format, unique in the store. `<slug>` is the `-n` name or the task name, lowercased, `[a-z0-9-]` only, at most 40 characters.
- **C-1.2** An attempt id is `<job id>/a<seq>` with `seq` starting at 1.
- **C-1.3** A lane id is `<provider>-<n>` (`codex-4`, `claude-3`). Lane ids are stable; a lane whose account or credential is rebound gets a new id and the old one is disabled.
- **C-1.4** An account key is `<provider>:<account id>`. For Codex the account id is the `account_id` from the home's `auth.json` claims when present, else the email; For Claude it is `<account_uuid>:<org_uuid>` as returned by the provider profile endpoint (C-10.6) for the lane's own credential; the email is a display label and never the key.
- **C-1.5** A request id is a caller-supplied string or a CLI-generated UUID4, at most 128 characters, printed back on submission.
- **C-1.6** Providers are exactly `codex` and `claude`. Model names in policy are short names (`astra`, `opus`) mapped to provider model ids in `policy.json`.
- **C-1.7** Timestamps in the store are ISO 8601 UTC with a `Z` suffix and second precision unless a column says otherwise. Epoch seconds from providers are converted on write.

## 2. State root and files

- **C-2.1** The state root is `$SUBFLEET_HOME`, default `~/.subfleet/`. Nothing is written outside it except: job workdirs and worktrees the caller named or the daemon allocated under `$HOME`, salvage refs inside the job's git repository, the `-o` export path, and `~/.claude/settings.json`, which only an explicit `daemon install --hooks` writes (C-23.25), never a job or a timer. The guard preflight's scratch home lives under `$SUBFLEET_HOME/tmp/` (C-23.23) and its verified-verdict markers under `$SUBFLEET_HOME/guard-cache/` (C-23.5), or under the directory `SUBFLEET_CODEX_GUARD_CACHE` names: an absolute value is the operator's explicit choice, a relative value must resolve inside the state root and is refused otherwise; the markers hold hashes, paths and timestamps only. The lane transfer (milestone 4, `docs/migration.md`) is the other exception: it edits the v1 roster it names, with a backup beside it, and relocates a transferred Codex home between `~/.codex-<n>` and `lanes/<lane id>/` under the state root (C-10.4).
- **C-2.2** Contents: `state.sqlite3` (plus WAL and SHM), `policy.json`, `lanes.json`, `daemon.sock`, `daemon.lock`, `daemon.log`, `jobs/<job id>/`, `lanes/<lane id>/` (provider homes when a lane has one), `worktrees/`, `conversations/<conversation id>/` (message text), `attachments/<sha256>.<ext>`, `catalog.json`, and `run/` (guardian control sockets, C-26.4).
- **C-2.3** `jobs/<job id>/` holds `prompt.md` (bytes as sent, written once), `manifest.json`, and per attempt `a<seq>/` with `stdout`, `stderr`, `start.json`, `exit.json`, `deliverable.md`, `lane.log`, and any provider raw stream. Files are created with mode 0600 and directories 0700.
- **C-2.4** A workdir under `/tmp` or `/private/tmp` is refused at submission (exit 7) unless `--allow-tmp`.

## 3. Store

- **C-3.1** One SQLite database, `journal_mode=WAL`, `synchronous=FULL`, `foreign_keys=ON`, `busy_timeout=5000`. The schema is `subfleet/store_schema.sql`; `store.py` applies it and records `schema_version`. Migrations are additive and numbered. Schema 7 adds restart-safe quarantine pacing and its notification outbox to attempts (C-5.7); migration records every numbered step and preserves existing jobs and their reserve authorizations, leaving old jobs unauthorized by default.
- **C-3.2** Every state transition of a job, attempt, notice, lease, or action is one transaction, and every transaction that changes state also inserts an `events` row in the same transaction. Retry-clock bookkeeping alone (quarantine recheck and notice retry due times) is atomic but creates no audit event; a changed containment census or any resolution still does.
- **C-3.3** Transactions never span a subprocess call, a network call, a sleep, or a filesystem sync other than SQLite's own commit syncs. Artifact copying, `ps`, `git`, and provider probes run outside transactions. SQL reads of the conversation store are allowed inside a main-store reservation transaction to keep the ownership recheck atomic with reservation. Native binding also takes the main-store lock before its conversation-store SQL write and synchronous=FULL commit; both paths acquire locks in that order, and catalog and filesystem work finishes before either lock.
- **C-3.4** Readers other than the daemon (the CLI in offline mode, the menu bar app, Logpile) open the database read-only. Only the daemon and the guardian receipt path write, and the guardian writes files, not rows. The desktop app (section 29) never opens the database; it reads `status.json` and `catalog.json` and changes state only through daemon ops.
- **C-3.5** The CLI refuses to write to a store whose `schema_version` is newer than it knows (exit 1 with both versions).
- **C-3.6** The daemon says who holds a store lock too long and who waits too long for one. The main store's lock, the conversation store's lock and its file-write guard (`conversation-files`, held across a file write's fsyncs, C-25.3) are `lockwatch.WatchedLock`s: re-entrant locks that record the thread holding them and when its outermost hold began, and change nothing about who gets them or when. A hold longer than `HOLD_REPORT_S` (2 s) is written to `daemon.log` once, with the Python stack the holder is running at that moment (a `subfleet-lockwatch` thread samples every 0.5 s), and again when it ends, with its length; a hold no sample caught says, when it ends, where it was held. A thread that has waited `WAIT_REPORT_S` (5 s) for a lock writes one line naming itself, the number of threads waiting and the longest wait, the holder and how long it has held, the waiter's stack and the holder's live stack, and then keeps waiting. Each lock and kind of report (`hold`, `released`, `wait`, and C-3.7's `read-pool` and `read-hold`) writes at most one line per `REPORT_EVERY_S` (60 s); the rest are counted, with the longest, in the next line, or in a summary line once the window has passed. A report that raises is dropped: it never fails an acquire, and never makes a release raise after what the lock guarded is done (the review of 5841d8b found an error in a report could make `transaction()` raise after its rows were committed). SIGUSR1 makes the daemon write the Python stacks of its threads (faulthandler writes at most 100) to `daemon.log` from `faulthandler`'s signal handler, so the dump arrives when every Python thread is stuck; it is registered as soon as the log is open (SIGUSR1's default action ends the process), and a log closes only once faulthandler cannot hold it (rule 3 below); a daemon taking the signal fresh first lets go any registration an earlier one left (tests build several), since registering over a live registration would not put the handler back over the SIG_IGN below. A daemon that registers the handler writes its identity to `daemon.lock` only once it has, with `"stack_dumps": true`, and rewrites it without the flag before it unregisters the handler (one that declines, below, writes its identity without the flag); whenever `faulthandler` takes the signal fresh (no daemon in the process is in `_ADVERTISED`, below), the daemon first sets SIGUSR1 to be ignored, with `signal.signal` on the main thread and libc's `signal()` off it, so what `faulthandler` puts back whenever it lets the signal go ends nothing; that replaces any SIGUSR1 handler the host set first (one in Python's table, off the main thread, makes the daemon decline instead), and a child the host starts after the last close inherits SIGUSR1 ignored. A host that sets a SIGUSR1 handler of its own while any daemon stands replaces the daemon's, which no daemon can survive: that is out of scope. `subfleet daemon stacks` sends the signal only to the identity `daemon.lock` records, as `daemon stop` does (C-5.3, C-5.4), and only when that lock says `stack_dumps`; otherwise it signals nothing and exits 69 with the fix (restart the daemon on this version, or `sample <pid> 5`). It reads the lock again just before the signal, and when the lock has changed since (a daemon that began to stop has dropped the flag) it signals nothing and exits 1, asking to run it again. A daemon built before the flag (the installed ad2c208 reports the same version, 2.0.0a0) is refused, not killed: the review of 5841d8b showed a process without the handler exits -30 on SIGUSR1. It waits up to `--wait` seconds (5) for the log to stop growing, and prints what was written; exit 69 when no daemon runs. Incident: 2026-09-25, a `list` whose statement takes 1 ms took 34-94 s, with 18 threads waiting on the store lock, and neither the log nor a native sample could say which Python code held it. While the interpreter runs, SIGUSR1 keeps a handler as long as any daemon in the process has a `daemon.lock` that says `stack_dumps` (`_ADVERTISED`, in the order they took the signal): `daemon stacks` reads a living pid and the flag, and with no handler its signal would be ignored and no dump would come. Three rules hold this, wherever an exception lands (a KeyboardInterrupt in an embedded host, at any line of a take, a close or a failed construction's cleanup), rather than by the order of their steps. (1) One routine, `_settle_sigusr1`, makes the registration match the set: the signal dumps into the stream of the member that took it last, so a dump for one member can land in another's log, and when none is left its handler goes, back to the SIG_IGN beneath it. Every take and every leave runs it, whoever held the signal, under one lock, so whatever an exception cut short in one, the next repairs; a take that raises leaves the set and settles again. (2) Membership follows the lock: a daemon joins just before it takes the signal, and marks itself leaving before it rewrites its lock without the flag; it leaves once its lock, read back by its path as `daemon stacks` reads it (a record that does not parse says nothing), no longer says `stack_dumps`, at its own close or, should an exception cut that short, at the next settle, even once its daemon object has been collected (a member that began to leave, or whose daemon is gone, is judged by what its entry keeps: its lock's path and whether it came to write the flag). A failed construction whose cleanup an exception cut short before it marked itself leaving is judged once its object is collected; until then it stays, its stream open, and dumps for others may land in its log. One whose lock may still say so (it came to write the flag, recording just before that write that it may, and the lock reads back with it, or cannot be read) and cannot be rewritten (EIO or ENOSPC) stays, holding SIGUSR1 or not, its log stream kept open, until its lock read back stops saying so (a later daemon at that root rewriting it); one that never came to write the flag leaves whatever its rewrite meets. (3) A log stream closes only when no hand-off can pick it (its daemon is not in the set) and faulthandler cannot hold it (`_HELD_STREAMS`: a stream joins before it is registered, the others leave once that registration has returned, all once the handler has gone; a stream in it closes only after a settle has moved the signal off it). A stream that may not close stays open while its daemon is a member or faulthandler may hold it (for good, if its lock keeps saying `stack_dumps` or cannot be read), which costs a descriptor, never a dump into a closed or reused one; once a settle has pruned it and moved the signal off it, it is collected when nothing else holds it (a handler an interrupted close left on its logger holds it until a later daemon given that logger's `id()`-based name lets the handler go). When the last member leaves, on either thread, and as the interpreter exits, faulthandler puts back the SIG_IGN beneath it, so a signal racing either ends nothing. A daemon that cannot make the signal safe neither registers the handler nor says `stack_dumps`, logging why, and never joins the set, so `daemon stacks` refuses it: when setting SIGUSR1 ignored fails, on either thread, and when it is built off the main thread while Python's own table holds a SIGUSR1 handler of the host's (Python's exit resets that to the default action before faulthandler lets go, and off the main thread nothing can put SIG_IGN in the table in its place). A second exception landing inside the recovery itself is out of scope (reviews of 02c6320, eac0706, 4fc5b49, 1efa0ef, 2300b43, 78bfbf0, 3cac1e8, 67b9adc, 6506619, 9a514a7, 674b99b and 8ebe6b6).
- **C-3.7** A read never waits for a writer. The daemon's store keeps `READ_CONNECTIONS` (6) read-only connections (`query_only`) beside its one writing connection, opened on first use. A read made outside a transaction (`Store.query`, `Store.one`) takes one of them for one statement instead of the store lock, so reads never queue behind a transaction, and SQLite steps them side by side. Snapshots may hold at most all but `STATEMENT_RESERVE` (2) of the read connections (`snapshot_share`, 4 of 6), so a one-statement read (a hook's lookup, the wait hub, the control loop) always has connections no snapshot can take. No read waits for a connection longer than `READ_WAIT_S` (1 s): after that it opens one of its own for that read alone and closes it after. Every such wait is counted (`daemon.status` `read_pool`) and written to `daemon.log` under C-3.6's rate limit (kind `read-pool`), naming the waiter, whether it opened its own, and every thread holding a read connection, for what and for how long. `Store.close()` closes a read connection only once its read has ended (it waits up to `CLOSE_WAIT_S`, 5 s; a read still going then closes its own when it ends) and refuses any read that starts after it, and one that was still opening a new pooled connection, which it does not wait for: that read closes the connection it opened. The write-ahead log is bounded: the writer sets `journal_size_limit` to `WAL_SIZE_LIMIT` (64 MiB), so each time SQLite restarts the log the file is cut back to that size, and the only checkpoints are SQLite's automatic ones, which are PASSIVE: a commit never waits for a reader, it checkpoints up to the oldest snapshot still open, and the commits after that snapshot ends checkpoint the rest and restart the log. The daemon runs no RESTART or TRUNCATE checkpoint, which would hold writers while it waited for readers. Since a long read holds the log back, a read connection in use longer than `HOLD_REPORT_S` (2 s) is written to `daemon.log` once with its thread's live stack (C-3.6's sampler, kind `read-hold`). A read made by a thread that holds the store lock goes to the writing connection, so a transaction still sees its own uncommitted rows. `Store.snapshot()` gives a block of reads one committed state without the store lock (inside a transaction, the transaction's state; a store without read connections holds the lock for the block instead). No transaction begins inside a snapshot on the same thread: it raises `SnapshotWriteError` before it writes, with or without read connections, because its reads would go to the snapshot and miss its own writes (the review of 5841d8b saw `add_artifact` record a duplicate that way); a commit that races a snapshot comes from another thread. `Timers.snapshot` and every capacity view read their rows inside one and build the view after it ends (`capacity.store_rows`, `Daemon._capacity_rows`, `Timers.view_rows`), so a view build holds no read connection; `Timers.snapshot` used to open a write transaction only to read, and the first version of this clause built each view inside its snapshot, so six views building at once held all six connections and every other read waited, with no bound and no log line (review of 5841d8b: 1.53 s with the store lock free). The hot reads are bounded by what they return, not by the history kept: a capacity view reads only the readings that can be the newest of their lane, scope and window (`Store.latest_reading_candidates`: per key, those at the greatest canonical `YYYY-MM-DDTHH:MM:SSZ` timestamp, plus every reading with another timestamp shape), not every reading, plus at most 24 hours of weekly provider history for status projections (C-11.9); a probe record is one step of the partial index `events_probe_holder` (`json_extract(data_json,'$.holder')` over the `probe.state` payloads `json_valid` accepts), and a gate's state the newest matching event found by SQLite (`json_extract`, newest first, one row), not every `probe.state` or `gate.state` event parsed in Python; batch labels and submission records are looked up by job id (`+kind`, so SQLite uses `events_job`), not by walking every `job.submitted` event; `sessions` filters session events by session in SQL, or takes only the newest per kind and session; and a view reads the reset-credit history, its reconciliations and the lane rows once (`ResetCredits.override_context`), not once per lane. Each rewrite answers exactly what the old form did; differential tests over randomized stores pin that (`tests/unit/test_store_contention_queries.py`). The one exception is a payload with a repeated key, which no writer makes (every payload is `json.dumps` of a dict): SQLite reads the key's first value and Python its last, so such a payload is found only under its first value, and Python checks what it parsed, so it is never returned or counted as another holder's or session's (review of 1516f3b). A probe record lookup takes only the newest row the index holds for a holder, so such a payload also hides its first value's older records: that holder gets none where the old walk found its previous one (review of 0e43105). A payload `json_valid` refuses is never filtered in SQL, and never given to `json_extract` (the session query guards it with `CASE`, since SQLite does not promise to test WHERE terms in the order written): a NaN or an Infinity, which `json.dumps` writes and `json.loads` reads, or a row that is not JSON at all. The partial index `events_not_json` hands every such row of the kinds asked to Python, which parses it as the old form did; a row that is not JSON, which no writer makes, is passed over where the old form raised. Both indexes are in `store_schema.sql`, so a store gets them the next time the daemon opens it, and neither can stop a store opening: `json_extract` is evaluated only for payloads `json_valid` accepts (an index on it alone fails to build over a single row that is not JSON). The store needs SQLite's JSON functions, built in since 3.38. `Timers` writes to the store only after releasing its own lock, which the control loop takes every tick. Incident: 2026-09-25, with the machine at load 85-100, a socket `list` whose statement takes 1 ms took 34-94 s while 18 daemon threads waited on the store lock; `docs/reports/2026-09-25-store-lock-contention.md` has the reproduction and the measurements.

## 4. Job and attempt state machines

- **C-4.1** Job states: `queued`, `running`, `waiting`, `succeeded`, `failed`, `cancelled`, `lost`. A `waiting` job has `wait_reason` in {`capacity`, `dependency`, `approval`, `uncertain`, `workspace`, `route`} and a `next_check_at`. Only `capacity` holds back the jobs behind it in its tier, and not while no lane can admit the job (C-11.8); a job waiting on its own `workspace` (C-6.8) or `route` (C-6.12) delays nothing else.
- **C-4.2** Attempt states and the boundary each one guards:

| State | Set when | Recovery if the daemon dies here |
|---|---|---|
| `reserved` | Lane lease, output lease, and any worktree lease taken; attempt row inserted | Release leases, mark attempt `failed` with class `unknown` and detail `reserved-no-launch`; the job may retry |
| `starting` | Guardian spawned; no `start.json` yet | If `start.json` holds a receipt, treat as `running`; else wait `start_grace_s` (10) then run containment (C-5.5) on the guardian pid and read `start.json` and `exit.json` again, because the guardian can write both and exit while the census runs: a receipt found then is taken on the next tick, `start.json` as above and `exit.json` as in the `running` row; else if the census is empty, release and retry; otherwise (not verifiable, or not empty) `quarantined`. A receipt is a file whose JSON value is neither empty nor false (`{}`, `[]`, `null`, `0`, `false` and `""` are none), on each tick and in this re-read alike |
| `running` | `start.json` read: pid, pgid, boot id, proc start recorded | If `exit.json` holds a receipt, move to `finalizing`; else if the guardian is alive by (pid, boot id, proc start), re-adopt; if its identity cannot be inspected, leave the attempt for the next tick; if it is dead, read `exit.json` once more (the guardian writes the receipt and then exits, so a receipt can appear between the first read and the liveness check) and move to `finalizing` when it is there; else run containment; empty means `lost`, else kill survivors and re-check, else `quarantined`. A receipt present at finalization always wins over a `lost` verdict reached before it was read (incident: 2026-09-05, under a load average above 45 a job whose provider had exited 0 was recorded `lost` with its receipt on disk) |
| `finalizing` | `exit.json` read; classification, salvage, export, notice pending | Re-run finalization idempotently; every step checks for its own completed output before acting; a census that is not verified empty waits out `exit_settle_s` (C-5.9) before quarantining |
| `succeeded`, `failed`, `interrupted`, `lost`, `cancelled` | Terminal | None |
| `quarantined` | Job ended; writers or their absence not yet verified | The daemon rechecks on C-5.7's pace. Verified-empty containment saves writable work or a turn's end snapshot and releases every attempt/job lease; live or unverifiable writers keep the quarantine. Operator commands remain for live writers and explicit overrides |

- **C-4.3** A job has at most one `accepted_attempt_id`. Acceptance and the notice insert are one transaction. A result from an attempt whose `seq` is lower than the job's current attempt, or whose state is not `finalizing`, is never accepted.
- **C-4.4** A job whose final attempt ends without an rc is `lost`, never `succeeded`.
- **C-4.5** `max_attempts` default 3; a new attempt is admitted only for classes `limited` (next candidate), `transient` (same lane after 60 s, once, then next candidate), `lost` on a read-only job, and a lane fault (next candidate). A lane fault is an `auth-dead` attempt (C-9.3) of a job with no lane pin, that is no conversation turn (its conversation decides a turn's failover, C-26.7), and that left its workspace as it found it: the job is read-only, or it is writable, its model never answered (`model_answered` false in the attempt's evidence, C-6.14: a writable attempt runs with permissions skipped, and what its model pushed, commented or wrote outside the worktree no tree shows; where an adapter cannot say, or read no event of the stream, so recorded `model_answered` null, it is taken to have answered), and its salvage at finalization (C-13.1) found the end tree equal to the attempt's start snapshot (no ref written, nothing left out, no error) and HEAD where the attempt began. Its lane is disabled as C-23.44 says, and the job moves on to the next candidate as for `limited`; the attempt is `failed` with class `auth-dead` and records `lane_fault` in its evidence (`class`, `lane_id`, `seq`, `model_answered`, and `workspace`: `read-only`, or `unchanged` with `head`). A job has at most one lane fault, and `max_attempts` does not count it: the lane fault grants the job one more attempt, so a job may have `max_attempts` attempts besides it (`Daemon._attempts_left`, which `_unlaunched` asks too), and a job allowed one attempt still moves on once: a writable job's lane fault ran nothing the job asked for (no model answered), and a read-only job's repeats only reading. (A revive's refused attempt may still have left its prompt and Claude Code's placeholder in the revived session's transcript, which the next attempt continues from; nothing else.) A job that moved on is no pilot (C-6.14): on a lane not proven it waits for Subfleet's own probe of that lane (C-11.4: the probe's fixed prompt, on the lane's credential, with none of the job's settings, `Daemon._needs_probe`), whose `auth-dead` is the lane's and disables it (C-23.44) and whose answer proves it. So with `admission.prove_idle_s` set, a job that moved on meets `auth-dead` again only on a lane a model answered on within the window, which points at the job. A second `auth-dead` of a job that already moved on ends it, `failed` with rc 5, and that lane is left enabled (C-23.44): two lanes refusing one job points at the job (a tool's `invalid_api_key` on stderr, a project setting that overrides the lane's credential), and a job that moved on without end would disable every lane it reached, where before this rule it disabled one. So no job's attempts disable more than one lane, as before (a probe disables only a lane whose own credential it found refused, C-23.44); a lane that really is dead is disabled by the next job that meets it, whose first `auth-dead` it is. `auth-dead` on a pinned job, or on one whose workspace changed, `cli-too-old`, `content-filter`, and any class on a writable job after the workspace changed require reconciliation first (C-13.3) (incident: 2026-09-30 15:43Z, an organisation disabled claude-5's Claude Code access; admission had put 37 jobs from about 20 sessions there within 40 s, and every one ended `failed` with rc 5 after a single attempt while lanes were coming back; C-6.14 now keeps such a burst to one attempt).
- **C-4.6** An attempt never changes lane or model. A new lane or model is a new attempt.

## 5. Process ownership and containment

- **C-5.1** The daemon spawns each attempt through `subfleet-guardian`, which calls `os.setsid()` so it leads a new session and process group, then spawns the provider command as its child with the same pgid, stdout and stderr redirected to the attempt directory, and an environment that contains the lane credential, the provider home, `SUBFLEET_ATTEMPT=<attempt id>`, `SUBFLEET_JOB=<job id>`, and `SUBFLEET_ROOT=<state root>`. The guardian never receives a secret in argv. It starts the provider only after it reads the byte `1` from its launch gate, the read end of a pipe the daemon passes it above descriptor 2, where the guardian's /dev/null standard streams cannot replace it. The daemon writes that byte only after it has recorded the guardian's identity; a guardian that reads anything else there, end-of-file included, starts nothing, writes neither `start.json` nor `exit.json` (C-5.2) and exits 127. A probe (C-11.4) and the turn a Claude lane's re-enrolment runs pass their guardians the same launch gate. The daemon runs at the default QoS: `daemon install` writes launchd `ProcessType` `Interactive`, because `Standard` clamps the daemon and every process it starts to the `utility` QoS (priority 20, which no thread can raise), and `Adaptive`, which launchd moves out of Background only on XPC activity (launchd.plist(5)), runs it darwinbg (priority 4), since its clients use a Unix socket. The guardian keeps its daemon's QoS and starts the provider under a `utility` QoS clamp (`/usr/sbin/taskpolicy -c utility`), which the provider and every process it starts inherit and none can raise, so agent work stays below the operator's apps however the daemon is scheduled; a thread's own QoS and `nice` reach no child, so neither would do. taskpolicy execs the provider in its own place: its pid, process group, environment and argv are those it would have had unclamped. When taskpolicy cannot spawn the provider it exits 66 with `taskpolicy: posix_spawn: <reason>` as the first line of the provider's standard error, and the guardian records that as C-5.2's spawn failure, rc 127 and a `spawn_error` in the words Popen's own failure has, and empties that standard error, as that failure leaves it, so no adapter reads taskpolicy's reason as the provider's. `SUBFLEET_PROVIDER_QOS=inherit` in the daemon's environment runs providers at the guardian's QoS instead, as does a host without taskpolicy.
- **C-5.2** Before creating the provider launcher, the guardian writes `start.json` = {`guardian_pid`, `pgid`, `boot_id`, `proc_start`, `started_at`} via temp file and rename. It spawns an isolated Python launcher that waits on a private pipe before executing any provider code, then republishes and fsyncs `start.json` with `child_pid` and `child_identity` = {`pid`, `boot_id`, `proc_start`}. Only after the file and directory are durable does it acknowledge the launcher, which execs taskpolicy/provider in the same PID. A crash or failed publication closes the gate; EOF prevents execution. Failed child identity inspection also refuses execution and reports a spawn failure with rc 127. The launcher error pipe closes on exec, preserving direct spawn-error reporting. After the provider exits it writes `exit.json` = {`rc`, `signal`, `finished_at`, `wall_s`, `child_pid`} the same way, then exits with the provider's rc. If the provider cannot be spawned, `exit.json` carries `rc: 127` and `spawn_error`.
- **C-5.3** Boot id is the stable `kern.bootsessionuuid` from `sysctl`; legacy receipts may carry `kern.boottime` seconds. Process start identity is the `lstart` column of `ps -p <pid> -o lstart=`. A pid is "the same process" only if both identities match. A legacy boot timestamp that differs from the current timestamp means unknown, not dead: macOS wall-clock correction can shift `kern.boottime` during one boot. Unknown identity never permits a signal or establishes death; a responsive daemon socket remains usable. Absent processes, reused start identities, and differing boot-session UUIDs still establish death.
- **C-5.4** The daemon may signal only a process group it recorded, and only after confirming the group leader's identity by C-5.3.
- **C-5.5** Containment collects live, non-zombie processes from the attempt's group; verified recorded identities and their recorded groups; parent-link descendants of these, the guardian and the published provider; cwd inside the attempt's workdir; and either exact `SUBFLEET_ATTEMPT=<attempt id>` or `SUBFLEET_ROOT=<state root>` marker in `ps -axEww -o pid=,command=`. An exact attempt marker holds even when the root marker is absent or different. An exact root marker holds when no nonempty attempt marker is visible; an explicit different attempt does not match through the shared root, so a parent turn and its waiter cannot hold a completed child run. A shared-cwd match is excluded only for a different visible attempt whose full process identity agrees across the initial table, marker confirmation and fresh cwd identity read; missing identity, PID reuse, or an unreadable confirmation retains the cwd source. Previously recorded identities, groups and descendants remain independent census sources. Attempt-id collisions can conservatively delay release. On some macOS hosts, environments are hidden for platform binaries (including shells, sleep and tail), and a rewritten process title can hide markers too: markers alone do not cover ordinary detached children. Each census uses one `ps -axo pid=,ppid=,pgid=,stat=,lstart=` snapshot for groups, identity and parent links, one marker `ps`, and one `lsof -nP -d cwd -F0pn` call capped at 10 seconds. Cwd matching uses kernel spelling (`folders.spelling`) for case, Unicode and firmlink aliases, resolves symlinks, and requires a whole path component: the workdir itself or anything inside it holds. A failed or malformed table, marker or cwd inspection is unverifiable and holds. A listed live process whose start identity is missing or whose identity capture fails is retained with unknown components; its pid, groups and descendants remain conservative census roots until death is verified. Unknown components never grant signal authority. No commands, environments or cwd paths are retained. Every observed descendant while the verified guardian or provider lives is durably recorded by pid, boot id, start time and group during C-5.11 inspections, kill and finalization censuses. Full censuses also retain other writers they observe. The newest 4,096 distinct identity/group observations are kept per attempt (about four full tables on the reviewed 1,100-process host, below 1 MiB of JSON); overflow holds conservatively until a proven reboot or explicit override, rather than forgetting a live root. Recorded groups remain roots after the observed member exits. A proven reboot discharges old groups. Proven reuse of the original attempt's recorded guardian leader discharges only that original attempt-group source. Retained or sampled groups hold until verified empty or a reboot is proven, even when their retained leader identity differs from the current leader (C-5.7). Descendants of retained group members are walked too. These roots grant no signal authority (C-5.6). Every cwd or marker match is inspected afresh for full identity and kernel process group, even when its pid was in the earlier table. Identity reads bracket the group lookup; a changed incarnation never inherits an earlier state, identity or group. Every distinct observed identity is retained before quarantine; a proven absent or zombie observation is removed. Group-read failure is unverifiable and leaves a conservative root. "Verified empty" requires every source to succeed and return no live writer, with no unresolved legacy child-publication evidence (C-5.7).

- **C-5.6** Kill protocol: mark the cancel request; SIGTERM the pgid; wait `term_grace_s` (15); SIGKILL the pgid; enumerate (C-5.5); SIGKILL surviving processes individually only when their ownership was recorded in the attempt's own group and their full C-5.3 identity is re-confirmed (a previously owned identity remains eligible after leaving that group); enumerate again, repeating for up to `kill_settle_s` (3) while the census is not verified empty, because signalled processes leave the process table only when the kernel finishes tearing them down and one read taken 50 ms after SIGKILL reports an exiting process as live under load (incident: 2026-09-05, two full-suite runs quarantined attempts that a rerun did not). The window ends early on the first verified-empty census and never widens what counts as contained. Verified empty releases the workspace and finalizes with `killed_by`. Anything else is `quarantined`.
- **C-5.7** A `quarantined` attempt keeps its leases (lane slot excluded), its workspace, and its worktree until the full C-5.5 census verifies its recorded writers, their process groups, their descendants, processes with cwd in the workdir, and every process matching the census-visible markers under C-5.5 gone. Automatic resolution is exactly as safe as `kill --confirm-dead`: the census is the same, no weaker and no stricter. Verified-empty quarantines release within one pace, including on the same boot; no reboot is required. The daemon rechecks automatically, at most once per attempt per policy `quarantine_recheck_s` (default 600 seconds in code, a positive finite number). A paced worker pass is offered at most once a second (or once per pace when shorter), checks at most eight due attempts, and runs no census on a request thread or inside a store transaction (C-5.11). The indexed due time is recorded before the census and survives restarts; preexisting quarantines are due immediately. The census has a shared residual: a writer never observed by lineage, recorded groups or descendant walks, outside the workdir when cwd is sampled, and running a platform binary whose environment macOS hides, or removing both markers from the census-visible environment (including hiding them by rewriting its process title), can be invisible after its parent exits. Changing the census-visible attempt marker to a different nonempty attempt also disables the root fallback (C-5.5). Starting a new session is not required. A detached platform shell with visible matching markers stays held; the residual applies only when no positive observation exists. A process observed even once through lineage, group, descendant, cwd or either visible marker is retained by identity and group after those matches disappear. A retained or sampled group never grants signal authority; both kill consumers require an identity-confirmed shape in the attempt's own group before adding ownership (C-5.3). A retained or sampled group holds both automatic and `kill --confirm-dead` resolution until it is verified empty or a reboot is proven, even when its retained leader identity differs from the current leader. The residual requires no positive observation; the writer may enter the workdir between samples and need not stay continuously outside it. Retention's separate `lsof` file-holder check protects the worktree only while an otherwise invisible process actually holds a file, mapping or directory there; intermittent append-and-close from an outside cwd can evade it. A legacy guardian without child publication or an exit receipt holds conservatively unless its provider identities were observed under the verified guardian launch identity, or captured as owned identities by its kill census. Those identities and groups must then be verifiably gone; no per-attempt `--force-release` or reboot is needed. With no such observation, the publication hold ends only with recovered child publication, an exit receipt, a proven reboot, or explicit override. On a proven reboot the census discards unqualified old guardian/child/group roots even if the current group leader has exited, while retaining matching current-boot identities and markers. A newly observed writer's identity is retained across rechecks and restarts, so an old reboot cannot exclude a matching current-boot writer. Saved boot observations are diagnostic, including malformed historical observation fields, and never pin an otherwise verified-empty census. An unverifiable census, including any enumeration or identity error, never resolves and is retried on the next pace. Each held recheck retains every observed process identity; only a changed census or newly retained identity creates a `quarantine.still_live` audit event. Verified empty uses `Daemon._resolve_quarantine`, the same path as `kill --confirm-dead`: salvage writable dispatch work or record a turn's end snapshot, then release every lease held by the attempt and its job. Receipts make restart recovery reuse the saved snapshot, and resolution commits once. `quarantine.self_resolved` records the census with no operator note and no override. A dispatch caller receives one release notice naming salvage saved or its failure; a turn receives one labelled Subfleet system line in its conversation timeline, delivered through a restart-safe outbox; retention keeps its job and manifest until the line is written. `status` lists quarantines older than one pace with age and the latest reason they remain held; `runs show` prints their evidence. Operator `kill` remains for live writers, `kill --confirm-dead` requests an immediate verified-empty recheck, and `--force-release` remains an explicit operator override recorded in `events`; automatic resolution never signals a process or uses force-release semantics.
- **C-5.9** After `exit.json` is read, a census that is not verified empty does not quarantine at once. The daemon allows `exit_settle_s` (3), measured from the first such census, for the guardian, which writes the receipt just before it exits, and for processes it already reaped to leave the process table, re-running the census on each tick. Survivors or an unverifiable census past the window quarantine the attempt with detail `writers remain after exit receipt`.
- **C-5.8** The daemon holds an exclusive `fcntl.flock` on `daemon.lock` and writes its pid, boot id, proc start, and version there. A second daemon exits 69. The CLI treats a lock whose recorded identity is dead as no daemon.
- **C-5.8a** A stopping daemon that has not ended `stop_grace_s` (30 s) after its stop was armed normally writes the Python stacks of up to 100 of its threads, newest first (faulthandler's cap), to `daemon.log` and calls `_exit(1)`. An independent kernel `ITIMER_REAL` timer, installed first, delivers SIGALRM with its default terminating action at `stop_grace_s + STOP_DUMP_MARGIN_S` (33 s). The nominal three-second margin normally lets a working faulthandler finish its dump and exit first. Scheduling or timer-installation delay, slow I/O, and blocked writes can consume that margin. SIGALRM ends any process still alive at the kernel deadline, including one whose ordinary dump is merely slow or descheduled, so diagnostics may be absent or incomplete even after successful faulthandler arming. It needs no GIL, successful `Event.set`, watcher wakeup, or new thread at stop time. If the kernel timer cannot be installed, arming exits 1 immediately instead of continuing without a bound. `close()` still releases `daemon.lock` only after every pool has drained, so the store keeps one writer. The bound ends the process instead of releasing the lock early, and the kernel frees the flock with the process. No guardian is signalled, and forked children do not inherit the interval timer; the next daemon adopts attempts still running (C-4.2), as after a SIGKILL. One nonblocking claim, created at startup and never released, permits only the first `arm` invocation to install the timers. A handler interrupting that invocation returns without setting the event until arming completes, so it cannot deadlock the owner before timer installation. Later invocations cannot replace either deadline. The stop is armed on the thread that begins it, before `stopping` is set: `close()` and the end of `serve_forever` call `arm` directly. A SIGTERM or SIGINT arms when the main thread runs its handler; a thread holding the GIL can delay that handler indefinitely. The handler skips `Event.set` once the event is set. A signal nested inside `set` remains bounded by the already installed timers. The startup watcher arms when anything else sets `stopping`; it is not the fallback. The stopping-line write follows timer installation and can itself block, so the line is not guaranteed to precede the dump. launchd's `ExitTimeOut` (which `daemon install` writes as `stop_grace_s` + 10 s; with none set, `launchctl print` reports 5 s) and `subfleet daemon stop` each wait `stop_grace_s` + 10 s for stops they initiate. After its wait, `daemon stop` re-verifies the identity (C-5.4) and sends SIGKILL. Raw signals and self-initiated stops do not start either external backstop. The grace outlasts probe containment during a stop: SIGTERM, up to `term_grace_s` of census polling, then SIGKILL and one census. The descriptor for the dump is opened when the daemon starts, so a daemon stopping because it ran out of descriptors can still write. A daemon built inside a test process arms nothing. The bound can end a stop that is slow but healthy, including the session mirror's flag publish, which is not crash-safe; launchd's SIGKILL, a crash or an operator's kill already cut that window. (Incident: 2026-09-25, pid 93697 shut its socket and then waited without a deadline on pool threads parked in `RLock.acquire`. It held the lock until a restart at 19:26Z, while every client was refused and every replacement exited 69; `docs/reports/2026-09-25-daemon-stop-wedge.md`.)
- **C-5.10** A worker that raises is retried with backoff, not on the next tick. The control loop offers every live attempt a pass could act on (C-5.11), every pending export, and admission to the worker pool each tick (50 ms), and a worker that raised used to be eligible again at once, so a cause that fails fast (a repository git refuses, a store error) was retried, and logged, twenty times a second for as long as it lasted. Now a key the control loop offers (an attempt id, `export:<job>`, `admission`, `timer-recovery`, `retention`) is not run again until 0.5 s after its first consecutive failure, doubling to a 60 s ceiling; a success clears the count; a running attempt's pass that stops at its pacing gate or cannot inspect, while an inspection that raised has not yet run to its end, is neither (C-5.11). A one-shot request is never paced: `resolve:<job>`, an operator's `kill --confirm-dead` or `--force-release`, is offered once, by the request itself, so holding it back would drop it after the caller was told it was accepted; it runs whenever it is asked for and its failure is logged each time. `daemon.log` gets the line on the 1st, 2nd, 4th, 8th (each power of two) consecutive failure, with the count and the next retry, and still only the exception's type, never its message (a provider or keychain error can carry a secret). The count is in memory: a restart forgives it, and recovery re-offers every live key as before (incident: 2026-09-20, `worker 20260920-081856-native-other-disability-opus/a1 failed: SalvageError` forty times in `daemon.log`; that cause was a git timeout, which paced itself at its own 15 s cap, and nothing in the daemon would have paced one that failed at once).
- **C-5.11** A running attempt or probe costs the daemon a bounded number of subprocesses, however often it is offered, and a tick costs a bounded number of store statements, however much history is kept. The control loop still looks at every live attempt each tick (C-5.10), and a pass still reads its exit receipt, its cancel request and its wall limit and inspects a running attempt's processes as C-5.12 says, from one table every running attempt shares. What the loop gives the worker pool is only the attempts a pass could act on: one statement a tick (`LIVE_TICK`) reads, for every live attempt, its state and where its receipts are, and its job's cancel request, start and wall limit, naming no column SQLite keeps on overflow pages; a `running` attempt with no file at `exit.json` (one `stat`), no cancel request, its wall limit not reached, no inspection due (C-5.12), no failed or deferred pass to repeat (C-5.10; offered so that a pass that succeeds clears the failure count, which a withheld attempt would leave stale) and, if this daemon started its guardian, that guardian not ended, is not given a worker that tick, because its pass would read those same facts and return having done nothing; the answer is for the tick it is given in, so a due time or a receipt that arrives after it is seen on the next tick, 50 ms on. Every other live attempt, and every doubt about what a pass would do (a `stat` that fails for a reason other than absence, a job row it cannot find, any error the filter itself raises), is given one as before, so an error is the pass's to raise and C-5.10's to pace, never the control loop's; a doubt about whether v1 still owns the attempt is a no (migration principle 3): it is given no pass, asked again next tick, and logged on the 1st, 2nd, 4th and later powers of two, and a pass that finds the attempt `imported_external` returns having done nothing; a `stat` that hangs holds the control thread, which is the cost of not reading the receipt on a worker. `imported_external` is read from the attempt's evidence until it says no, and an attempt this daemon owns is remembered as such until it is no longer live (property: in every combination of state, receipt, cancel request, wall limit, inspection clock and pending retry, an attempt the loop withholds is one whose pass returns having acted on nothing, committed nothing and changed no pacing; measured 2026-10-02 on `main`, whose control loop has no conversation or turn keys, with `tools/measure_idle_cost.py --running 26 --history 2500 --waiting 4 --evidence-bytes 8192` on the live daemon's interpreter build, three alternating rounds at a load average between 135 and 300: the saturated window took 20.2%, 17.7% and 16.3% of a core before, 11.9%, 12.2% and 4.8% after). A probe's wait loop, which has no shared table, reads its receipt, its job and its deadline every pass, and asks `ps` whether its guardian is still the recorded process (C-5.3) once every `inspect_interval_s` (1 s), and once more in a check that finds a new group member, to confirm the leader before recording it (C-5.4). It records the probe's owned group members from C-5.5's group source alone, one `ps -axo pid=,pgid=,stat=,lstart=` snapshot, inside that check and at most every `OWNED_CENSUS_INTERVAL_S` (0.5 s, so with the default interval on every check), capturing an identity (C-5.3) only for a member it has not recorded or whose start has changed. A running attempt's inspection that raises leaves no due time, so the retry C-5.10 schedules repeats it in full: it asks for a table at once, and is given the last one while that is less than an interval old (C-5.12). Until an inspection has run to its end, a pass that could not inspect (another attempt's read was running; the table, its boot identity or a table to record the group from could not be read; the guardian's liveness was unknown) or that stops at the pacing gate returns `DEFERRED`, which neither clears C-5.10's count nor adds to it, so a cause that lasts is retried and logged on C-5.10's backoff (review of the merge, 2026-09-26: such a pass returned normally, which counted as recovery, so whenever a `ps` read took 0.5 s or more each retry landed inside the next read and the count went 1, 0, 1). The boot identity is read as C-5.12 says; the full census, whose marker source reads the environments macOS exposes, is taken only to decide a release, kill, loss or quarantine. Pending exports are found with one statement (`PENDING_EXPORTS`), not a lease lookup per accepted job. A `wait` re-reads the store only as C-15.5 says, after a top-level transaction has committed a change or every `WAIT_RECHECK_S` (1 s), through one reader for all waiters; a worker pass wakes waiters only when the store's generation moved while it ran, and makes that decision before its key is released. Incident: 2026-09-24, the per-tick inspection (three subprocesses per running attempt per 50 ms tick, and every 0.5 s a census that dumped every process's environment, 2.3 MB), waiters re-reading their jobs on every worker pass, and an export sweep of 266 statements a tick saturated the store lock; `daemon.status` took 38.8 s, every client timed out, and shutdown hung until SIGKILL (docs/reports/2026-09-24-daemon-inspection-wedge.md). (Merged 2026-09-26 with C-5.12, which replaced this clause's per-attempt `LIVENESS_INTERVAL_S` pacing of running attempts with the shared table.)
- **C-5.12** Inspection is rationed; decisions are not. Each tick (50 ms) looks at a running attempt's exit receipt, its cancel request and its wall clock, which are files and rows, and a pass reads its `start.json` and `exit.json` (C-5.11 says which attempts are given a pass). Its processes are inspected at most once per `inspect_interval_s` (1), from one process table (C-5.5's snapshot) that every running attempt shares; an inspection that raised is repeated at C-5.10's backoff, on the same table while it is less than an interval old (C-5.11). A table expires one interval after its read began. An attempt falls due when the table it was last given expires (one this daemon launches, one interval after it started the guardian, so that its first table is read after the guardian started; one it recovered, when it first asks), and is given the last table read if that expires later, which means a table read after the one it last had and less than an interval before it fell due; otherwise it reads a new one, and that is the only time a read begins. So the table's `ps` begins at most once per interval however many attempts ask and however long it takes (when it takes longer than an interval, reads run back to back, one at a time), and only the inspection that reads waits for it, and for the table's boot identity, which the reader reads before any attempt is given the table: one that finds a read running returns and asks again on its next tick, so a slow or hung `ps` or `sysctl` holds one worker, not one per running attempt (except the `kern.boottime` read below, which the legacy attempts given the table wait for together), and delays only the reading attempt's per-tick reads. The reader is whichever attempt asks first once the table has expired, and the control loop offers running attempts in the order the store lists them, so one attempt can be the reader interval after interval and wait out every slow read. An abnormal guardian death is seen by the first table read that begins after it, which begins by the first tick one interval after the death that finds a free worker, or as the read then running ends if that read (`ps`, then the boot identity) is slower than that, once that read and one fresh read (below) have run. A read that failed is rationed like one that worked, and an inspection given a failed read asks nothing singly and decides nothing, as a guardian that cannot be inspected decides nothing (C-4.2), so that a `ps` outage costs at most one capped read per interval rather than one per running attempt. The table says that the recorded guardian is alive (C-5.3), when it is, and lists the members of its group, whose identities are recorded as owned (C-5.6) only while that same table shows the recorded guardian alive and leading the recorded group; the environment scan is not run for this, because only group members are recorded. A shared table may say "alive" and nothing else, and says it only where `liveness` would (C-5.3): on an exact match of pid and start, and of the boot identity or, for a guardian recorded with a legacy boot timestamp (its UUID `sysctl` failed once as it started), a timestamp that C-5.3 matches to the table's `kern.boottime` seconds. A table reads those seconds the first time a legacy record needs them and keeps them for its life, failed or not, so every legacy attempt given the shared table shares one `sysctl`, and one that asks while it runs waits for it. A guardian it does not show alive is asked about afresh (C-5.3's own reads) before anything follows, and one that the fresh reads find alive has its group recorded from a table read for it alone, one `ps` outside the shared read (rare, since a table read after the guardian started shows it); and every census that decides (start grace, a dead guardian, the kill protocol and its settle window, C-5.9's window, quarantine resolution, probe containment) is read fresh, never from the shared table. The shared table's reader reads its boot identity just after `ps`, and a census's table the first time a live pid needs it; each reads it once, whatever the answer: every attempt given the shared table shares that read, and when it fails no attempt asks singly and nothing is decided, as for a failed `ps`; a UUID read that fell back to `kern.boottime` seconds has failed for every attempt recorded with the UUID, which C-5.3 could only call unknown against them. One boot-identity read is reused for 5 s, and only a boot session UUID is: a failed read is not remembered, and neither are the `kern.boottime` seconds that a failed UUID read falls back to, against which every process recorded with the UUID would compare as unknown and so could not be signalled; a process whose start matches is never called dead on a boot-identity mismatch until a fresh read agrees. `procs` starts `ps` and `sysctl` without `fork` (`close_fds=False`, the condition under which CPython uses `posix_spawn` on macOS), because a fork costs in proportion to the daemon's memory; the daemon's other process starts (`git`, the guardian) still fork. Every descriptor the daemon opens is close-on-exec, but macOS has no `pipe2` and no `SOCK_CLOEXEC`, so a pipe or socket that another thread is creating at that instant can be inherited, by a `ps` or `sysctl` only, which holds it until it exits within its 10 s cap: the most that follows is a close seen that much later (`close_fds=True` would close it, but CPython on macOS then forks). The one descriptor this package hands down on purpose, the guardian's launch gate, is closed by the guardian before it inspects anything. The per-tick check for exports to retry is one query driven from `leases`, and the desktop login hint (C-10.3) is parsed only when `stat` says the file changed. What slows for every attempt is only how soon an abnormal guardian death is noticed and how soon a new group member is recorded as owned, and the attempt whose inspection is reading the table waits out that read, `ps` and `sysctl`, before its own per-tick reads; and a member missed is never signalled individually (C-5.4): while it stays in the group `killpg` reaches it with the rest, and one that has left the group is quarantined (incident: 2026-09-20, with four attempts running the daemon held a full core, about fifteen process starts a second: three to ask about each healthy guardian on every tick and a census of `2 + 3N` every 0.5 s; the laptop's thermal guard, which pauses the operator's command-line processes at or above 20% CPU while the lid is closed on battery, SIGSTOPped it for two hours; measured 2026-09-24 with `tools/measure_idle_cost.py`, production tick, timers off, `origin/main` and the first version of this clause (845d663): idle 7.8% and 0.5% of a core; four running attempts and twelve waiting jobs 46.5% and 3.9%, with the `ps` it started 71.5% and 2.7%; measured again 2026-09-26, after this clause's review, at a load average near 7: idle 3.0% and 0.2%; four running and twelve waiting 29.0% and 1.9%, with its `ps` 49.3% and 2.1%).

## 6. Admission

- **C-6.1** Submission validates before touching the store: workdir exists and is not under `/tmp` unless allowed; prompt file readable; `-o` path's directory exists; sandbox in {`read-only`, `workspace-write`}; `--task`/`--tier` valid per policy or `-m`/`-a`/`-H` pins consistent; a read-only job with `-o` inside `-C` is allowed (the daemon writes it).
- **C-6.2** The payload digest is SHA-256 over the canonical JSON of: prompt bytes (as base64), resolved workdir, git head of the workdir or null, task, tier, pinned model, pinned lane, sandbox, sorted exclusions, resolved `-o` path or null, `allow_desktop`, policy hash. A repeated request id with the same digest returns the existing job id (exit 0); with a different digest, exit 2.
- **C-6.3** Admission of an attempt is one transaction that: chooses the lane and model from the decision (section 11), takes the lane slot lease `lane:<lane id>:slot:<n>` (a turn's is `lane:<lane id>:slot:turn-<n>`, C-26.9), takes the output lease `out:<resolved path>` if `-o` is set and not already held by this job, takes the worktree lease if writable (on the write target, C-6.5: `worktree:<target>` for a detached writer, and for a conversation turn a row of its own, `worktree-turn:<target>:<job id>`, beside which it needs `worktree:<target>` free, C-24.5; a session is not a place and takes no lease), inserts the attempt as `reserved`, and records the decision. Launch happens after commit. The decision is evaluated before the transaction, off the store lock, on rows read in one read snapshot (C-3.7); the evaluation `_prepare_route` makes to decide on a probe is the one reserved on, so a job is evaluated once, not twice, before its reservation (a transient retry's pinned pair is also evaluated, as before, by `_retry_waits_on_a_slot`; and when the lane chosen is not the job's to probe, C-11.4's choice evaluates it again with lanes left out, and an evaluation again after a moved route leaves the same lanes out). The transaction never builds or reads a capacity view, reads no table whole but the small lane, lease and reset-credit ones, and never calls `evaluate`. It checks the decision on rows it reads with a few small queries (`Daemon._route_rows`, `route_check.still_stands`): every lane row, marked and merged as a view marks and merges them (`capacity.mark_desktop`, `Timers.merge_lane`, `capacity.credential_latched`); the attempts in flight (`attempts_live`); the probe leases; the readings added since the snapshot, by id (a reading id is reused only after the newest reading is deleted, so while the snapshot's newest reading stands, every reading added since has a greater id); the closures not released (`closures_active`, and by id any the snapshot held with an empty `released_at`); the lanes a confirmed reset-credit override (C-23.17) covers now, decided for the lane rows now at a view's clock (`Actions.override_context`), since whether one covers a lane turns on the lane's account key and home, so a lane enrolled or moved since can gain or lose one with no action changing (the review of 9dd4f35 found a `hard` job placed without its probe on a lane enrolled meanwhile); and the parents of the jobs a parent cap counts. The decision looks at the lanes of the models it walks (`scheduler.model_lanes`: each model's provider's, or the one lane the job's pin names), and only those are judged. A lane whose values of the fields `evaluate` reads (`scheduler.LANE_FACTS`), readings, open closures, attempts in flight in the job's pool, slot block and override are what they were, and whose own clock has not reached its horizon, is judged as it was; each other lane is judged again, alone, in memory, at the check's clock (`scheduler.judge_lane`), and ranked against the candidates that did not change (`scheduler.rank_key`). A lane's horizon (`capacity.lane_horizons`, recorded off the lock with the early decision) is the first instant at which the clock alone could change how that lane is judged: a reading it counted as fresh stops being so (its `observed_at` plus `reading_ttl_s`, or its `resets_at`), a latest provider window renews even when its observation is already stale (C-11.3; a reset at or before its own observation is already unusable and adds no clock), a reading observed after the view's clock becomes fresh, or a closure ends. `scheduler.evaluate` reads the clock through those usage/reset guards and a closure's `until_at`, so before its own horizon a lane whose rows did not change is judged as it was, whatever any other lane's clock does. Each of these can close a lane, not only open one: the review of 5841d8b found a reading going stale, and the review of f48df54 an override ending (the lane's reading, held out, is below the floor) and a reported closure on a reserved model ending (the lane's slack behind a probe becomes `unmeasured`, C-11.7). `Timers.enrich_view`, which relabels an overridden lane's readings, and `_pick`, which holds them out, both decide an override at the view's clock, so one view never does the one without the other; a lane whose override began or ended since is judged again with its readings held out or put back. A fleet or parent cap that began or ended refuses or frees every lane alike, so every lane of the walk is judged again then; and when the model the decision chose has no candidate now and the chain goes on, the next models' lanes are judged, as `evaluate` walks them. The check then makes the decision an evaluation of the rows the transaction sees, at its clock, would make, exactly and in `evaluate`'s order (`route_check.as_now`): each changed lane's verdict, detail and evidence put in place, each reading it carries aged and labelled at the check's clock (a `provider` reading past `reading_ttl_s` is `stale-provider`), `evaluated_at` that clock's second; so the probe a lane needs (C-11.4) is read from now's detail, and a wait recorded on the decision is clocked by now's verdict (C-6.10). It goes on with that decision, the same lane or another, or none. It does not decide, and the transaction rolls back before it has written anything, only when it cannot rebuild what the decision rests on: the job's pin now names another lane (a re-enrolment moved the binding it follows, C-11.2); an evaluation now would raise, as the one made again off the lock then does; a policy loaded since; a clock earlier than the instant the early decision's view was built at (a clock that stepped back); a reading the snapshot held that was deleted (retention deletes readings only with a pruned job's attempts); or a row it cannot read (a timestamp that does not parse), which the evaluation made again then settles as C-6.12 says; a check that raises on every try settles the job so too (a `route` wait naming the error, backed off), never leaving it `route-moved`. Then the route is evaluated again, off the lock, and checked again, up to `ROUTE_TRIES` (3) times in one pass; then the job keeps its place (`route-moved`, C-6.11: the later jobs it competes with wait behind it, C-6.9) and the next pass, which follows at once, looks again. A clock or a row on a lane the decision does not look at never sends it back: on d04b8b3 the check refused any decision past the fleet-wide horizon, and sixty Claude lanes' staggered readings, refreshed on a 120 s cycle, refused every check of a Codex job three seconds after its evaluation, thirty passes running (review of d04b8b3); on 5d14f98 it refused a cap that began or ended, and a timer probe's lease (which counts toward the detached fleet's cap, C-6.4) held at each evaluation and gone at each check refused a job every try (review of 5d14f98). Age alone voids nothing: `ROUTE_REUSE_S` (5 s), which sent every evaluation older than that to be made again inside, is gone, since the check is exact and, on 2026-09-26, the store lock was waited for longer than that. The transaction also finds the job no attempt in flight or quarantined before it reserves one (C-26.9's two passes never take the same job, and this makes that a rule of the store). Two changes the check does not see, as the evaluation it replaces did not: `Timers` updates its in-memory probe status (`Timers.metadata`) just after the commit that records the probe, so a check made between the two reads the status from before the probe; and a commit by another process, which C-3.4 allows only as a hand edit of a running daemon's store. `daemon.status` counts under `admission.route_evaluations`: checks that kept the early decision's choice, a lane or none (`reused`), and checks that chose again from the lanes judged again (`rechosen`); evaluations made again after a check could not decide (`again`), and why (`moved`: a pin that names another lane now, an evaluation that would raise, or what the check rests on could not be rebuilt; `old`: a clock earlier than the view's; `error`: the check raised); jobs left for the next pass (`deferred`); and the lanes checks judged again (`rejudged`). Incidents: with the daemon held to 5% of a core, evaluating a whole capacity view inside this transaction held the store lock for 13.5 s (`docs/reports/2026-09-25-store-lock-contention.md`); on 2026-09-26, at load 110 to 170 with 48 jobs queued, it held it for 7.7 to 12.6 s at a time while other threads waited up to 43 s, because some commit had landed since the early evaluation nearly every time (`docs/reports/2026-09-26-admission-latency.md`).
- **C-6.4** Caps with defaults in `policy.json`. The concurrency caps are `max_active_attempts` (attempts in flight across the fleet, a probe's reservation included), `max_in_flight_per_lane` (detached attempts on one lane), `max_in_flight_unmeasured` (on a lane with no `provider` reading fresher than `reading_ttl_s`, 120; with it unset, `max_in_flight_per_lane` applies there too) and `max_active_attempts_per_parent` (attempts under one ancestor, C-6.9). The submit counts are `max_child_jobs` (children one parent may create in all) and `max_writable_per_session` (live writable jobs one session may hold, C-6.5). Each of these six is null by default, which is no cap, and the shipped policy says so; a positive whole number sets one, and anything else is refused naming the key. Until 2026-09-27 the concurrency caps were 4, 2 and 1, a fixed 1 beneath the unmeasured cap that no policy could raise, and a parent cap of 1 that the policy did not show (Max: "we should uncap everything and instead use prioritization"); until 2026-09-28 the submit counts were 8 and 8 (Max: "remove *all* caps"). `policy.cap` and `policy.lane_slot_cap` are their only readers. What bounds the fleet without them is the providers' own limits and `headroom_floor` (C-11.3, C-4.5), and C-11.3's load band, which spreads work without refusing it; the machine guard (C-6.13) can be turned on. The other bounds are not counts and keep their defaults: `max_wall_s` 21600 per job; `max_attempts` 3; `max_tokens_observed` optional per job; `workspace_git_timeout_s` 60, `worktree_add_timeout_s` 180, and `workspace_retry_max` 8 (C-6.8).
- **C-6.5** Refusals are exit 7 with the fix named: API-key home, workdir under `/tmp`, writable job that would write in a checkout on `main` or `master` (C-13.2), `-o` path held by a live job of another request id, a symlink loop in the requested `-o` path, a writable job for a session id that already has one running from another instance, a second writable job on the same worktree (a detached in-place job where a writable conversation turn is live, queued or running, or its attempt quarantined and its `worktree-turn:` row still held, is such a second writer; turns among themselves are not, C-24.5), a writable job beyond `caps.max_writable_per_session` when a policy sets it (none by default, C-6.4), a child beyond `caps.max_child_jobs` when a policy sets it (none by default), an isolated review (`-I`) that would inherit `CLAUDE_CODE_MANAGED_SETTINGS_PATH` or a remote managed-settings variable, and an isolated Codex review whose home carries managed requirements or a non-empty system or project config layer (C-23.3). This list is the index of refusals: a new refusal is added here, not in a clause of its own. What holds a writable job is where it writes. The write target of an in-place job is the real path of the checkout that contains its workdir (`git rev-parse --show-toplevel`), or for a conversation's turn outside git its folder (C-26.10), so `/repo` and `/repo/sub` are one target and two linked worktrees of one repository are two; a target is spelled one way (`folders.canonical`: `~`, symlinks, `.` and `..` resolved, and each name as the kernel stores it, whatever case or Unicode form it was typed in), so on a case-insensitive volume `~/Scratch` and `~/scratch` are one target, as a conversation's workspace is (review of 5e9f2fbd, P3-4); the target of any other writable job is the worktree the daemon allocates for it (C-6.6), which no other job shares. One instance of a session may therefore hold any number of writable jobs on distinct targets, with no count unless a policy sets `caps.max_writable_per_session`, a backstop against a runaway caller and not a throttle (C-6.4: no count bounds what runs by default). An instance is a process: the caller's pid with its boot id and start time (C-5.3), read by the daemon at submit and recorded with the target in the `job.submitted` event, so a recycled pid is never mistaken for it and no schema change is needed. A submission is refused when the session has a live writable job whose recorded instance is another process that is still running (the twin), and also whenever the two cannot be told apart: the submission carries no pid, either process cannot be inspected, or the live job recorded no instance. An instance that is provably gone (no such pid, or the pid now belongs to a later process) is not a second instance: a session resumed after a restart or an account switch is one instance whose detached jobs survived, and it may dispatch again. A revive is always another instance of the session it continues (C-23.54), so a revive and any other writable job of that session refuse each other as before.
