Both questions need `ps` or `git`, so they are answered under the submit lock before the inserting transaction, which re-checks with SQL alone and refuses any live job the submission was not cleared against (C-3.3) (incident: 2026-09-20, a session handing five stalled threads to parallel lanes was refused every writable job after the first with "caller_session is held by a live job", whichever worktree it named; the code compared session ids and never instances, so it refused the one instance this clause never meant to stop). Milestone 9 adds: a socket `submit` of `kind: "turn"` or a `turn:` request id (C-26.1); a person-only operation from an agent (C-25.6); a writable turn whose workspace contains the state root, `~/.claude`, `~/.codex`, a lane home, or the guard's directory, and a turn on `main` or `master` without `allow_main` (C-26.10). Output paths, including their parents and absent destinations, compare by the shared folder identity at conflict checks, reservation, export and recovery: resolve the kernel spelling, then NFC on a sensitive volume; on an insensitive volume strip Unicode default-ignorables, equate dotless i with i, and use NFD(casefold(NFD(path))). Display spelling stays as supplied and old raw lease aliases still exclude another owner. Known conservative gaps lean toward over-collision: full casefold expansions, dotless i on APFS, default-ignorables on APFS, and canonical normalization on sensitive volumes can refuse distinct files. An OSError, including PermissionError, ELOOP, EIO or TimeoutError, falls back to the exact supplied string and is logged once per path rather than stopping unrelated jobs. This may under-collide aliases while the filesystem is unreachable, matching 2.1.11. An identical stored output path remains refused even if a quarantined owner holds only its canonical lease. External path replacement or directory renaming during a live lease remains an under-collision limit of path-based ownership (audit L4). Identity answers are memoized only for one submit, admission pass or export, outside the main-store transaction, with no stored column or back-fill. For legacy alias lease pairs, the oldest acquired_at wins, breaking ties by holder then lease key; other holders persist export_error: superseded by <job>, append that reason to their notices, and release all their job and accepted-attempt leases at export. Every output export either records an export artifact or export_error, including missing ownership or deliverables, and releases those leases.
- **C-6.6** A writable job runs in a worktree the daemon allocates under `worktrees/<job id>/` from the caller's repo at its current head, unless the caller passes `--in-place`, in which case the daemon takes the worktree lease `worktree:<realpath>` on the checkout that contains the caller's directory (C-6.5; a conversation turn, always in place, holds a row of its own there instead, C-24.5); the provider still starts in the directory the caller named. A Claude job in an allocated worktree starts at the caller's place in it (a job run from `repo/pkg` starts in `<worktree>/pkg`), that place named as git spells it (`git rev-parse --show-prefix`, so a directory given in another case, in decomposed Unicode or through the `/System/Volumes/Data` firmlink is the committed one), and a resume starts where its source attempt's launch started; a Codex job starts at the worktree's top, because Codex's workspace-write sandbox writes only under its working directory; a directory the caller's head does not hold (untracked or ignored) is not in the worktree, so the job starts at its top.
- **C-6.7** `prompt.md` holds the caller's prompt bytes exactly as submitted; it is the digest input and is never rewritten. Prepended text is applied in two places and recorded separately: at submit, the daemon prepends to a workspace-write job that is not in place a note naming its worktree, the caller's checkout it must never write and the caller's place in the repository (with or without `--no-preamble`), and before that the write template unless `--no-preamble` (the sandbox is known at submit); the manifest records whether the template was given (`preamble`), and a resume keeps its source's choice; at launch, the Claude adapter prepends the headless block unless the prompt already contains the marker line `<!-- subfleet:headless -->` (the provider is known only after routing). The adapter writes the text actually sent to `<attempt dir>/prompt.sent.md`, the launch's `stdin_path` points at it, and it is recorded as an artifact with role `prompt-sent`.
- **C-6.8** Workspace preparation (the main or master re-check, worktree allocation, reading HEAD, and the baseline snapshot) runs before every admission, so a waiting job repeats it on each pass and one slow pass must not end the job. Every git call in it is capped by `caps.workspace_git_timeout_s` (60; `git worktree add` by `caps.worktree_add_timeout_s`, 180); finalization's salvage uses the same cap, and a caller with no policy in hand reads `SUBFLEET_GIT_TIMEOUT_S`. A git call that did not finish is never read as an answer: a timed-out `rev-parse` is not "no HEAD" and a timed-out `symbolic-ref` is not "not on main", and neither is a git killed by a signal (a negative return code: a memory-pressure kill, or a signal sent to the process group), which, as a timeout, is transient. At submit it is exit 1 with nothing stored; at launch nothing is launched, and the attempt ends with the cause as its detail: one that is transient ends as an attempt whose guardian could not be started does (`failed`, class `unknown`, never a spawn error), so the job is queued again while it has attempts left, waiting first under the backoff below (`wait_reason` `workspace` and a `job.workspace_deferred` event with `stage` `launch`), 5 s doubling per consecutive attempt that ended so, counted from the store, so an admission pass that succeeds between two of them does not reset it, and its next admission asks again (review of ceacf18b: as a spawn error it was classified `unknown`, which is never retried, and a killed git ended the job; review of that fix: queued at once, a check that kept getting no answer at launch while admission's got one used up the job's attempts in as many passes); a conversation turn has one attempt, so such a turn fails; at admission it is a failure classified here. Transient failures are a timeout, an `OSError` whose errno is EAGAIN, EINTR, ENOMEM, EMFILE, ENFILE, EBUSY, ETIMEDOUT, ENOBUFS, or ENOSPC, and git ending one of its `error:` or `fatal:` lines with a lock file another git process holds (`Unable to create '….lock': File exists.`) or a full disk (`: No space left on device`, or `write error. Out of diskspace` for a write that stored nothing), its `strerror` text for those transient errnos, or its errno-less index/ref write, allocation, or reftable lock and transaction I/O failures, whatever their cause (an I/O error or a permission problem writing a lock file reads as a full disk would), read under the C locale (C-13.1); a file name git prints in such a line could end it with one of these texts, and is then read as one: bounded, as every transient failure is: the job moves to `waiting` with `wait_reason` `workspace` and a `next_check_at` 5 s out, doubling per consecutive failure to a 300 s ceiling, holds no lease, blocks no other job, and returns to `queued` on the first pass that succeeds, which also resets the count. After `caps.workspace_retry_max` (8) consecutive transient failures, or at once for any other failure (git exiting non-zero, a permission error), the job is `failed` with rc 1. Nothing is discarded: each deferral is a `job.workspace_deferred` event and the terminal failure a `job.workspace_failed` event, both carrying `error_type` (the underlying exception, so `TimeoutExpired` rather than its wrapper) and `error` (git's verb, the cap, and its stderr, or the OS error; a path and an errno, never a credential); the notice text ends with the same two fields; `show` returns the latest record as `workspace`; and `daemon.log` gets one line per deferral and per failure, naming the job. The consecutive count is held in memory, so a daemon restart forgives it while the events keep the history. A `worktrees/<job id>/` directory that is not a worktree (a `git worktree add` killed at its cap) is removed and rebuilt before the job is admitted; no attempt has run in it. The baseline snapshot seeds its temporary index from a copy of the real index (mtime kept) and reads the baseline with `read-tree -m`, so its entries are the baseline's while the stat data of every unchanged path carries over and `add -A` hashes only changed files; the copy's assume-unchanged and skip-worktree bits, which `read-tree -m` keeps, are cleared across the whole index before `add -A`, since `add -A` skips such paths and the empty-index read has none, so both reads give the same tree; bytes and mtime come from one open file; with no usable real index (none yet, unmerged entries, a copy git cannot read or clear, or any other failure of a seeding step, a git killed by a signal included) it reads the baseline into an empty index in a fresh temporary directory (review of ceacf18b: git 2.55 is killed by SIGSEGV reading an index whose entries are garbage, a killed git is transient, and so every try of the snapshot failed the same way, the fallback also tripping on the crashed git's `index.lock`). A seeding step stopped at its cap did not finish rather than fail, and the snapshot fails with it, transient, as any git call past its cap does: under the load that stops the seeded read the unseeded one, which hashes every tracked file, is slower still (the incident below), so falling back would spend a second cap on each try before the same failure (review of the P3-1 fix). Trusting stat data is git's own model: a rewrite that keeps size, mtime and inode is read as unchanged (incident: 2026-09-23, axiom-corpus, 59,822 tracked files and 14 GB: the empty-index snapshot took 39 to 51 s at a load average near 6 and timed out at the 60 s cap on all eight tries, so no writable job could be admitted there; seeded, the same tree took 0.5 s). A refusal raised during preparation (C-6.5) is still exit 7 with its fix and never waits (incident: 2026-09-20, under a load average near 10 a writable job went from queued to `failed` in 19 s with "workspace preparation failed", no attempt, and nothing in `daemon.log`; the same snapshot took 0.6 s minutes later and an identical resubmission ran; twelve jobs ended that way between 2026-09-19 and 2026-09-20, read-only ones after as long as 70 minutes in the queue, because the caps were 15 s and 5 s, the exception was discarded, and every failure was terminal).
- **C-6.9** Queued jobs are considered in priority order: class, then tier in the policy's declared order, then oldest first (plan amendment 11), except `priority` jobs use FIFO across tiers (C-6.16). The classes, first to last (2026-09-27, Max: "uncap everything and instead use prioritization"): `attended`, a conversation turn from the Subfleet app (it has a pass of its own, which runs first, C-26.9); `priority`, a detached job whose caller or ancestor caller the operator selected (C-6.16); `session`, a detached job someone is waiting on now: a gate round, or a job whose caller's Claude Code session is live (a `~/.claude/sessions` row names its `caller_session`, in either case, and the row's pid is still the process that wrote it: `registry.validated` compares the row's `procStart` with one process-table read) or whose parent job is unfinished; a caller's pid alone (`caller_pid`) is not evidence, since a live pid proves a process, not the caller; and `background`, every other job (a caller that has exited, a launchd or cron submission, a batch whose dispatcher is gone). A batch a live session submits is `session`. Liveness is read once per pass, off the store lock but for one indexed query (`Daemon._liveness`), and `scheduler.priority_class` and `scheduler.ordered_jobs` are pure functions of it; with no liveness to read every detached job other than `priority` is `session`. The hold-back below applies only while the job's pool has a count cap (`scheduler.hold_scope`: for a detached job `max_active_attempts`, `max_in_flight_per_lane` or `max_in_flight_unmeasured`, and then behind any older competing job; for a turn either turn cap, likewise; for a job with a parent whose only count is `max_active_attempts_per_parent`, only behind an older competing job that shares an ancestor with it, since that count is shared only within a family). In an uncapped pool, the default, no job waits behind another: a later job never takes a slot an older one needs, because there are no slots to take, and a job no lane admits waits on its own clock (C-6.10) without holding anyone. Two scarce things keep their order with no cap at all. A lease: C-26.9's queue, which asks no job's kind (submit refuses a second writable job in one checkout and a second job on one output path, C-6.5, so detached contention is rare); a job is never queued for, nor held behind, nor kept from the last slot for (capped or not), a job waiting for a lease the first job holds itself: a retry keeps its job-held leases, and the waiter moves only once the retry has run (review of PR #72: two read-only resumes of one native session, the retrying one `background` and the waiter `session`, each waited for the other until `max_wall_s`). And a lane's probe lease: a detached attempt takes the lowest free `lane:<lane id>:slot:<n>` from n = 1, so `slot:0` is the admission probe's alone (C-11.4) and a probe waits for no attempt (review of the uncap plan: with no per-lane cap a busy lane nearly always had an attempt on `slot:0`, and an older writable job waiting to probe it lost the slot to each later job that needed no probe). And a probe's turn (2026-10-08): an admission probe (C-11.4) says nothing about the job that carries it, since its prompt is fixed and it runs on the lane's credential in a private directory, so the jobs of a detached pass that wait on a probe form a line in the pass's order (`Daemon._probe_line`), and a job carries the probe of a model on a lane only when no job ahead of it in the line waits on a probe of that model and could use that lane (`scheduler.probe_turn`): a lane of the model's provider, its pin's if it has one, and none that its own exclusions or a transient retry's name (`scheduler.probe_reach`; review of #153: a job that excluded a lane held that lane's turn from a job pinned to it). Otherwise it goes to a lane of the same model whose probe is its own (C-11.4's choice), or, with none, it is held `probe-pending` with `behind` naming the job whose turn it is, on a capacity clock (C-6.10), and is looked at on the first pass that finds nobody ahead of it waiting on that probe. A job is in the line while its last look left it waiting on a probe, its clock running or not, and not while it waits on an operator (`uncertain`); each detached pass builds the line again, so a job that started, ended or now waits for anything else holds nobody. Like a lease's queue this holds with no cap, and it holds only jobs that want a probe of the same model on a lane both could use: never a job that needs no probe. So among jobs that want the same model on the same lanes, starts follow the pass's order whatever the probes answer, and C-11.4's rotation bounds how long a job waits behind another for a lane it could use (incident: 2026-10-03, a `hard` review waited 64 minutes and never started while hard reviews submitted after it probed the lanes its own probes had run out of time on, and started; `docs/reports/2026-10-08-probe-vehicle-starvation.md`). A job that cannot be placed on this pass (it is `waiting` on `capacity` with a `next_check_at` still ahead, its probe is pending, no lane is admissible, or a lease it needs is held) holds back the later jobs of its tier **that compete with it**, and no others. A job no lane can admit (C-11.8) holds back none: one whose lane pin names a lane that refuses it for a standing reason, or one that every lane of every model of its chain refuses for one. It waits for no slot another job could take, so no later job is held behind it, kept out of a slot for it, or queued behind it for a lease. Admission asks this on every pass, not only at a look: a pin before the job is compared with any other, a job without one as it is put among the waiters or keeps a lease's place (`scheduler.unadmittable` on its clock, in a pool with a hold-back or for a job holding a lease's place; `scheduler.refused_for_good` of a decision with no lane). So a job put on its clock by a path that is not a look (a transient retry's 60 s) or whose lanes turned since its last look holds nobody while no lane can take it (incident: 2026-09-29 ~21:53Z, a job pinned to claude-9 when an account switch made claude-9 the desktop login held 38 younger standard Opus jobs `held behind` it while claude-7 was open). Two jobs compete when some model could serve both: a pinned job's models are its one pin; a task job's are its chain from its tier upward, exactly the chain routing walks (C-11.2); a job whose models cannot be told (a lane pin with no model) competes with every job. Lane pins narrow it further: two jobs pinned to different lanes never compete, whatever their models, because neither can take a slot the other waits for; a job with no lane pin, or a pin that cannot be resolved, could use any lane and so competes on models alone (incident: 2026-09-22, three Fable gate reviews pinned to three lanes; the oldest waited on its own lane's provider limit until 19:00Z and held the other two, whose lanes were open, for six hours). So an older Opus review keeps its place against later Opus work, including work that could promote into its chain, and a Fable-pinned job does not queue behind it. With `max_active_attempts` set, a job that passes an older waiting job of its tier leaves one active-attempt slot free (`max_active_attempts` minus one), so the older job starts the moment its capacity appears rather than waiting out the jobs that passed it; with nothing older waiting, the full cap applies; and at `max_active_attempts` the pass ends: nothing later is evaluated. In a capped pool the order above still decides who is older: a waiting `session` job holds back the later `background` jobs of its tier that compete with it. Tiers never hold each other, as before (incident: 2026-09-20, an Opus review that no lane would admit, every Claude lane `reserve:fable:unmeasured` and Astra closed until the next day, headed the `standard` tier; admission held the whole tier behind it, so three Fable-pinned jobs, tier-less and therefore `standard`, stayed queued beside eleven free Fable lanes and nothing at all was admitted for more than three hours).
- **C-6.10** A capacity wait is rechecked on a clock that backs off, and a recheck that reaches the verdict it reached last time adds no decision row. The first recheck is 1 s out (plan amendment 11); each consecutive recheck with the same verdict doubles it, to a 30 s ceiling; a reset the decision knows about (a closure's `until_at`, a reading's `resets_at`) that falls sooner is still checked on time. The verdict is the chain walked, the lane and model chosen, the policy hash, and each rejected lane with its reasons (`scheduler.verdict_signature`); readings, utilization, and `evaluated_at` are evidence and are left out, and so is `no-slot` on a lane that is rejected for another reason as well: a lane that is closed, reserved, or excluded is no nearer to taking the job when its slot empties, slots fill and empty all day, and a probe's reservation counts toward the fleet cap, so each probe cycle (C-18.1) marks every lane `no-slot` for the second it runs. A lane whose only reason is `no-slot` keeps it: that job is waiting for a slot, and that is its verdict. A changed verdict is recorded and restarts the clock at 1 s. The same doubling applies to three waits that are not on a closure or a reading, so the known-reset rule has nothing to say about them: a job waiting on a lease another job holds, which was a fixed 1 s; a job whose route could not be prepared with no clock set (its probe's slot is held, or the lane changed hands); and a job whose chosen lane and model changed after its probe. The last two had no clock at all and were prepared again, with a new probe directory, on every tick; a probe directory made for a reservation that then does not happen is removed, since no record names it and recovery would never collect it. Backing off is not meant to cost a start: each pass compares the leases no probe holds (the turn pass also those admission probes hold, C-26.9) with those it saw on the pass before, counting the ones it took itself, and when one has gone (an attempt ended, a job released its worktree or its output path) every backed-off capacity wait other than a probe's own is looked at on that pass. Capacity that appears any other way (a fresh reading, a closure an operator lifts, a lane enrolled) is seen at the next recheck, so the most this clause can add to a start is 30 s. Only a capacity wait is brought forward. A job whose wait became `workspace` (C-6.8) loses its record and keeps that clock, because a look brought forward there spends one of `workspace_retry_max` retries, and eight unrelated releases would end the job. The wait after an inconclusive probe keeps its clock too, because bringing it forward would re-probe the provider on every release. That clock is `PROBE_RETRY_S` (60 s) from the probe's reservation, not from its end, so a probe its deadline stopped leaves its job due at once, and the job, which keeps its turn (C-6.9), is looked at first on the next pass; one that failed in a second does not make a probe a second (until 2026-10-08 it was 60 s from the end, and since probes run inside the detached pass the job's next look came a whole pass later: 10 to 21 minutes on 2026-10-03). It gets the other half of the rule, and a probe that ends the same way adds no second decision row; its `job.probe_waiting` event names the lane, the model, the probe's class and the clock. The record (verdict, consecutive rechecks, first and last look, and the latest hold in full) is held in memory per job, replaced whole on every look so that another thread never reads half of one, and dropped when the job is placed or ends. A restart therefore forgets it, as it forgets C-6.8's count: recovery makes every capacity wait due once, since what each was waiting for may have changed while no daemon ran and nothing else could bring a persisted wait forward, at the cost of one decision row per waiting job; `why` reports the record (C-6.11). C-11.5 is unchanged: the decision is still stored per attempt, and a waiting job still has the latest verdict it reached (incident: 2026-09-20, three jobs no lane would admit were each re-evaluated once a second for hours; every look prepared the workspace with git, scored every lane, and wrote a 22 KB decision row whose only new content was fresher readings: 1,628 rows in ten minutes with no attempt reserved, 29,923 rows and 681 MB of a 709 MB store, the file growing about 170 MB an hour, and the daemon at a full core; the decision said `earliest reset: 2026-09-20T22:20:00Z`, more than an hour off, on every one of them; by the next morning the store was 2.3 GB, 2.2 GB of it 98,014 decision rows. Replayed over the 80,996 rows of the six worst jobs, the verdict changed 74 times).
- **C-6.11** A job that is not placed always has a stated reason, and a fleet that places nothing says so. Each admission pass records, per job it leaves unplaced, why: `behind-older-job` with the older job's id and the tier (C-6.9); `fleet-full` with `max_active_attempts`; `slot-kept` with `kept_for`, `live`, and `max_active_attempts`, when a lane would take the job and the fleet is one short of its cap because C-6.9 keeps the last slot for an older waiting job of the tier (it is not reported as a full fleet, which an operator can see it is not); `parent-cap` (these four, and `behind-older-job`, only in a pool with a count cap, C-6.9; in an uncapped pool `no-slot` means a probe, a latched credential or, with other lanes held otherwise, a pilot holds the lane); `lane-proving`, when every lane that could take a detached job has room only but for its pilot (C-6.14), each rejection's `slot_block` naming the pilot (`proving:<attempt id>`); `disk` with `free_gb`, `reserved_gb`, `floor_gb`, `resume_margin_gb` and `placement_reserve_gb` (C-6.17); `machine-busy` with `class` and the reading that held it (`load_per_cpu` and `load_threshold`, `memory_pressure` and `memory_threshold`, C-6.13); `lease-held` with the lease keys another job holds (output keys use C-6.5's canonical comparison identity even for a legacy raw guard; the job's `out_path` keeps its display spelling), among them a key the job needs free but does not take (a turn's `worktree:<target>`, the `worktree-turn:` rows of the turns in a detached writer's folder, C-24.5), which is never queued for (and, for a turn, `queued`: free keys kept for an older turn, C-26.9); `probe-pending` with the `lane` and `model` of the probe, and `behind` when that probe is an older job's turn (C-6.9); `route-moved` with `tries`, when the job's route could not be settled in C-6.3's `ROUTE_TRIES` reservations in a row (a commit before each check moved the lane its pin names, or removed a reading the decision rested on) and it keeps its place for the next pass; `attempt-live` (an earlier attempt of the job is still live or quarantined, found before the job is looked at or again inside its reserving transaction); `conversation-blocked` with `conversation_id`, `blocked_by` and `legacy_hold` (a turn whose conversation is blocked, C-24.5), or with `error_type` and `error` when its turn manifest is missing, cannot be read, or does not name its conversation, message and provider, or its conversation could not be checked for a reason other than a store error, which holds that job alone (a store error is the pass's, C-6.12); `message-settled` with `conversation_id` and `state` (its message was withdrawn after the job was made; the job is cancelled, C-24.7); `approval`, `uncertain`, `workspace` (C-4.1); `route` with `error_type`, `error`, and `deferrals` (C-6.12), which is reported as that whatever else holds the job while its clock runs; `pin-unadmittable` with `lane_id`, `reasons`, `closures`, `since` and `fail_at` (C-11.8: its pinned lane can never admit it), which is reported as that whatever else would hold the job, except a wait on `approval` or `uncertain`; otherwise the commonest first reason lanes rejected it for (`reserve:fable:unmeasured`, `closed:account`, `no-slot`, ...), with the `next_check_at` of any wait that has one, and `for_good` with the standing reasons when every lane refuses it for one (C-11.8), in which case `why` adds that it holds no other job back; a pass that asks it again on the job's clock replaces the last look's `for_good` with its own answer. A wait that is a person's or a retry's to end (`approval`, `uncertain`, `workspace`) is reported as that even when the fleet is also full or an older job is also ahead of it. `hold` is the last pass's answer and `recheck` describes the last look (`recheck.label` is what that look found): the reason is the latest look's, not the first's, whether or not the verdict repeated; a look that ends without changing a verdict or a clock still refreshes it; and a pass that does not look at the job repeats the last hold in full (`leases`, `max_active_attempts`), so `why` never degrades between looks. A pass that raises publishes nothing: the holds and the `admission` snapshot stay as the last whole pass left them, because half a hold set would read as nothing left pending, and C-5.10 logs and paces the failure. When every lane has a standing reason the label is the commonest of those and not `fleet-full`: a probe's reservation counts toward the fleet cap, so such a job would otherwise read `fleet-full` for the second each probe runs. The record is in memory and replaced whole each pass; C-26.9's turn pass and the detached pass each replace their own jobs' part, and `hold` is read from the two together. `subfleet why <job>` returns it as `job` (`state`, `tier`, `wait_reason`, `next_check_at`, `hold`, `recheck`, `decision_source`, `decided_at`) and as `queue`, the same in sentences, ahead of the decision; for a turn it also returns `lanes`, its lane ids by reason, the first reset among the closed ones and the capacity blocks (`conversations.waits.lane_summary`), from which its message says why it waits (C-24.4). A queued or waiting job with no decision row (admission held it back, or no pass has reached it) is evaluated for the answer and marked `decision_source: evaluated-now`; no decision is recorded and no job changes, because answering is not admission. The evaluation asks who the desktop login is, as every evaluation does (C-10.3), and a newly verified identity is recorded as the `desktop.identity` event it would have been on the next pass. A job that ended without a decision prints its state and `No decision recorded.`, unless admission refused it (C-6.12), which prints `Refused at admission: <message>` in its place The CLI never prints `null`, also against a daemon that predates `queue`. `daemon.status` carries `admission`: `pending` (unplaced jobs other than those held as `approval`, `uncertain`, `workspace`, `attempt-live`, `conversation-blocked` or `message-settled`, which are a person's or a retry's to end), `placed_at`, `idle_for_s` and `idle_since` (null unless jobs are pending and none has been placed), `reasons` (count per reason), and `open_lanes`; it is one snapshot, replaced whole at the end of a pass, so a status request never reads half of an update. `open_lanes` is the lanes owned by v2, enabled, not the desktop login while Claude Code uses it (C-10.3), identity not mismatched, under no account-wide closure, with a slot free (always, while no per-lane cap is set) and no probe or pilot (C-6.14) holding it. Open means capacity exists, not that it fits: a job can still be refused an open lane by a model-scoped closure, the reserve, or its own exclusions, and the reasons say which. `subfleet status` prints one `admission:` line while `idle_for_s` is set, and its `running jobs:` count and table cover live jobs only. When jobs have been pending with none placed for 60 s, `daemon.log` gets one line (count, seconds idle, open lanes by id, reasons by count, and the first job in admission order), and one line when placing resumes or nothing is left pending. The line is a warning when a lane is open and some job is held for a reason other than ordinary queueing (`fleet-full`, `slot-kept`, `parent-cap`, `no-slot`, `lane-proving`, `lease-held`, `probe-pending`, `behind-older-job`, `route-moved`, `machine-busy`); otherwise it is information, because with no lane open or a fleet at its cap waiting is the expected answer. The fleet is looked at again every 600 s while the stretch lasts: a warning is written each time, information at most every 3600 s, so a wait that was expected when it was first logged becomes a warning within ten minutes of becoming one (incident: 2026-09-20, fifteen jobs from six sessions sat queued for more than three hours beside open lanes; `subfleet why` printed `null` for every one of them, because a job admission skips gets no decision row and the CLI printed the missing value instead of the daemon's text; `subfleet status` said `running jobs: 519`, the size of the store, because `daemon.status` carries every job as `jobs`; and `daemon.log` had not gained a line since the daemon started).
- **C-6.12** A job whose route cannot be evaluated is that job's problem and never the pass's. Evaluating one job (`scheduler.evaluate`, and `scheduler.probe_required`, which checks an unmeasured-reserve authorization) is isolated from the rest of the pass, both where the route is prepared and inside the reserving transaction, which rolls back before the job is settled; a `ValueError` (a `PolicyError` is one), `KeyError`, `TypeError`, `AttributeError`, or `IndexError` from it settles that job and the pass goes on to the next, in its tier and every other. What it settles to depends on whose the error is. A `scheduler.RouteError` is the job's own: its pin names several lanes, its pin and its model are of different providers, or its authorization is not whole. (A pin that names one lane which can never admit the job is not a route error: C-11.8 holds it and, after a grace, fails it with rc 3.) No wait can fix that and only the caller can say what was meant, so the job is `failed` with rc 2, what submit would have answered, its `wait_reason` and `next_check_at` cleared (as for any job `_fail_queued` ends); its notice and a `job.route_refused` event carry the message (`error_type`, `error`), `daemon.log` gets one line naming the job and the message, `why` prints `Refused at admission: <message>` ahead of any decision recorded before it (`refused` in its result), and a worktree admission cut for it under `worktrees/<job id>/` that no attempt used is removed, since `jobs.worktree` is set only at reservation and retention would never collect it. Two of those errors can also come from a policy edit rather than the job: a provider conflict (a tier's chain moved to another provider under a job already pinned to a lane) and an authorization's model check. Those are refused only under the policy the job was accepted with (`jobs.policy_hash` equal to the running policy's); under any other they are treated as below. Anything else is not the job's: bad capacity data, a policy that no longer knows the job's task, tier, or model (submit checked each against the policy it ran under, so a policy edit is the operator's to fix), or a defect. That is never terminal, because a restart can bring the fix and failing it could fail every queued job at once: the job waits with `wait_reason` `route`, 5 s out doubling per consecutive failure to a 300 s ceiling, each deferral a `job.route_deferred` event carrying `error_type`, `error`, `deferrals`, and `next_check_at`, and a `daemon.log` line naming the job and the error's type only (C-5.10: the message is in the event and in `why`). A route wait is not a capacity wait: it holds back no other job (C-6.9) and no released lease brings it forward (C-6.10). While its clock runs its hold is `route` with the error (C-6.11), whatever else would hold the job; once the clock is due the job goes through the pass like any other, and a pass that evaluates its route resets the count. `why` names the error, from the hold and, when `why`'s own evaluation raises, in place of the decision (`route_error` in its result); `daemon.status` counts the hold under `admission.reasons`. The count is in memory; recovery makes every route wait due, as it does capacity waits. Route holds are not ordinary queueing, so beside an open lane the idle line (C-6.11) is a warning. A store error is not isolated: that is the pass's, and C-5.10 retries it. An accepted job is always evaluable as far as submit can tell: an empty task or tier is stored as none. A transient retry (C-4.5, C-9.5) is the daemon's choice of pair, not the job's, and is pinned to the last attempt's lane and model id only while that pair could run: the running policy resolves the id (a current id or a `retired` alias) to a model of the lane's provider, and the lane (or its re-enrolled successor) is enabled, v2-owned, and not identity-blocked. While it is pinned, its C-6.9 demand is that one model on that one lane; once a look has let the pin go, the job's demand is its own on that pass and while its clock runs, so it neither passes an older job it competes with nor is passed by a younger one (held behind one after a look, it waits on a C-6.10 capacity clock like any other held look, so the passes before that clock prepare no workspace and score no lane), and the next due look evaluates the pair again (a lane that recovered takes its one retry; a restart, which forgets the look, changes nothing). When the job is due, the pair is evaluated as admission would evaluate it. If the lane refuses it only for want of a slot (in flight, the fleet or a parent at its cap, a probe holding it), the retry waits for that lane. If the lane refuses it for anything a slot will not end (a closure, the desktop login, the job's own exclusions, a latched credential, the floor, the reserve), or the pair cannot be evaluated, the job routes as submitted, which is C-4.5's "then next candidate". Before this, a pinned retry waited on its lane until `max_wall_s` cancelled the job (incident: 2026-09-22, five jobs pinned by email, `-a max@axiom.org` and `-a max@thesisinstitute.org`, with a task and no model, named a Claude lane and a Codex lane at once in the capacity view (C-11.2); `resolve_lane` raised inside `_admit_pass`, which caught nothing per job, so every pass aborted at the first of them: `worker admission failed: ValueError (128 in a row)`, no attempt reserved between 14:45:05Z and 18:02:45Z (10:45 to 14:03 EDT), and every later job in every tier waited; `why` caught the same error and printed `No decision recorded.` for those five, so nothing pointed at the stalled pass. Admission resumed within a minute of the five pins being rewritten to `claude-11` and `claude-9` by hand. The same resolution had stalled admission once already that day: a gate review pinned `-a max@axiom.org -m fable` held every pass from its submission at 13:24:34Z until it was cancelled at 13:48:53Z, no attempt was reserved from 13:24:08Z to 13:50:32Z, and `daemon.log` shows the count reaching sixteen twice only because the lane-pins cutover restarted the daemon at 13:35:39Z).
- **C-6.13** (the machine guard, 2026-09-27; off by default, Max's to turn on) With no count cap (C-6.4), admission can hold the detached jobs of a class at the door while this machine is saturated, and never holds an `attended` conversation turn or a `priority` detached job (C-6.16). The validator rejects both classes in the guard. Policy `admission.machine_guard` names, per class (`background`, `session`), a `load_per_cpu`, a `memory_pressure` (`warn` or `critical`), or both; it is null by default, which holds nothing (2026-09-28, Max: "remove *all* caps", "nothing should be queued"), and a class it does not name is never held. The proposed setting (`policy.MACHINE_GUARD_PROPOSAL`) holds `background` jobs at 6.0 per logical CPU or `warn`, and `session` jobs at 10.0 or `critical`. A job is held while the larger of the 1- and 5-minute load averages divided by the logical CPU count is at or above its class's `load_per_cpu`, or while `kern.memorystatus_vm_pressure_level` (1 normal, 2 warn, 4 critical) is at or above its level: the larger average holds as soon as load rises and lets go only as it stays down, so a dip does not release a burst. The reading (`subfleet/machine.py`: `os.getloadavg`, `os.cpu_count`, `sysctlbyname`) is taken once per pass, costs no subprocess, and holds nothing where it cannot be read. The check (`scheduler.machine_hold`) comes before workspace preparation, so a held job costs no git and records no decision; its hold is `machine-busy` (C-6.11), ordinary queueing that is never logged as a warning, and it holds no other job back. It never counts attempts. Why: the load was what jobs run, not the providers: measured at about 20:15 EDT on 2026-09-27, at a load average near 120 on 18 logical CPUs, Claude's own processes used about 31% CPU in all while one job's `bfs` search used 453% and test suites and `uv` builds about 430%, and at load 110 to 170 the day before the daemon itself starved (C-6.3's incident). [owner unit, fake; module `subfleet/scheduler.py`, `subfleet/machine.py`, `subfleet/daemon.py`]
- **C-6.14** (a lane is proven before a burst, 2026-10-03) A lane is *proven* while a model has answered on it within `admission.prove_idle_s` (900 s by default; a positive number of seconds, or null, which holds no lane). A model answers when an event of an attempt's stream shows it (`Adapter.model_answered`: for Claude an `assistant` frame a model served, a model id that is not Claude Code's `<synthetic>` sentinel, no API-error marker and no `error` kind at all, a `result` whose `usage` counts a token and that is not marked `is_error`, or a `system/thinking_tokens` event with a positive count, which Claude Code 2.1.284 writes while the model streams its thinking; when in doubt it says no, since a lane read as proven takes a burst; for Codex an item only the model produces (`agent_message`, `reasoning`, `command_execution`, `file_change`, `mcp_tool_call`, `web_search`, `todo_list`) or a `turn.completed` whose usage counts a token; never `system/init`, a hook event, a placeholder frame, `thread.started`, `turn.started` or an `error` item), when an attempt's classification records `model_answered` or its adapter's verdict is `ok` (no adapter reaches `ok` without an answer, and a stream shape the predicate does not know must not leave a lane one attempt wide for good), and when an admission probe (C-11.4) or a timer's keepalive or heal turn ends `ok`. On a lane that is not proven, a detached attempt in flight that has not answered is its *pilot* (the least attempt id when there are several), and while one is in flight the lane is `no-slot` to every other detached job, with `slot_block` `proving:<attempt id>` laid over the view's `unavailable_lanes` as a probe's lease is (C-11.4); a lane a probe or a latched credential already holds keeps that reason. So a burst onto a lane idle that long places one attempt, and the rest go to lanes that can take them (C-11.3's order) or wait. The pilot's first answer proves the lane and frees it the way a released lease frees a slot (C-6.10: every backed-off capacity wait is looked at on the next pass; only an answer on a lane that was not proven does this, since a proven lane's every start answers too); a pilot that ends without one leaves the lane unproven and the next job its pilot; a pilot that has not answered `admission.prove_wait_s` after its reservation (300 s by default; null waits however long) is a pilot no longer, so its lane takes one more attempt, the next pilot, and a pilot that hangs costs its lane one attempt per wait, not every attempt until `max_wall_s` (`daemon.log` says so once for the attempt; five minutes because the incident's refusals took 78 to 181 s to arrive under its load); a pilot's `auth-dead` disables the lane (C-23.44) with the rest of the burst still queued, not failed, and its job moves on when C-4.5 says so. A job that moved on from an `auth-dead` lane is never a pilot: on a lane not proven it waits for Subfleet's probe of the lane (C-4.5, C-11.4), so a pilot is never a job that moved on, and a pilot's `auth-dead` disables the lane. A probe that answers `auth-dead` has disabled its lane, so the job's next evaluation goes on at once, as after a `limited` one, rather than on the clock an inconclusive probe sets (C-6.10). A probe that ends `ok` proves its lane even when its identity does not bind (C-10.6): the lane answered, so it is not dead, though the probing job waits for the identity as before. A pilot whose wait lapses wakes admission as an answer does. A conversation turn is never held for a pilot and is never one (C-26.9: a person is waiting). The hold is room, not a standing refusal: in an uncapped pool it holds no other job back (C-6.9), it is never `for_good` (C-11.8), a transient retry pinned to the lane waits for it (C-6.12's "only for want of a slot"), and a job it keeps off every lane it could use reads `lane-proving` (C-6.11), ordinary queueing. The attempt worker reads each live detached attempt's stream file for that answer (`Daemon._read_answer`): at most once a second, 256 KiB at a time, only as a regular file and never waiting in `open()`, a line still unfinished past 4 MiB dropped and skipped to its newline, never after the first answer, and nothing it finds or fails to read is the attempt's trouble. What the daemon hears is in memory (`_lane_answers`, `_attempt_answers`, on the clock its views are built on) and recorded as a `lane.answered` event (lane, attempt, `source`: `stream`, `finalization`, `probe`, `keepalive` or `heal`); a starting daemon seeds its memory from the newest 5,000 of them, so a restart neither proves again a lane that answered a minute ago nor takes an attempt that answered before it for a pilot. The early view (`_capacity_view`) and the reservation's check (`_route_rows`, C-6.3) lay the marks with one pure function (`capacity.pilot_marks`) over the rows and clock each reads, so a pilot placed, or an answer heard, between the two changes that lane, which is judged again, and the check's decision is the one an evaluation then would make. Why a lane is proven by an answer and why 900 s: usage readings, probes of the usage endpoint and the profile endpoint do not show this (on 2026-09-30 claude-5 read above its floor while its organisation had turned Claude Code off), so the only proof is a model answering, and the window bounds how long the last one is trusted. A lane in use proves itself with each attempt it starts and never waits; one idle for fifteen of C-18.1's 60 s probe intervals costs one serialized start, its pilot's time to a first answer (seconds; one to three minutes at the incident's load), and nothing while another lane can take the work. The limit of the rule: a lane whose access goes within the window of its last answer is still taken as proven, and can take a burst; each of those jobs then moves on once (C-4.5). With PR #72's load band (`admission.lane_spread`, C-11.3): the band orders the lanes that can take a job and spreads a pool across them, but the only lane above its floor still took every job; the hold refuses a lane before the ranking, the band orders after it, and a proven lane rejoins at its band. [owner unit, fake; module `subfleet/capacity.py`, `subfleet/scheduler.py`, `subfleet/daemon.py`, `subfleet/adapters/claude_stream.py`, `subfleet/adapters/codex.py`]
- **C-6.16** (priority callers, 2026-10-06, Max: "give axiom promise maximum priority") Policy `admission.priority_callers` is null by default, or a list of nonempty Claude Code caller session ids, canonicalized by stripping whitespace and lowercasing, as native-session checks do. A detached job is `priority` if its `caller_session`, or any ancestor job's caller reached by `parent_job_id`, is in that list, in either case; inheritance includes finished ancestors and is independent of caller liveness. Missing parents end the walk; cycles terminate after visiting each parent at most once. Admission reads only the ancestors it needs, once per detached pass, with indexed queries; when the list is null or empty it reads no ancestors for priority. Classes order `attended`, `priority`, `session`, `background`; within `priority`, FIFO ignores tier and tied timestamps preserve submission order. Other classes keep tier then FIFO, and null or omitted priority callers preserve the previous ordering exactly. A waiting priority job holds back later competing detached jobs of every tier, priority or not, and a job that passes it keeps it the last slot of a capped pool, as C-6.9 does for a waiter of the job's own tier; a waiter that is not priority still holds back only its own tier. Turns remain `attended` even with a selected caller, and their separate pass and waiter queue are unchanged (C-26.9). The machine guard never holds priority work for any load or memory reading (C-6.13), while every other hold, capacity and lease rule still applies. `subfleet why <job>` includes `class priority (admission.priority_callers)` for direct and inherited priority, and its job object carries `class`. `daemon.status` carries configured `priority_callers` as session ids and available registry names; `subfleet status` lists those names with their ids and uses the id alone when no name or registry is available. [owner unit, fake; module `subfleet/policy.py`, `subfleet/scheduler.py`, `subfleet/daemon.py`, `subfleet/render.py`, `subfleet/cli.py`]

- **C-6.17** (disk admission, d636, 2026-09-29; paced recovery, 2026-10-09) `admission.disk` is off by default (`enabled: false`); the hub opts in through its live policy. Defaults are `floor_gb: 40`, `resume_margin_gb: 5`, `placement_reserve_gb: 1.5`, `reserve_ttl_s: 600`, and `path: null` (the state root's volume). The loader accepts a boolean enabled flag, finite nonnegative floor and margin, finite positive reserve and TTL, and an optional nonempty path string; it rejects unknown keys naming the exact setting. Each detached admission pass reads available bytes once through an injectable `os.statvfs(path).f_bavail * f_frsize` reader; GB means 1e9 bytes. Before workspace preparation, a detached placement, including a retry and a C-6.16 priority caller, needs `measured_free - outstanding_reservations - placement_reserve >= floor`. A refusal latches disk admission until effective free reaches `floor + resume_margin`, after which placements consume reservations one by one. Each committed detached attempt reserves its budget from `reserved_at`, released at execution end (`finished_at`, including finalization) or TTL, whichever comes first. Reservations are in memory and reconciled from live attempt rows before each pass; size and TTL in attempt evidence preserve the same budgets on restart, and recent older attempts use policy defaults. Latch transitions are events so a restart does not bypass hysteresis. Attended turns never read, wait on, or reserve disk capacity; probes remain exempt. An unreadable enabled path holds detached work with its error. As C-6.13 does, the gate records a `disk` hold in the admission pass's in-memory wait reasons, without a lane lease or a capacity backoff; `why` reports free, reserved, floor, margin and placement budget, and status carries one disk line with free, reserved, floor and holding/open/disabled. Disabled or absent disk policy reads no disk, records no reservation and preserves existing admission decisions. This paces launches and does not replace retention. [owner unit, fake; module `subfleet/disk.py`, `subfleet/policy.py`, `subfleet/daemon.py`, `subfleet/render.py`, `subfleet/cli.py`; design `docs/reports/2026-10-09-disk-admission.md`]

## 7. Cancellation

- **C-7.1** `kill` inserts a cancel request in the job row (`cancel_requested_at`) in one transaction and returns; the daemon performs C-5.6 asynchronously. `kill --wait` blocks until terminal.
- **C-7.2** If the cancel commits before acceptance, the job becomes `cancelled` even if an attempt later reaches `finalizing`; that attempt's artifacts are kept and it is marked `interrupted`. If acceptance commits first, `kill` returns 0 and prints "already finished".
- **C-7.3** A parent's cancel cancels every child job not submitted with `--independent`, in the same transaction that records the parent's request.
- **C-7.4** A job that is `queued` when cancelled becomes `cancelled` immediately and releases its leases. An admission probe is never reserved, and its gate never opened, for a job whose cancellation has completed.

## 8. Artifacts and export

- **C-8.1** Every file the daemon publishes (deliverable, `-o` export, `manifest.json`, receipts) is written as: temp file in the destination directory, `os.fsync(fd)`, `os.rename`, `os.fsync` on the directory descriptor.
- **C-8.2** The deliverable is captured once at `finalizing` from the adapter (C-12.6) and recorded in `artifacts` with role `deliverable`, path, bytes, and SHA-256. The raw stream, stdout, stderr, and lane log are recorded with their own roles. Nothing appended to a transcript after the attempt's exit changes the deliverable.
- **C-8.3** The `-o` export is a copy of the accepted deliverable published by C-8.1 after acceptance. A failed export sets `export_error` on the job and emits an event; the job stays `succeeded`.
- **C-8.4** Retention: newest 500 jobs or 2 GiB (policy `retention.jobs`, `retention.bytes`), whichever binds, counted over jobs that are not conversation turns (turn jobs have their own budget, C-26.12), computed outside transactions in a maintenance pass; jobs that are active, `quarantined`, have unread notices addressed to a session, hold salvage refs referenced nowhere else, or are a gate's evidence are never pruned. A notice with no session can never be read, so it pins nothing (review IR-17). Probe and keepalive results live in `readings` and `events`, not in `jobs`. I5: a job with its own allocated worktree (writable, not in place) is protected as `turn-folder` by the archive driver's `_pin_reasons`, at selection and again before committing, while a live `worktree-turn:` or `worktree-read:` row names its recorded worktree or a folder inside it (a conversation in a repository nested there keys its row on that repository); a row keeps no in-place job, whose folder retention never removes. Its retirement transaction checks `folders.turn_holds` on the canonical worktree and every folder inside it before taking `retire:<job>` and `worktree:<folder>` together, and the commit transaction checks them again on the journal's canonical spelling, so a recorded worktree spelled in another case, or none recorded, does not hide a row; turn reservation checks that fence in the same store transaction and refuses writable turns and readers on that folder until retirement releases it. A `worktree:` lease held by anything but the job's own retirement (`retention:<job>`) keeps the job as `worktree-lease`: one on its recorded worktree keeps any job, and one on a folder inside it keeps a job with its own allocated worktree, as a turn row does (a detached in-place writer in a repository nested there holds `worktree:<that repository>`, and its quarantined attempt leaves its job `lost` with the lease kept); the retirement transaction reads those leases on the canonical worktree and every folder inside it (`folders.exclusive_inside`, one key range) before taking the fence, and the commit transaction reads them again on the journal's canonical spelling. Known limit: reservation fences only a turn's or a detached writer's own folder, so a turn or writer in a repository nested inside the tree can be reserved during a retirement, while the tree is in quarantine. The commit then finds its job (`worktree-in-use`, while the job has not ended), its row or its lease and rolls the retirement back, unless the folder was already in quarantine when submission spelled it and the tree's own name (its job id) was given in another case than the volume stores (a native session's recorded cwd, a conversation created before d69bd59f spelled its workspace at creation, or an in-place writer whose tree moved into quarantine after submission checked that its workdir exists and before submission spelled it): `folders.spelling` keeps the names it cannot look up as given, so the row or lease keeps the tree's name in that case, and once its job has ended neither the row checks nor the lease checks match it (review of 8a112986; for a writer, executed in the review of c3cf6a55; a different case on a name above or inside the tree is still matched). The checks also miss a writer reserved inside the tree's quarantine location itself (`retention/<job>/worktree/…` under the state root): its lease names that path, outside the tree's range, so once its attempt is quarantined, retention reclaims that folder with the tree (executed in the review of c3cf6a55). Folder strings are compared as recorded, with canonical spelling established outside transactions (`folders.canonical`), never filesystem inspection under the store lock. Pruning archives first (d635, `docs/desktop/retention-archive.md`): a job's files (worktree and job directory, byte for byte as clones or copies, except a tracked file whose raw bytes are a blob a network remote's refs reach in a repository that is not scratch, read back whole, and except regenerable output: inside the entries a tool writes at the top of a worktree directory whose structure says it is a virtualenv, `node_modules`, `__pycache__` (all of it) or a tool cache carrying `CACHEDIR.TAG`, which git tracks nothing in and ignores entirely (names compared NFC-normalized and casefolded) and no nested repository holds, none of them holding a `.git`, the files a rule proves regenerable (an installed distribution's file whose sha256 its RECORD names, from a distribution uv installed from an index or a network URL; bytecode with a magic number beside its source; a tool cache's own files; nothing of `node_modules`), which are deleted with the tree and not restored, while everything else in that directory is archived), its worktree's admin directory and every object that directory names, its salvage commits and baseline (parents of one anchor commit, the only head of a bundle of every commit no network remote holds; a salvage ref whose commit the verified anchor reaches is referenced elsewhere), and its rows (`rows.json`) are written to `$SUBFLEET_HOME/archive/<job>/` and read back before the rows are deleted; after that, an entry is deleted only if its signature (ctime included) is unchanged right before the unlink, or, for a file that had other links when archived and whose ctime alone moved, only if its bytes still hash to the archived sha256, and anything new or changed goes to `$SUBFLEET_HOME/retention-conflicts/<job>/`. A salvage snapshot's skipped paths are protected unless the archive itself holds their bytes and reads them back; a caller's `salvage_referenced_elsewhere` vouch releases only the ref pin, never the file-preservation check. A regular file with neither stored bytes nor a verified omission or regenerable proof keeps the job as `protected`, with `unarchived path` and its path in the result's `deferred` reasons, never `pruned`; this check also runs before committing a resumed archive, whose journal bypasses the builder. A job whose tree another tool holds aside, or whose tree left or came back while it was being retired, is kept. A pass retires the oldest eligible jobs in a bounded batch, without sizing every job first, parks a job whose archive needs more than its slice with its progress (a slice always makes new progress, and parked jobs take turns), defers a busy or failing one (keeping what it archived; a repeated error waits longer), counts a job it cannot measure as decided, and never runs a repository-wide `git worktree prune`; the daemon retains measured sizes and archive progress between passes. While work remains, a completed batch that advances (including a newly cached size, a parked archive slice, publication, partial verified deletion, or a prune) is followed 5 s later; an advancing batch that reaches its deadline warns and raises `TimeoutError`, on C-5.10’s worker clock. A deadline with neither pruning nor advancement marks `TimeoutError`, warns once with the store’s job count, rearms the hour and does not raise. A cancelled pass marks `CancelledError` and rearms the hour even after progress. A completed pass with nothing more, or a batch with no advancement, marks and rearms the hour from its end. In every rearming outcome `_last_maintenance` is set last, after status bookkeeping, so a bookkeeping failure leaves retention due. A deadline limits new starts, never commits of verified archives; even slow pre-selection work permits one job to start and receive its archive slice. A failed batch holder scan marks `ScanFailed` and rearms the hour, even after quarantine, rollback or archive work, to avoid cycling the store against an unavailable scanner. Delivered service notices older than 14 days are pruned first, once per retention call (one bounded batch); a failure there is logged and never stops job retention. Recovery finishes journaled retirements before new selection and prioritizes a job left with a journal-less `retention:<job>` lease. Its events, each pool in its result and the daemon's log lines report the bytes freed (tracked files a remote holds and regenerable output, deleted without a copy) apart from the bytes moved into the archive and the bytes the archive adds (its bundle, manifest, summary and rows, and byte copies), with the net. A job whose baseline no network remote holds, and whose bundle would carry more than `retention.remote_less_history_bytes` (default 64 MiB) of history, is kept; a job registered in a repository inside another job's tree pins that job while it has rows. Retention's children run under the `utility` QoS clamp (C-5.1). Archives are kept until a person removes them.

## 9. Evidence labels and classification

- **C-9.1** Reading labels: `provider` (a server reported it; source and age recorded), `stale-provider` (the same beyond `reading_ttl_s`), `admission-observed` (this model recently succeeded or was rejected on this lane; remaining quota unknown), `local-backoff` (a routing decision with an explicit expiry and reason), `unknown`. No other label exists. A percentage is rendered only from a `provider` or `stale-provider` reading, and a stale one is marked stale.
- **C-9.2** Classification precedence is authentication, then admission, then quota, and the classifier records which evidence answered each. Outcome classes: `ok`, `limited`, `auth-dead`, `cli-too-old`, `content-filter`, `transient`, `unknown`. The raw rc and signal are always kept beside the class. An attempt the daemon signalled (`killed_by` set: an operator's kill, the wall limit, recovery of a dead guardian's group) is never `ok`, whatever its rc and deliverable, except a conversation turn whose driver recorded `complete` (C-24.4: that is the provider's own end-of-turn event, not an exit status, so a stop that came after it does not undo it): finalization records it `unknown` with the detail `stopped by <killed_by>: exit <rc> after the daemon's signal is not a finished deliverable`. The daemon decides this, not the adapter, so it holds for every provider; like C-12.6's empty-deliverable rule it is recomputed from the attempt row on every finalization while `finalization.json` keeps the adapter's verdict, and whenever either rule overrides that verdict the attempt's evidence keeps it as `provider_verdict` (incident: 2026-09-24, three running Codex jobs were cancelled; Codex exited 0 after the SIGTERM and left its last interim progress message, 32 to 61 words such as "I am checking the newer validation code before finalizing", in `last.md`, which the adapter classified `ok`, "Codex completed with a deliverable").
- **C-9.3** `auth-dead` requires a 401 from a usage endpoint, an explicit organisation-block message, a refresh-token-revoked event, or the provider's refusal of the lane's own key ("Incorrect API key provided", `invalid_api_key`; codex-1, -3 and -6 failed every job that way on 2026-09-25 while classed transient). For Codex any other 401 is `transient`. For Claude, a credential-failure signature (a 401, `authentication_error`, a revoked token) in a run that never reached `system/init` is `auth-dead`, because the credential never authenticated; after `system/init` it is not auth evidence. A 403 from the Claude usage endpoint on a setup token is expected scope and is not evidence. A limit-looking phrase with a successful `system/init` is `limited`, not `auth-dead`. A Codex conversation turn's `unauthorized` error is not evidence on its own: the turn is `unknown` and the lane's usage endpoint decides (design §7). Only what the CLI and the provider wrote is evidence of `auth-dead` (2026-10-03): stderr, the frames that are the CLI's own (`claude_stream.cli_placeholder`: Claude Code's placeholders, marked `is_api_error_message`, with a missing or `<synthetic>` model, or carrying a credential or organisation error kind), the result's `errors`, and its text when it is marked `is_error` (`StreamSummary.cli_texts`); Codex reads only its failure events and stderr, as before. A model's own words quoting these phrases (a review of this classifier) are not: an `auth-dead` attempt disables its lane (C-23.44) and its job may move on (C-4.5), so a job whose answer quoted them would disable each lane it reached. Every Claude and Codex classification records `model_answered` in its evidence, whatever its class: whether any event of the attempt's stream showed a model answering (C-6.14), or null when no event of it could be read. `system/init` is no such event: every attempt refused on 2026-09-30 had one, then the placeholder (`error` `oauth_org_not_allowed`, every usage counter zero), and a `success` result marked `is_error` with `api_error_status` 403 and `duration_api_ms` 0 (fixture `tests/fixtures/claude/org-block-synthetic`).
- **C-9.4** `limited` carries scope (`account` or a model id), the provider's reset clock when reported, else a guessed clock of now + 3600 s marked `clock_source: guessed`, and the evidence (event, stderr line, or rc). It writes a closure (C-9.6).
- **C-9.5** `transient` covers 5xx, stream disconnects, DNS and TLS failures, and "model at capacity" messages. It never writes a closure; it allows one same-lane retry after 60 s.
- **C-9.6** A closure is (lane, scope, until, reason, clock source, source event). A new closure on the same lane and scope extends `until` but never shortens it. Closures expire by clock; nothing else releases a provider-limit closure except a later `provider` reading that shows the window reset.
- **C-9.7** Codex windows are classified by duration (`window_minutes` 300 is `five_hour`, 10080 is `seven_day`, anything else keeps its minute count as the key), never by slot position.
- **C-9.8** Claude `rate_limit_event`: `unifiedWindows.<window>.utilization` is a fraction in [0, 1] and `resetsAt` is epoch seconds; `status: allowed` yields `provider` readings for the shared five-hour and weekly windows with scope `account`, and a scoped weekly Fable reading when `seven_day_overage_included` is present; over-cap utilization is stored as exhausted (1) with the original retained in evidence; `status: rejected` yields no utilization reading; it yields one `admission-observed` reading for the requested model whose `window` is the literal `admission` (the only value of `window` that is not a duration key), with `resetsAt` as the closure clock when present, and `errorCode` recorded. Named rejection types `seven_day_overage_included`, `seven_day_opus`, and `seven_day_sonnet` close their respective model scopes; shared and unknown limit types close the account. `overageStatus` is parsed independently and never treated as admission evidence.

## 10. Lanes and credentials

- **C-9.9** The Claude usage sensor that costs no model turn is `https://api.anthropic.com/api/oauth/usage`, read with the lane's own credential (source `oauth-usage`). It yields `provider` readings for the account windows `five_hour` and `seven_day` (scope `account`) and one `provider` reading per `limits[]` row of kind `weekly_scoped`, scope = the policy model id for the row's `scope.model.display_name`, window `seven_day`; utilization is normalised to a fraction. HTTP 429 yields no reading and the server's `Retry-After`, which the timer honours before asking that lane again; 401 is auth-dead; 403 is no-scope; anything else is unavailable and yields no reading. Reads are paced `reserve.usage_spacing_s` apart (3) because the endpoint penalises bursts (2026-09-06: fourteen back-to-back reads earned `Retry-After: 3035`). C-10.6 holds: the readings count only when the profile endpoint, asked with the same credential, binds the lane. The timer's periodic Claude probe is this read on idle and busy lanes, with pacing and Retry-After honoured in both cases; a model turn is spent only by the pre-launch probe (C-11.4) and keepalive. A Claude home lane's login lives in the config directory's `.credentials.json` or, on macOS, in the keychain item `Claude Code-credentials-<sha256(CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR)[:8]>` (observed 2026-09-06); its access token expires within hours and carries a refresh token the CLI renews when it runs. An access token past `expiresAt`, or a 401 on a home lane with a refresh token, is `expired-token`, never auth-dead: on an idle lane the timer spends one minimal turn under that home (C-23.47's heal, at most once per 20 minutes per credential epoch) and reads usage again. A busy lane runs no heal turn and leaves token renewal to its attempts (C-18.3).
- **C-9.10** Claude limit-reset cards and promotional credits are read, never spent. Every `claude_cards.interval_min` (360; the first read a minute after start) and on `subfleet cards --refresh`, the daemon reads every Claude Code login under `claude_cards.logins_dir` (`<state root>/logins`) on a worker of its own. The sensor's own requests are GETs: `https://api.anthropic.com/api/oauth/profile`; `https://api.anthropic.com/api/oauth/usage?cedar_ember=1&skip_spend=1`, paced as C-9.9 paces usage reads; and `https://api.anthropic.com/v1/code/promo/cloud_credit`. Each carries Claude Code's own User-Agent, `claude-cli/<installed version> (external, cli)`: asked with `claude-code/2.1.286` instead, the server answered the cards block `eligible: false, ineligible_reason: "surface"` with no grants (one comparison, 2026-10-05). A lane's setup token cannot read these (403, C-9.3), so the logins are read, not the lanes' credentials. A login is bound to every lane whose recorded identity its profile returned (C-10.6) and to every lane that recorded no identity whose label equals the login's folder name, shown as "by name"; before the login's own identity is known its folder name is matched against every lane. The name binding never records an identity; it decides whether a heal may be spent and whether a hold covers the login, which lanes are shown beside it, and which lane ids a declared plan end may be filed under. An expired login is healed by one minimal Claude Code turn under its folder (C-23.47: one Haiku turn, no MCP server, in a process group of its own, with no other credential in its environment; none starts once the daemon is stopping; the group is sent SIGTERM and two seconds later SIGKILL after 120 s or when the daemon stops, and is killed however the turn ends, after it exits or on an error while its output is read; its output is read as UTF-8, with any byte that is not UTF-8 replaced), at most once per `claude_cards.heal_interval_min` (60), and only when `claude_cards.heal` is true and the login backs at least one lane, enabled or not, none of them under an operator hold (read for every login, from the closures and from the `lane.held` and `lane.released` events): an account under an operator hold, or one that backs no lane, is never spent on. A stop also ends a read between its requests. A login the CLI says it cannot renew (its output names a refresh that could not be done) is `login-dead`, and no turn is spent on it until its stored login changes; a heal that could not run (timed out, no CLI, a stop, or a failure that names no login problem) is `unavailable` and is tried again. A `lapsed` or `login-dead` login is looked at again at most daily until its stored login changes. A 429 is waited out for the server's Retry-After or an hour, whichever is longer. The snapshot, `<state root>/claude-cards.json`, is published as C-8.1 publishes (mode 0600), after each login a heal was spent on as well as at the end of the pass, and keeps per login: a status (`ok`, `no-login`, `login-expired`, `login-dead`, `lapsed`, `no-scope`, `rate-limited`, `unavailable`, `held`); the plan (`organization_type`, `rate_limit_tier`, `subscription_status`, `billing_type`; `claude_free` or `canceled` is `lapsed`, either one on its own: per help article 17007452 a reset is no longer available once the account cancels, and the one scheduled cancellation observed read `active` in claude.ai's `subscription_details`); the `cedar_ember` cards, each with `resets_left`, `ends_at`, `usable_now` and `clears`; every dollar block with a positive `limit_dollars`, as a credit (the claude.ai frontend reads `iguana_necktie` as the claimed cloud-session credit, its expiry from the claim status's `expires_at` and failing that from the block's `resets_at`, and so does the sensor); the cloud credit's claim status, normalised as Claude Code normalises it, with when it was last read; and what was lost, one entry per card or credit, each recorded once with why: every card the last good read held unused and every credit with money left, `lapse` when a later profile read finds the plan lapsed (but `expired` for one whose end, as last seen, that read finds already past: past its end it was gone whatever the plan did), or `expired` when an `ok` read after its end, as that read gives the end, still shows it unused or unspent or no longer lists it (a read that is not `ok`, or whose cards block is missing or ineligible, records no card expiry). A login folder that now answers with another identity starts afresh: nothing of the previous account is shown for it, used for it or recorded lost, and that includes the previous claim status, which would otherwise date the new account's cloud credit. A read that fails keeps the last good cards, credits and claim status beside its status. An `ok` read whose cards block is missing or ineligible keeps the last block that listed cards and records itself beside it (`cards_unlisted`: when, the ineligible reason or a missing block, and when the cards were last listed); with no such block it keeps its own. Cards so kept are judged lost from the read that last listed them, as they are across failed reads, and their alerts say when they were last listed and which read listed none and why (a failed read since changes neither). `subfleet cards --help` says that a read, `--refresh`'s or the timer's, may spend a heal turn. `status`, `status.json` (`claude.cards`) and the alerts read the snapshot, and show nothing while `claude_cards.enabled` is false. `status` and `subfleet cards` say "no reset card" only for a login whose cards block lists none (a card that ended unused is listed), and their line for each warning names the card or credit it is about, a loss's included. An alert (`warn`) names an unused card ending within `claude_cards.warn_days` (5); an unused card, or a credit with money left, on a plan that is lapsing (a subscription status other than `active`, or an end declared in `<state root>/claude-plan-ends.json`, by login or lane, within `warn_days` and before the card or credit ends: none of the reads observed showed a cancellation scheduled for the end of a billing period, and claude.ai's `subscription_details`, which does, refuses OAuth tokens); money left on a credit that expires within `warn_days`; and an unexpired credit the account is eligible for and has not claimed, on a claim status read within two days. These are sent again daily and clear without a "recovered" notice. Each lost card or credit is alerted once, for `warn_days` after it was seen. Nothing in Subfleet redeems a card or claims a credit: no code calls `POST /api/organizations/{org}/reset_rate_limits` or a promotion's claim, and the heal's prompt is a fixed reply request. Spending a card is the operator's act: a card is worth more held for a model launch, and the Codex rule (one at a time, only for a waiting job) applies with more force.
- **C-10.1** A lane row is an immutable binding of provider, account key, credential reference, credential epoch, optional home, `owner` (`v1` or `v2`), and `desktop` flag. The credential reference is a keychain item name (`claude-quota-<email>`, as v1) or a home directory path; the store never holds a secret.
- **C-10.2** Enrolment probes before recording: Codex reads `auth.json`, refuses an API-key login and a free plan, and probes the usage endpoint; Claude runs one Haiku turn under the token with `--output-format stream-json --verbose` and reads the `rate_limit_event`. A re-enrollment runs only that turn through its process fence; the keychain reads that resolve a keychain-token credential run outside it.
- **C-10.3** A lane whose Claude identity (C-10.6) equals the identity of the desktop app's keychain credential is `desktop`. It is refused while Claude Code is using the desktop login, unless the job has `allow_desktop`, and is otherwise a candidate that sorts after every other lane (C-11.3) (2026-09-27, Max: excluding it "makes sense if we're using sf thru the cc app but not if thru the sf app"; until then it was never a candidate without `allow_desktop`). Claude Code uses the login while its own registry, `~/.claude/sessions/<pid>.json`, has a row that is alive, is not a Subfleet run, and shows use. Alive: its pid is live and, where it records `procStart`, that process started then (`registry.validated`, against one process-table read; a stale row whose pid another process reuses is dead, and one without `procStart`, an older Claude Code, is alive on its pid). Not a Subfleet run: an `sdk-*` row is left out only when a live attempt owns its process (its process group is the attempt's `pgid`, or it is the attempt's child) or runs its session (`native_session_id`); the entrypoint alone proves nothing about the credential, so any other headless run counts like the Claude app's rows (`claude-desktop`) and a terminal's (`cli`). Use: a `busy` status, or a status time (`statusUpdatedAt`, else `updatedAt`) within `admission.desktop_recent_s` (1800) of now; a row with no status, or a status and no time to judge it by, is unknown and counts, whatever its start. A registry directory that cannot be listed is use, and so is each row file whose process is live but whose content cannot be read or whose `pid` is missing, not a number, or not the one the file is named for; an absent directory is none (`registry.listing`, `registry.desktop_login_in_use`). The daemon reads it at most every 2 s, never under the store lock, as part of the one registry read C-6.9's classes use (`Daemon._registry_read`: rows and answer are kept together, reused for 2 s after the read finished however long it took), keeps a read only if none that began later was kept meanwhile (two reads may overlap; the slower never replaces the newer, whatever either found), ages the answer from when that read began, and a reservation treats an answer older than `DESKTOP_IN_USE_MAX_AGE_S` (10 s) as use; and puts the answer on the desktop lane as `desktop_in_use`, a `scheduler.LANE_FACTS` field; a desktop lane without the field is in use. The reservation reads it again: the answer is refreshed off the lock before each reservation try, and `_route_rows` marks the desktop lane with it, so C-6.3's check judges that lane again when Claude Code became active (or idle) since the evaluation (review of the uncap plan: reusing the view's answer reserved the lane after Claude Code became active). The admission-probe reservation refuses the lane only while it is in use, reading the answer refreshed just before its transaction as the attempt reservation does. `status.json` judges the lane with the same signal (`Timers.desktop_in_use`). Each change, and the first answer after a start, is a `desktop.in_use` event (`in_use`, `was`, and the counts of `busy`, `recent`, `unknown` and `subfleet` rows that decided it), written by admission (at the start of the detached pass, and after each refresh a reservation or an admission probe places by, so a change a job was placed by is on record) and never by a read op (`status`, `why`, `lanes`), under a lock of its own: a read op never waits for that write (C-3.7). Timers (usage probes, keepalive, touch) still never use the desktop lane, and `status.json` marks it dispatchable only while it is not in use.
