The desktop identity comes from the profile endpoint using the keychain credential itself, re-read each probe cycle; `~/.claude.json` `oauthAccount` is a hint that `doctor` compares against the profile and reports when they disagree, never the authority. While the desktop identity cannot be verified, every lane whose label matches either the cached email or the last verified desktop identity is treated as `desktop`. For Codex, `~/.codex` is observed and never a lane, and a numbered lane that shares the app's account is `app_shadowed`: dispatchable, ordered like any other lane, and last for automatic reset credits (C-23.46).
- **C-10.4** A lane with `owner: v1` is never a candidate. Ownership changes only by `subfleet lanes transfer <lane> --to v2` (or `--to v1`), which records an event. For a Codex lane the transfer also relocates the home: `~/.codex-<n>` moves to `<state root>/lanes/<lane id>/` on `--to v2`, before ownership flips, and back on `--to v1`, after it flips, each in one rename. v1 discovers Codex homes by directory glob from every caller (CLI, gates, launch agents) and reads no roster, so the relocation is the fence and the roster entry is the record. The lane keeps its id: the credential is unchanged, only its path moved. A transfer to v2 is refused while v1's ledger shows a run live on that home; a transfer back is refused while a v2 attempt is live on the lane.
- **C-10.5** The credential value is resolved inside the adapter's launch builder and placed only in the child's environment. It is never logged, never written to `lane.log`, never in `manifest.json`.
- **C-10.6** A Claude identity is the pair (`account_uuid`, `org_uuid`) returned by `https://api.anthropic.com/api/oauth/profile` when requested with exactly the credential that produces the lane's usage reading. A usage reading is recorded only when the profile identity equals the lane's recorded identity. When the profile is unavailable, the reading is stored with evidence `identity-unverified` and no windows; when it differs, with evidence `identity-mismatch` and no windows, and the lane is not a candidate until an operator re-enrols it. A setup-token lane whose token lacks profile scope keeps the identity recorded at enrolment, and its stream readings carry evidence `identity-enrolled`. Incident: on 2026-09-05 v1 attributed the Axiom account's usage to RulesAtlas because it trusted `~/.claude.json`; the evidence is `~/axiom-eng-briefs/opus-expiry-20260905/identity-fix-live-result.json`.
- **C-10.7** A desktop credential and an enrolled inference-token lane are distinct lanes even when their labels match; identity, not email, joins two credentials to one account, and `doctor` reports two enabled lanes with one identity as a defect (C-23.45).

## 11. Routing evaluation

- **C-11.1** `policy.json` is the routing data. Required keys: `tiers`, `chains` (task to ordered tier entries, each a short model name or a non-empty preference list of distinct current short model names), `fallback: "upward-only"`, `permissions` (task to sandbox; a submit that names no sandbox, `sandbox: "policy"`, gets its task's entry, else `*`, else read-only, and an isolated review or gate round always reads only; a writable result outside a git repository is refused with the fix `-s read-only`, never downgraded; d261, 2026-09-24), `models` (short name to provider, id, optional effort and scope), `retired` (alias to short name), `desktop_login: "never"`, `caps` (C-6.4), `reset_credits`. Optional `network: {codex_workspace_write: bool}` (d260, default false in code, true in the shipped policy): a writable, non-isolated Codex job gets `-c sandbox_workspace_write.network_access=true`, and a Codex conversation turn under `bypass` gets `networkAccess: true` (under `ask` and `accept-edits` a network command still reaches the person as an approval); wherever the network is granted the launch also carries `-c features.unified_exec=false` (C-23.6), because unified exec's `write_stdin` feeds a shell text the never-rules guard never sees; read-only and isolated launches never get the network. The file's SHA-256 is the policy hash on every job.
- **C-11.2** A chain entry is a short model name or a non-empty list of distinct known, non-retired short model names in preference order. Evaluation for a `--task`/`--tier` job flattens the entries from the tier's index upward, removes duplicates keeping the first occurrence, and walks that list in order. Within a tier, earlier models are preferred: a later model is tried only when no lane of an earlier model admits the job. Order across tiers is unchanged, and `fallback: upward-only` never uses an entry below the job's tier. For each model, candidates are lanes of the model's provider that are enabled, `owner: v2`, not `desktop` while Claude Code uses the desktop login (unless allowed, C-10.3), not in the job's exclusions, not under an unexpired closure for scope `account` or the model id, and with a free slot (C-6.4; always, while no per-lane cap is set). The first model with a candidate wins. A `-m` pin evaluates one model and never falls back; `-a` or `-H` evaluates one lane. A pinned job evaluates one model, its `-m` model or else the first model of its task's chain from its tier, and so runs only on that model's provider. The pin is resolved to one lane id by `scheduler.resolve_lane` once, at submit, against the lanes admission evaluates named as the capacity view names them (each lane's row with its latest probe verdict merged in, as `Timers.enrich_view` merges it, so a Codex lane also answers to the email its usage probe reported, and keeps it through a probe that could not read the account; the same roster serves admission and recovery), and the job stores that lane id. The name as typed is kept in the `job.submitted` event (`pin.requested`, `pin.lane_id`) and, except for a job carrying an unmeasured-reserve authorization, in the request digest; a retry of an accepted request whose name no longer resolves to one lane is answered from the lane it was accepted on, so a retry still finds its job (C-6.2). An authorized job's digest binds the lane id it was granted for (C-11.7a), so its retry after the name comes to name another lane is refused as a different payload. A lane id matches only itself, except that a disabled lane whose credential an enabled lane now holds (a re-enrolment, C-10.2: the same account on the same credential) resolves to that lane, so a job pinned before a re-enrolment runs on its successor, and attempts on a lane and on its successor are attempts on one lane when retries and exclusions are counted (C-4.5: one same-lane retry, then the next candidate), a resume included, whose launch accepts the successor of the lane it recorded because the native session lives under the credential's home, not under a lane id (C-12.3); a job carrying an unmeasured-reserve authorization never follows, because the authorization names the lane id it was granted for (C-11.7). Any other name (an account key or its account part, a label, a Codex email, a home) may match several lanes, and narrowing drops only lanes that could never take the job: lanes of another provider than the job's, taken from the job's model or task, else from the flag that pinned it (`-a` a Claude account, `-H` a Codex home, sent as `pinned_provider`), a disabled lane when an enabled lane that also matches is bound to the same credential, and a lane whose credential proved to hold another account (C-10.6) when another lane of the same provider also matches (across providers the name stays ambiguous); such a lane keeps the email its probe read as `observed_email`, never as a name it answers to. A name given with `-a` or `-H` names a lane of that flag's provider only, so a job whose model runs on the other provider is refused at submit (exit 2), as v1 refused `-a` with a Codex model; a lane id says its own provider whatever flag carried it. A name that still matches several lanes is refused at submit with exit 2 naming the lane ids (`-a <lane id>` pins one). Resolving once means no later roster change (a probe reporting an email, a lane enrolled) can make an accepted pin ambiguous. A job accepted before this rule may still carry a name: each daemon start rewrites the pin of every unfinished job whose name resolves to one lane (`job.pin_canonicalized`, from and to; a job carrying an unmeasured-reserve authorization is never rewritten) and logs one line for each it leaves. One that names several lanes is refused at admission (C-6.12). One that names no lane (`pinned lane ... is unknown`, C-17.3) is a pin no lane can admit: C-11.8 holds it as `unknown`, tells its caller, and fails it after the grace; `subfleet doctor` fails its `unfinished jobs pin lanes by id` row while any job carries such a pin; a repair that raises is logged and skipped, never holding up recovery. Exclusions (`-x`) are not narrowed: a name excludes every lane it matches, which errs toward not running. A route evaluation (`Daemon._pick`: admission and its check of a transient retry's pinned pair, `why`, `run --dry-run`) reads only the rows a route can depend on: the active attempts (`ROUTE_ATTEMPTS`, by the `attempts_live` index and without `evidence_json`) and the jobs that have a parent or an active attempt (`ROUTE_JOBS`), because only an active attempt occupies a slot or counts against a parent's cap, only a job with a parent extends an ancestry, and an active attempt's job says by its `kind` whether it counts as a turn (C-26.9); status and the operator's views still read every row. C-6.3's reservation recheck (`_route_rows`) takes its known jobs from these rows and reads any other job on an ancestry by id, a few primary-key lookups, and reaches the same answer as from every row (a differential property test). Over any store, `scheduler.evaluate` reaches the same decision over these rows as over every row (a differential property test over stores of detached and turn jobs with parent chains and cycles and attempts in every state, for detached and turn candidates; measured 2026-10-02 on `main` with `tools/measure_idle_cost.py --running 26 --history 2500 --waiting 12 --evidence-bytes 8192` on the live daemon's interpreter build, three alternating rounds: with every lane closed and 12 jobs rechecked, 8.9%, 8.3% and 8.8% of a core before, 1.5% in each round after; with 26 running, 24.0%, 19.0% and 21.5% before, 14.9%, 15.5% and 16.3% after).
- **C-11.3** Every comparator starts with the lane's `desktop` flag (false first, C-10.3), then for a turn `lane_id != affinity_lane` (false first, C-26.2), then load band `in_flight // admission.lane_spread` in the job's pool (default 2; null means band 0). The band spreads a burst before usage readings move (2026-09-27); it adds no cap or waiting. Claude next keeps its stranded term `not stranded_scopes` (false first, C-23.37); both providers then use `not ranking_measured` (false first), weekly reserve class, five-hour reserve class, binding weekly reset ascending, weekly headroom descending, in-flight ascending and lane id. A reserve class is true only when the corresponding known headroom is strictly below `admission.weekly_reserve` (default 0.02) or `admission.five_hour_reserve` (default 0.10); false sorts first. Both are finite fractions in [0, 1], preference only: every otherwise eligible lane stays eligible. Codex carries a five-hour window when its primary or secondary window has duration 300 minutes (C-9.7), so both providers receive the five-hour guard. Weekly headroom is `1 - utilization` of the least-headroom fresh applicable `seven_day` window (account or requested-model scope); its own reset binds too. Equal weekly headrooms bind the earliest known reset, then scope. A missing reset sorts after known resets within the same preceding keys and reserve class. If any latest applicable provider window observed no later than now and within `caps.reading_ttl_s` has a reset at or before now, the lane ranks as unmeasured until another reading arrives or that window ages past the TTL, with no invented utilization. Stopped windows outside the TTL cannot demote the lane indefinitely. This is ranking uncertainty only: admission freshness (`measured`), pre-launch probes and pick exclusions keep their existing predicates. Missing/stale readings remain eligible but unmeasured; weekly and five-hour headroom are unknown when unmeasured, reserves are false, reset unknown, then in-flight and lane id decide. Fresh means numeric provider evidence observed no later than now, at most `caps.reading_ttl_s` old (default 120 seconds), with any reset still future. Eligibility still uses the existing applicable-window utilization floor (`headroom_floor` default 0.15; the live policy remains 0.0), slots, closures and C-11.7 model reserve. C-11.7 slack no longer orders candidates. Defaults are justified by the seven-day replay in `docs/reports/2026-10-03-rank-earliest-reset.md`. A lane being proven (C-6.14) is no candidate for a detached job: the hold refuses it before this ranking, and the load band orders only what is left, so the band spreads work across the lanes that can take it but never stops the one lane above its floor taking every job, which the hold does; a proven lane rejoins at its band.
- **C-11.4** Before dispatching expensive work (writable, or tier `hard`) to an unmeasured lane, the daemon runs a probe with the model the job wants (C-10.2 style); a `limited` result closes the scope; `ok` records `admission-observed`. For other work the job's first attempt is the probe. A conversation turn never triggers a probe (C-26.9). A probe launch uses the fixed prompt `Reply with exactly OK. Do not use tools.` in a private read-only directory under the state root and carries `SUBFLEET_PROBE=1` in the child environment, so tooling and the fake providers can tell a probe from work. The probe's reservation re-checks, inside its transaction, that the lane is enabled, is not the desktop login while Claude Code uses it (C-10.3, the answer refreshed just before), and has no credential a timer found revoked or unusable since the evaluation (review of PR #72). A probe that ends `ok` proves its lane (C-6.14), as a keepalive or heal turn that ends `ok` does. A probe still running at its 60 s deadline, counted from its reservation (the instant its lease is taken, not before the wait for the store's writer lock; review of #153), is stopped there and says so (`stopped: deadline` in its record and in the `probe.completed` evidence, however the probe completes: by its receipt, by recovery, or with an adapter that raised; a probe whose guardian was already gone at the deadline was stopped by nobody and says nothing; a look at the guardian that failed is no evidence that it is gone, C-5.3): what the adapter reads off a stream cut short is not the provider's own ending. On 2026-10-03, 23 of the 25 probes that read `unknown` or `transient` had run to the deadline; the `transient` ones read so only because Codex prints `failed to refresh available models: request timed out` at startup, as 60 of the 64 first Codex attempts of the jobs submitted in that window did, 53 of which succeeded (`docs/reports/2026-10-08-probe-vehicle-starvation.md`). After a probe of a model on a lane ends other than `ok` with a binding identity (C-10.6), `limited` or `auth-dead`, and was not quarantined, the job's next probe of that model goes to a lane of that model it has not had such an answer on this round: the first `evaluate` ranks once those lanes are left out (`Daemon._probe_choice`; in that decision they read `excluded`). A lane that needs no probe there is taken as it is; a model the chain promotes to is never taken this way, at the reservation either: if the check (C-6.3), with those lanes still out, walks on past the model rotation chose to a later one (`scheduler.promoted_past`; an earlier model of the chain is no promotion and is reserved), or chooses no lane where a lane left out was refused for that alone (`scheduler.left_out_only`), the job is not reserved: it is held `probe-pending` on C-6.10's clock and looks again when that is due (a verdict that holds whatever was left out, such as a full fleet, is recorded as any is; review of #154); when no other lane of the model would take the job, the round starts again; a lane pin has no other lane to go to. The same choice passes over a lane whose probe is an older job's turn (C-6.9), and a lane whose probe this look could not reserve (its `slot:0` held, the lane disabled, moved to v1 or the desktop's, or its credential latched since the evaluation), which the next look tries again (review of #153: a timer that took codex-1's slot between each evaluation and reservation kept a job from ever probing, while it held codex-2's turn). So a lane whose probes keep saying nothing costs a job one probe a round, not every probe, and every lane that would take a job is probed for it within one round, which bounds how long a job held behind it waits for a lane it could use. The round is held in memory per job, as C-6.10's records are, and a restart forgets it.
- **C-11.5** The decision record is JSON: chain walked, per-model candidate list with each rejected lane and its reason, readings and closures consulted, chosen lane and model, and the policy hash. It is stored per attempt and printed by `subfleet why <job>`; `subfleet run --why` and `--dry-run` print it without dispatching. Each candidate's explanation includes reserve class (clear, weekly, five-hour, weekly+five-hour, or unmeasured), binding weekly scope/reset/headroom, five-hour headroom and reading age in seconds. Judgement records a stable `reading_observed_at`; explanations derive age at the evaluation clock, rather than including a continuously changing age in the lane verdict. Age is the oldest fresh applicable provider window used for ranking; if none is fresh, the oldest latest applicable provider observation, or unknown with no reading. When the eventual model is unspecified, `pick` requires every configured model of the family to qualify and ranks the same aggregate weekly binding window and reserve flags it displays; the existing preceding keys use the first model's evaluation. `pick --all --json` retains each model's detail; text `pick` sends evidence to stderr, preserving its path/email stdout contract.
- **C-11.6** The observed 06:33 case (excluded desktop lane, Opus chain exhausted) must evaluate to Astra with the reason "opus: no candidate lanes after exclusions; promoted".

- **C-11.9** Human `subfleet status` and `status.json` show weekly quota projections per lane and scope: reported `used`, `projected_unused`, `rate_per_hour`, `resets_at`, and `basis` (`trend` or `rate unknown`). Use the nonnegative first-to-last slope over 24 hours of provider or stale-provider observations sharing lane, scope and reset; fewer than two observations or less than one hour of span means unknown rate and all remaining quota unused. For the forecast, advance usage along the trend from its observation to now, keeping a fixed trend stable as time passes; displayed `used` remains reported. Clamp unused to zero through reported remaining quota; omit expired windows. Human totals sum account windows once per lane, separately per provider, naming the last included reset and unknown rates. This is a projection from the provider's own readings, not a promise. History is bounded to 24 hours in the status snapshot and never changes admission. [owner unit; module `subfleet/quota_projection.py`, `subfleet/render.py`, `subfleet/status_json.py`]

## 12. Adapters

- **C-11.7** Reserve rule. A Claude Max account carries a shared weekly window (`seven_day`, all models) and a model-scoped weekly window for each reserved model (`reserve.models`, today `fable`). Use of the reserved model counts against both; use of any other model counts against the shared window only, so the shared window is what ends a week of the reserved model. For a job whose model is not reserved, a lane is a candidate only when fresh complete `oauth-usage` readings, or shared/scoped readings paired by event time and attempt from one `rate_limit_event`, give slack = (1 − shared utilization) − `reserve.cap_ratio` × (1 − reserved utilization) ≥ `reserve.min_slack`: a lane with no fresh paired measurement is rejected `reserve:<model>:unmeasured` unless an active provider-reported model-only exhaustion proves the reserved model cannot use capacity before its reset; that case requires a fresh supervised probe of the requested model and never overrides fresh measured reserve restrictions, a lane below the slack `reserve:<model>:reserved`, and a lane whose usage read shows no reserved window is free. Candidates that pass this model reserve follow C-11.3's weekly comparator; slack remains admission evidence and is not a ranking key. `cap_ratio` is the reserved bucket's size relative to the shared one; the payload does not state it, so it is set above any ratio the observed accounts allow (2.0 on 2026-09-06, from an account at 94 shared and 49 reserved, which bounds it below 1.92) and lowered only from measurement. Incident: 2026-09-05, Opus lanes on two accounts exhausted their shared window and ended their Fable week with half the Fable bucket unused.
- **C-11.7a** An operator may explicitly authorize only an unmeasured reserve verdict for one new dispatch using `--allow-unmeasured-reserve REASON` with both lane and model pinned. The daemon resolves the lane to its enrolled ID, persists the reason/evidence (nonblank, at most 2,000 characters), includes it in request idempotency and audit data, and requires a fresh same-model admission probe before reserving the work attempt, even for standard read-only jobs or previously observed admissions. The decision continues to call reserve capacity unmeasured. This exception never overrides measured reserve restrictions, account/model closures, identity or desktop protection, ownership, exclusions, or capacity caps; never rotates accounts or promotes models; and is not inherited by subsequent jobs or resumes. Dry-run remains side-effect free. An unsuccessful or identity-unbound probe cannot authorize work. Tests: `tests/unit/test_scheduler.py`, `tests/fake/test_reserve_authorization.py`.
- **C-11.8** A lane pin that can never admit its job holds nobody back, is said once to its caller, and ends after a grace. A lane refuses a job for a *standing* reason when no wait for capacity ends it: the job's own exclusions name it (`excluded`); it is the desktop login while Claude Code uses it (or that cannot be told) and the job lacks `--allow-desktop` (`desktop`, C-10.3); the job is a turn and the lane has its own config directory (`config-dir`, C-26.2); it is owned by v1 (`owner-v1`), disabled (`disabled`), or its credential proved to hold another account (`identity-mismatch`, C-10.6); its last probe found its credential revoked or missing, or, on a Codex lane, its token expired after the one heal its credential epoch allows had run (`credential-latched`, with `capacity.credential_gone`: a revoked epoch; `revoked`, `auth-revoked`, `no-auth`; or `expired-token` with `heal_spent`, which the timers' probe sets on a Codex lane when a `timer.heal` for its current epoch has run and the token is still expired, C-23.47), which only a new login or a re-enrolment ends; or it is closed for scope `account` or the job's model until more than `admission.pin_hold_far_s` from now (`closed:<scope>:<until>`; default seven days, the longest usage window Subfleet reads, `seven_day`: a closure that ends later is a hold, such as an operator's `lanes hold --until`, rather than a wait). Any other expired token latches the lane's slot too but may still come back with no one acting, so it is not standing: one no heal has been tried on yet, and one on a Claude home lane, whose heal the timers retry every 20 minutes (C-23.47). A job pinned to such a lane waits as for capacity and is neither told nor failed here; the timers' alerts already tell the operator of the expired token, with the login command (`alerts.py`). Nor are a slot, a reading, the floor, the reserve, or a nearer closure, which are capacity. A pin that names no lane in the roster is `unknown`. A decision carries no lane's credential, so a latch is judged on the lane's row (`scheduler.standing_refusals` takes it; `refused_for_good` is given the pass's lane rows), and `evaluate`'s decisions are exactly what they were. `scheduler.pin_unadmittable` resolves the pin and judges its lane exactly as `evaluate` does (`prepare`, `judge_lane`) and keeps only the standing reasons (`scheduler.standing_refusals`), so when it names one, `evaluate` over any view with the same lane rows, closures and latched credentials places the job nowhere, whatever the readings, attempts, probes and caps; and when it names none, the job is placed on its lane in the best capacity those facts allow (every count cap lifted, every lane measured idle, nothing in flight, every nearer closure ended, every expired token that may still heal healed). A pin whose route cannot be evaluated is C-6.12's, not this clause's. Admission asks it on every pass of every queued or waiting job with a lane pin, except one waiting on `approval` or `uncertain` (a person's to end), after the `max_wall_s` check, a route wait's hold (C-6.12) and a turn's conversation hold (C-24.5), and before C-6.9's order, the machine guard (C-6.13), a workspace or a probe. It reads only the lane rows marked and merged as a view marks them (`capacity.mark_desktop`, `Timers.merge_lane`, as C-6.3's check does), the closures open now, and the latched credentials (`Daemon._pin_view`, read once per pass and only by a pass that needs it); whether Claude Code uses the desktop login is asked only when a lane is the desktop's, and the turn pass takes the last answer read (`_desktop_answer`, unknown reading as in use) rather than list the registry itself. A job so refused is held `pin-unadmittable` (C-6.11) and holds nobody back (C-6.9). The first pass that finds it begins an episode: one `job.pin_unadmittable` event (`lane_id`, `reasons`, `closures`, `since`, `fail_at`), the job `waiting` on `capacity` with `fail_at` as its `next_check_at` (30 s out when it never fails; admission looks at the job every pass whatever its clock), and one `daemon.log` warning. Once the episode has lasted `PIN_NOTICE_AFTER_S` (60 s), so that a state one pass saw (a re-enrolment between its two commits, a registry read that failed once) tells nobody anything false, the job gets its one notice, unless the store holds a `job.pin_noticed` event for it already that no withdrawal followed: a job that ran and came back, or outlived a restart, is never told twice. The notice is a `service_notices` row for its caller's session (else the policy's `alerts.operator_session`, as `ping` addresses one; with neither there is no one to tell, C-15.8, so no row is written, the `job.pin_noticed` event is recorded with no notice, the job is still told at most once, and `why` and `status.json` show its hold), recorded by a `job.pin_noticed` event naming it (id, session and creation time): `<job id>: waiting; its pinned lane <lane> can never admit it: <why>.`, then the fix (resubmit it unpinned, or pinned to another lane with `-a` or `-H`, then `subfleet kill <job id>`; or make the lane usable again: re-enable or re-enrol it, release its hold, or stop using the desktop login in Claude Code), when it fails, and that it holds no other job back. `notice.pending` names its job (`job_id`, from that event, matched by id, session and creation time together, because a service notice's id is reused once the newest row is deleted: it has no AUTOINCREMENT), so the session hooks surface it, also in a session Subfleet launched (C-15.2, C-26.13), as a message rather than a run's end (C-15.3); a ping, a nudge or an alert still names none, even one that got an old pin notice's id. A pin notice still `pending` (shown to nobody) is withdrawn, matched the same way, with a `job.pin_notice_withdrawn` event, in the same transaction as the job's terminal notice however the job ends (`Daemon._notice`), and when its episode ends because the lane can admit it again: it is no longer true, and one withdrawn unread does not count as the job's one notice. When a pass finds that the lane could admit it again (capacity allowing), the episode ends with a `job.pin_admittable` event and the job is due at once, not at the failure its episode had set. A job whose episode has lasted `admission.pin_grace_s` (default 1800 s; null never fails it and the notice says it waits until that changes; 0 fails it on the pass that finds it, and any grace under `PIN_NOTICE_AFTER_S` fails it before its service notice would go, since its terminal notice says it all; `fail_at` is computed from the one clock read that pass makes) fails with rc 3 (C-17.3): its terminal notice (C-15.1) reads `no lane: its pinned lane <lane> could never admit it from <since> on: <why>; fix: ...`, a `job.pin_refused` event carries the lane and the reasons, `why` prints that as `Refused at admission: ...`, and a worktree admission cut for the job and no attempt used is removed, as C-6.12's are. Never while an attempt of it is live or quarantined: it is held until that settles. A conversation turn is held and nothing more, with no notice and no failure: its message is the person's, and the conversation carries its end (C-26.12). The episode's clock survives a restart: the daemon reads each unfinished job's episode from those events when it opens the store. Why fail and not only notify: a notice that is not a job's end reaches a session only at its next prompt or start (C-15.2 layer 3), while the caller of a pinned job is usually an agent blocked in `subfleet wait` or armed by the PostToolUse hook (layers 1 and 2), which wake only on a terminal state (`waits.TERMINAL`); and a job that never started has no `max_wall_s` to end it, so it would otherwise wait for good. The grace lets a quick fix land first: the desktop login switched back or Claude Code closed, a lane re-enrolled (a pin follows a re-enrolment to its successor, C-11.2), a hold released. A job with no lane pin that every lane of every model of its chain refuses for a standing reason (a model whose provider has no lane enrolled is `no-lanes`) keeps its hold as before and is neither told nor failed here, since the fix is the fleet's, not the caller's, but it too holds nobody back (C-6.9): `scheduler.refused_for_good` asks it of each decision with no lane, and `scheduler.unadmittable` of a job on its clock (once per model and job shape per pass), and its hold carries `for_good`. Incident: 2026-09-29 ~21:53Z, job 20260929-134236-salvage-r3-fix was submitted with `-a claude-9`; an account switch made claude-9 the desktop login; `subfleet why` said `no lane admits it (desktop)`, the job stayed queued with no word to its caller, and C-6.9's FIFO held 38 younger standard Opus jobs behind it while claude-7 was open. Review of 76c81f9c (2026-09-30): the one notice rested on memory a pass forgets while the job runs; an expired token was called "never"; the notice never reached a session Subfleet launched; grace 0 read the clock twice; a job no lane can admit kept a lease's place on its clock; and `notice.mark` never marked a service notice (C-15.3). Its re-review (fc6c146d): an expired token whose one heal was spent is standing (the fix for the first finding had exempted every expired token); a withdrawn notice's reused id labelled a later ping with the job; and an unread notice rode along with a job that ended some other way. Round 3 (b917c466): a Claude home lane's heal is retried every 20 minutes, so its expired token is not standing either, and only a Codex lane's spent heal is.
- **C-12.1** `subfleet/adapters/base.py` defines the interface; adapters return data and never touch the store. Methods: `enroll(credential) -> LaneInfo`, `probe(lane) -> list[Reading]`, `build_launch(job, attempt, lane, credential_env) -> Launch`, `classify(attempt_dir, exit_info) -> Outcome`, `attest(attempt_dir, launch) -> Attestation`, `deliverable(attempt_dir, launch) -> bytes | None`, `resume_launch(job, attempt, lane, prompt) -> Launch | None`.
- **C-12.2** A `Launch` is argv, env additions, cwd, and the paths for stdout, stderr, and any raw stream file, plus `native_session_id` when the adapter chose it up front (Claude `--session-id`).
- **C-12.3** Codex launch mirrors v1's `bin/subfleet-codex` argument construction: `codex exec --json -m <model id>`, effort via `-c model_reasoning_effort=<effort>` when the model has one, `--sandbox <read-only|workspace-write>`, `-c hooks=<guard override>` when the guard is armed, `--output-last-message <attempt dir>/last.md`, the prompt on stdin, `CODEX_HOME=<home>`, `CODEX_API_KEY` and `OPENAI_API_KEY` removed from the environment. The thread id is read from the `thread.started` event. `codex exec resume <thread id>` on the same home is the resume launch.
- **C-12.4** Claude launch: `claude -p --model <model id> --session-id <uuid4> --output-format stream-json --verbose`, permission flags per sandbox as v1 `bin/subfleet-claude` builds them, with the writable MCP restriction in C-12.9, `CLAUDE_CODE_OAUTH_TOKEN=<token>` (or `CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR=<home>` for a lane home), `ANTHROPIC_API_KEY` removed from the environment, the prompt on stdin. `claude -p --resume <session id>` is the resume launch.
- **C-12.5** Attestation: Codex from the rollout's model field; Claude from the transcript located by session uuid under `~/.claude/projects/`, per-message `model` within the attempt's recorded byte range, exactly one transcript match required. Result is `attested`, `mismatch` (with served model), or `unattested`; never a false positive.
- **C-12.6** Deliverable: Codex from `last.md` when present, else the last `item.completed` agent message in the JSON stream. Claude from the transcript's final assistant text within the attempt's range (v1 `prefer_transcript_text` rule), else the stream's `result` text. An empty deliverable with rc 0 is class `unknown`, not `ok`.
- **C-12.7** Fixtures: `tests/fixtures/<provider>/<case>/` with `stdout`, `stderr`, `rc`, and `expected.json`; cases at minimum: success, hard limit with clock, hard limit without clock, model-scoped limit, credits rejection (the experiment-0 Fable payload), auth dead, refresh-token revoked, CLI too old, content filter, stream disconnect, model downgrade (Claude), spawn failure. Fixtures are redacted: no tokens, no cookies, no `Authorization` values.
- **C-12.8** Fake providers `tests/bin/codex` and `tests/bin/claude` are Python scripts that read `SUBFLEET_FAKE_SCENARIO` and replay the matching fixture, honour `SUBFLEET_FAKE_DELAY_S`, and for the `nested-setsid` scenario spawn a `setsid` grandchild that sleeps 30 s.
- **C-12.9** Writable detached Claude jobs start with `--dangerously-skip-permissions --strict-mcp-config --mcp-config <config>`; the config is `{"mcpServers":{}}` unless that job names servers with repeatable `subfleet run --mcp NAME`. The daemon resolves only those names from the sources Claude Code would read at the submitted workdir, using its scope precedence ([verified sources and launch audit](reference/claude-mcp.md)); an unknown name is refused at submit with exit 2 and the available names. The job records its opt-in names and a copy of exactly their entries; `runs show` displays the names, and every attempt and resume starts exactly that set from the job's copy. A resume inherits its source's set and cannot change it. A missing or damaged copy refuses launch rather than falling back to ambient configuration. No policy default opts a job into servers, and a job that names servers must route to Claude. Read-only launches, probes, and isolated reviews keep their existing empty MCP configuration and refuse an opt-in. Subfleet.app conversation turns are outside this ruling: their existing permission and MCP behavior is unchanged, including the read-only turn flags.

## 13. Salvage and workspaces

- **C-13.1** Salvage runs only for a writable job, only at `finalizing`, `lost`, or kill, and only when the worktree's tree hash differs from the baseline recorded at `reserved`. It builds a temporary index from the working tree (tracked and untracked, honouring `.gitignore`), `commit-tree` with the baseline as parent, and writes `refs/subfleet-salvage/<branch>-<utc>-<attempt seq>`, the branch sanitized to `[A-Za-z0-9_-]` and cut to 200 characters, so the name is always one git can write. HEAD, the real index, and the working tree are untouched. The ref is recorded as an artifact with role `salvage`. A nested repository with no commit checked out, which git cannot index (a gitlink names a commit), is left out of the snapshot rather than failing it: when `add -A` fails, the untracked nested repositories that `ls-files -o` lists (one `path/` entry each, inside an untracked directory too, honouring `.gitignore`), and those where a tracked file was (`diff-files` names the path, now a directory holding `.git`; its removal is still recorded), and whose HEAD does not resolve (`rev-parse --verify HEAD` exits 1) are excluded by literal pathspecs, and `add -A` runs once more. Nothing git prints decides what is left out, and any other `git add` failure fails the snapshot as before, beside such a repository or not: an unreadable file, or an object git could not write (a read-only `.git/objects/xx`, a full disk). The same holds for the reservation's snapshot (C-6.8) and for a turn's two snapshots (C-26.14), so a turn's diff does not show such a repository; the turn's receipt (`trees.json`) and its evidence (`turn_trees`) list those its end snapshot left out (`skipped`). Salvage runs git under the C locale (`LC_ALL=C`, `LANGUAGE` unset), since it reads git's messages, and with no repository named by the environment (`GIT_DIR`, `GIT_WORK_TREE`, `GIT_COMMON_DIR`, `GIT_OBJECT_DIRECTORY`, `GIT_ALTERNATE_OBJECT_DIRECTORIES`, `GIT_NAMESPACE`, `GIT_QUARANTINE_PATH`, and the daemon's own `GIT_INDEX_FILE`) and no way of reading pathspecs set by the environment (`GIT_LITERAL_PATHSPECS`, `GIT_GLOB_PATHSPECS`, `GIT_NOGLOB_PATHSPECS`, `GIT_ICASE_PATHSPECS`: under the first the exclusion above was read as a file name, and the snapshot failed on the repository as before), which `subfleet daemon start` drops from the daemon's environment too, as a turn's diff (C-26.14) and submit's reading of where a job starts in its worktree do for their own git (review of the P3-3 fix: under the first a path's diff showed no changes, and under `GIT_GLOB_PATHSPECS` or `GIT_ICASE_PATHSPECS` `--literal-pathspecs` was a fatal error), and reads what git prints as the file system names things (`os.fsdecode`), never as strict UTF-8, so a branch or file name that is not UTF-8 is carried, not an error; every salvage error it records (receipt, evidence, notice, audit event) and every workspace error C-6.8 records is valid UTF-8, a byte that is not UTF-8 written `\xNN`, since an error quoting such a name would otherwise make the receipt unwritable and finalization raise on every try. The salvage receipt lists what was left out (`skipped`, each path one line of valid UTF-8, with bytes that are not UTF-8 and control characters escaped), whether or not a ref was written; the attempt's evidence gives `salvage_skipped` (the count and the first five); and the job's notice says `salvage left out <n> nested repositor(y|ies) with no commit, kept only in the worktree: <paths>; the worktree is kept: <path>`. At finalization a transient salvage failure (C-6.8's kinds, including git naming a lock file another git process holds or a full disk) is tried `SALVAGE_TRIES` (3) times in all within one daemon run (the count is in memory, so a restart starts it again); that failure, or any other, is recorded in the receipt and in the attempt's evidence (`salvage_error`), and finalization goes on without a salvage ref, so the attempt ends and frees its lane. The job's notice carries the failure when the job ends with that attempt; an attempt after which the job waits to try again (C-4.5) has it only in its evidence, since a notice is written only at the terminal state (C-15.1). When the job then ends without finalizing another attempt (cancelled while it waits to try again, failed or refused while its retry is prepared, or a retry that was cancelled before it launched or was quarantined), its notice says so (C-15.1) and names that last attempt, `attempt a<seq>: <class>, rc=<rc>: <detail>`, then what its salvage could not save as above (its own start snapshot, `baseline_ref`, holds the work of the attempt before it, so it is not what its salvage saved: read so, a retry whose own salvage failed lost `the worktree is kept`, review of the P2 fix) and the start snapshot held after it (below), `the worktree after attempt a<seq> is held under <ref>`; when that attempt never launched (its guardian could not be started, or its main/master re-check got no answer from git), it changed nothing, so the attempt before it is named the same way, back to one that ran. A retry that reached finalization with no provider started (a spawn error whose receipt names no child: refused on main at launch, a home or credential that could not be resolved, a binary that could not be spawned) changed nothing either, so its notice names the attempt before it the same way after its own summary. A `salvage.baseline_held` event that is not as admission writes it (a ref that is one line of valid UTF-8, a hex commit, a numeric seq, `skipped` as above) is left out, never a reason the cancel or failure cannot commit. It never says only `cancelled before launch` (review of ceacf18b: such a job's notice said that, and nothing of the failed salvage or of the held ref). That attempt's work is then only in the next attempt's start snapshot (C-6.8), a tree object no ref holds, which the next attempt goes on to edit: so when the job's last attempt carries `salvage_error`, admission holds that snapshot under `refs/subfleet-salvage/<job id>-a<seq>-baseline` (a commit on the first admission pass's HEAD; later admission passes reuse this first held snapshot even if the caller edits or commits while the retry waits), records it once in a `salvage.baseline_held` event before reservation, names it in the new attempt's evidence (`baseline_ref`) and records it as that attempt's artifact with role `salvage`; a job that ends before that attempt is reserved (cancelled while it waits for capacity) records the ref as its last attempt's artifact instead, when it ends, and names it in its notice, and a cancel that lands while admission writes the ref, before its event, is answered by admission recording the ref so and saying where it is in one more notice; the nested repositories with no commit that snapshot left out, which no ref can hold, are named in the event (`skipped`) and, for any writable attempt whose start snapshot left one out, in its evidence (`baseline_skipped`, the count and the first five), so retention must preserve those paths in its archive before removing the worktree; failing to hold it, including having no HEAD or baseline after a failed salvage, is a C-6.8 workspace failure, so the job waits or fails and nothing runs in the worktree meanwhile. A quarantine's release (`kill --confirm-dead`) salvages once and records a failure at once: `kill` has answered before the release runs, and the job's notice went out at the quarantine, so a failure or a path left out goes into the release's audit event, the attempt's evidence, and one more notice for the job (its C-15.1 first line, then `released from quarantine (attempt a<seq>)` and the lines above). The worktree is kept (C-13.4) unless retention itself archives and reads back the bytes the snapshot left out. A salvage ref, including one a caller says is referenced elsewhere, never proves preservation of those paths; archive retention copies nested repositories byte for byte instead of recomputing a tree with `add -A`.
- **C-13.2** A private salvage ref may be written from any branch. What is refused, at submit, at admission and again at launch, is a writable job that would write in a checkout on `main` or `master`: an in-place job whose workdir is on one, and any job whose allocated worktree has been switched to one between attempts. A job that is not in place never writes where its caller stands: the daemon cuts it a detached worktree from the caller's head (C-6.6), so it may be submitted from a checkout on `main`, and the caller's HEAD, index and files are untouched. Any push of a salvage ref to a branch named `main` or `master` is refused.
- **C-13.3** Before a new attempt on a writable job, the daemon reconciles: salvage if dirty, record the salvage ref and the current head as the checkpoint, and start the next attempt from the same worktree with a prompt suffix naming the checkpoint. A limit hit after an hour of work loses nothing committed or salvaged.
- **C-13.4** Lane worktrees the daemon allocates live under `$SUBFLEET_HOME/worktrees/`, never `/tmp`; a job's own is `jobs.worktree`, or, while that is NULL, the `worktrees/<job id>` admission allocated before recording it. A path left out of salvage keeps its worktree unless retention's archive itself holds and verifies that path's bytes, even when the salvage ref is referenced elsewhere. They are removed only after the job is terminal, no live conversation turn or reader holds their folder (I5: `folders.TURN` and `folders.READER`, C-8.4), retention has checked those rows and taken its canonical `worktree:<folder>` fence atomically against new turn reservations, and retention has archived and verified them (C-8.4); their registration is removed with them, after every object it names is in the archive's bundle. A worktree allocation that failed, and a conversation worktree rebuilt after an interrupted add, remove only their own unlocked registration, never others by a repository-wide prune.

## 14. Guard prerequisites (milestone 1)

- **C-14.1** The private never-rules hook and TRUST remain byte-for-byte copies of the reviewed v1-derived pair under `private/guard/`, excluded from the wheel and source distribution. The portable core resolves the installed operator overlay at `<state root>/guard/never-rules-hook.sh` and `<state root>/guard/TRUST`; doctor and launch preflight share file validation. Missing, malformed, or mismatched files refuse executable Codex work with code 7. Tests pin the private pair's original SHA-256 values and stage a separate synthetic fixture only inside isolated test state roots. See `docs/private-guard-overlay.md` for installation and old-launch preservation.
- **C-14.2** Before the first executable Codex job on each lane home, `doctor` and the daemon run the trust preflight: the installed `codex` version, the hooks trust hash the guard relies on, and the override string are checked against `TRUST`; a mismatch refuses Codex launches with exit 7 and names the fix. A verdict is keyed on the codex version, the home, the override string, and the seeded-config fingerprint (C-23.5); a re-picked lane whose key differs is verified again before launch.
- **C-14.3** A Codex launch with `workspace-write` always carries the guard override; a Claude launch relies on the global hook and `doctor` reports if it is missing from `~/.claude/settings.json`.
- **C-14.4** The isolation matrix test runs each fake provider under each sandbox and asserts the child could not write outside the workdir in `read-only`, could write inside it in `workspace-write`, and inherited no `CODEX_API_KEY`, `OPENAI_API_KEY`, or `ANTHROPIC_API_KEY`.

## 15. Notices and waiting

- **C-15.1** Every terminal job except a conversation turn writes a `notices` row for its caller session in the same transaction as the terminal state, after that transaction has written the state; a notice for a job whose row is not yet terminal is refused. A job has one such notice, and a quarantine's release whose salvage failed or left a path out writes one more, as does a retry's start snapshot that admission held while a cancel ended the job (C-13.1). The first line is `<job id>: <state>; rc=<rc>; deliverable=<path>; out=<-o path>`, read from the job row inside that transaction: the job's terminal state and rc, the pair `wait`, `runs` and `runs show` report, never the final attempt's outcome class and rc. The deliverable and `-o` paths name files only for an accepted job (`succeeded` with an `accepted_attempt_id`); for any other job both are `-`, whatever its attempt left on disk. The PostToolUse hook's line for a job with no notice row (C-15.2 layer 2) is rendered from the job row by the same function, so one terminal state has one rendering; likewise `wait`'s line names an `-o` file or deliverable only for a `succeeded` job, and `runs show` falls back to the `-o` path only for one. Then the summary: for a job whose last attempt reached finalization, `attempt a<seq>: <class>, rc=<rc>: <detail>`, and when the job did not accept that attempt's non-empty deliverable, `output kept, not accepted: <path>`, with `; -o <path> was not written` when the job has an `-o` path; for a job that ended without one, why (cancelled before launch, quarantined with its census, the refusal, the workspace error of C-6.8). A job that ends between two attempts says so first (`cancelled while waiting to retry`, or `<state> while preparing the retry: ` then the refusal or workspace error), and a retry that ends without reaching finalization names itself (`attempt a<seq>: ` or `attempt a<seq> quarantined: `); then comes the attempt before it (and, past one that never launched, the one before that), its salvage and any held start snapshot, as C-13.1 gives them; a retry that reached finalization with no provider started (a spawn error whose receipt names no child) gives its own summary and then the attempt before it the same way. Then a line for each lane the job moved on from (C-4.5, C-23.44), whatever ended the job. Then any uncertainty (`quarantined`, export failed, unattested) (incident: 2026-09-24, three running Codex jobs were cancelled and correctly became `cancelled` with rc 130, their attempts `interrupted`, nothing accepted and nothing exported, but each notice was written from the attempt, `ok; rc=0; deliverable=<path>; out=<-o path>` and "Codex completed with a deliverable", so the session was told the job had succeeded and where its output was; the hook fallback later announced the same jobs `cancelled; rc=130` from the job row). A conversation turn's outcome is its message's state (C-24.1), which the app shows.
- **C-15.2** Delivery layers, most to least reliable: `subfleet wait` in a background Bash call; a PostToolUse `asyncRewake` hook (milestone 4); SessionStart and UserPromptSubmit hooks surfacing rows with state `pending` or `offered` (inside a process Subfleet launched, only rows that name a job; C-26.13); a best-effort socket push through the v1 mechanism, kept until tickle, muster, and `ping` have tested replacements.
- **C-15.3** Notice states: `pending` (written), `offered` (a delivery attempt was made; transport recorded), `acknowledged` (`notice.ack` from the session, or the session ran `runs show <job>`), `surfaced` (printed by a hook). A notice may be offered more than once; it is acknowledged once. The states are a ladder, `pending` < `offered` < `surfaced` < `acknowledged`, and `notice.mark` never moves a notice down it: an offer whose `notice.pending` read came before a hook surfaced the notice leaves it `surfaced`, so no hook prints a notice a hook has already printed. `notice.mark` and `notice.ack` both take a service notice by its negated id (as `notice.pending` returns it) and set its state in `service_notices`; until 2026-09-30 `notice.mark` updated only `notices`, so a hook that printed a service notice left it `pending` and printed it again at every prompt. The session hooks print every notice that is not a run's end under their own header, `subfleet: <n> message(s) for this session:`, each with the time it was queued, after the runs' header and its footer: a service notice (a `ping` message: a restart nudge, `subfleet ping`, a timer alert, or a pin notice that names its job), told apart by its negated id, or a notice that names no job, such as a v1 outbox message the importer carried over (incident: 2026-09-29, 1,749 service notices were `pending`, and session 1cc3982f was shown service notice 1382, a restart nudge queued two days before, as "1 detached run dispatched by this session finished while it was not running"). `subfleet wait` and `run --wait` return terminal receipts without acknowledging notices, including when the waiter outlives its conversation turn.
- **C-15.4** `wait` is a server-side long poll with a per-call deadline of at most 60 s, counted from when the daemon read the request; the CLI loops until the job set is terminal or `--timeout` expires (exit 124), asking again after a busy answer (C-16.7). `wait` never wakes headless lanes and never targets a lane session.
- **C-15.5** `wait` has one reader, the wait hub (`subfleet/waits.py`). A waiter registers the jobs it waits on before its first read, reads its answer when it starts, and reads again only when the hub wakes it and once more when its deadline passes, so that a job that ended then is answered, not timed out; it never re-reads on another waiter's wake-up, nor on a timer while the hub's thread runs. The hub looks at the store's generation (C-5.11) when poked by `Daemon._notify` and otherwise every `WAIT_POLL_S` (0.1 s). When the generation has moved, a waiter has registered, or `WAIT_RECHECK_S` (1 s) has passed, it reads the state of every watched job and which of them hold an `out:` lease, one statement each whatever the number of waiters, and wakes each waiter whose jobs may all be returned: ended, a succeeded job's export done, or unknown to the store (the waiter then reports the unknown job, as it always did). So a wait whose last job ends by a commit on the daemon's store returns within `WAIT_POLL_S` plus three reads, poked or not: the hub's read in progress when the commit landed, the hub's read that sees it, and the waiter's own; a commit the generation does not count (another process's, C-6.3) is seen within `WAIT_RECHECK_S` plus those reads. No read has a time bound of its own: each takes what SQLite takes, plus up to `READ_WAIT_S` for a read connection (C-3.7). Waiting costs one read per commit for all waiters together, and because registration comes before the first read, no wake-up is lost. A waiter is always woken or times out. A hub pass that raises, in its read or anywhere else, wakes every waiter to read for itself, and the hub goes on. If the hub's thread ends anyway, it wakes every waiter as it goes, and the next registration or the next wait starts another thread. It does not try to start one less than `WAIT_RECHECK_S` after its last try, and a start that fails (no thread to be had) is logged and tried again after that; until a thread runs, each wait is cut to `WAIT_RECHECK_S` and its waiter reads again, and a waiter's registration ends with its wait whatever happened to the hub. `daemon.status` reports the hub under `wait_hub`: its waiters, whether a thread runs, and its reads, deaths, restarts and failed starts. A stopping daemon wakes every waiter, which answers `{"timeout": true}`. Incident: on 2026-09-25 ~21 PostToolUse hooks each held a `wait`, and every worker pass woke every waiter to re-read its jobs behind the store lock.
- **C-15.6** A hook costs one connection per question and no inspection. Its client does not check `daemon.lock` before connecting (`Client(verify_lock=False)`): that check costs a `ps` and a `sysctl` per process, and the PostToolUse hook runs on every Bash call of every session, while a hook exits 0 silently whichever way the daemon is down, which a refused connection already says. Its reads (`list`, `notice.pending`) are C-16.5's lookups.
- **C-15.8** (a notice has a reader) A service notice is written only for a session named: the one a `ping` names, else the one `alerts.operator_session` names; in either, a blank or non-string value names none, and a name is used without the spaces around it. There is no default inbox. Text that is only whitespace is no text, and text that is not a string is refused with exit 2. A `ping` with text that names no session, when no operator session is set, is refused with exit 2 and writes nothing; a `ping` without text is a liveness question and writes nothing, whatever it names (`doctor --live` sends one with no text and no session, and `subfleet ping` with no text, or at a prompt with none piped in, prints `pong`); `subfleet ping` names the session given, else the Claude session it runs in, else none, and leaves the rest of this rule to the daemon; and a timer alert, when no operator session is set, is delivered by being shown (C-18.4) and writes no notice. `notices [--session ID] [--all] [--ack|--withdraw [--reason TEXT]] [--json]`, v1's spelling (C-17.1), lists every session's unresolved (`pending` or `offered`) notices, job and service, or one session's, a service notice under its negated id as `notice.pending` returns it; `--all` adds the `surfaced` and `acknowledged` rows retention still keeps. Listing (op `notice.list`) reads only and marks nothing, so a session's own hooks still surface what was listed, and with no daemon it reads the store (C-17.5). Every listed row carries its `fingerprint` (`store.notice_fingerprint`: its creation time and a digest of its text). An action names one session (`--session`; without it, exit 2) and acts on exactly the unresolved notices the listing returned for it, each only while it is still the row listed: a notice's id is reused once the newest row of its table is deleted (neither table has AUTOINCREMENT), so the action sends each row's fingerprint with its id and the daemon acts on a row only while both match, and a notice written after the listing is untouched; two rows alike in id, session, creation second and text are one notice to the session that reads it. `--ack` acknowledges them (`notice.ack` with fingerprints; without them, as `runs show` and the hooks send it, it acts on ids alone, as before): the operator's acknowledgement, for notices the operator has read. Its answer names the rows it acknowledged and the rows it kept (acknowledged already, or no longer the row listed), and the CLI reports those, not the ids it asked about. `--withdraw` deletes the service notices among them (`notice.withdraw`) and leaves the job notices, naming how many: a job's notice is its terminal record and is acknowledged, never withdrawn, and a `notice.withdraw` that names one is refused with exit 2 and changes nothing. A withdrawal deletes only the rows it names that are that session's, still `pending` or `offered`, and, when the call gives fingerprints (the CLI always does), still the row listed. A row a hook printed (`surfaced`, C-15.3), a session acknowledged or another took the id of since the listing is kept and reported as kept. Fingerprints, when given, are strings, one per id, or the call is refused with exit 2; an id named twice is acted on once, with its first fingerprint, by either action. The deletion happens in one transaction with one `notice.withdrawn` event naming the session, the reason (`--reason`, which needs `--withdraw`, even when empty), each id, the creation span, and how many of each subject went. It claims no delivery, and loses no record of what was withdrawn (incident: 2026-10-03, `alerts.operator_session` had never been set, so from 2026-09-19 every timer alert, about 120 a day, was parked for the literal session `operator`, which no Claude session holds, no hook surfaces, no verb listed and C-23.26 never prunes: 1,637 rows, none read, among them each `claude credentials: ... is auth-dead. Run: ...` line C-23.52 exists to put in front of the operator; every `doctor --live` parked a `doctor` row there too, seven in all).

## 16. Socket protocol

- **C-16.1** Unix domain socket at `daemon.sock`, mode 0600. Newline-delimited JSON, one request then one response per line. Request: `{"v": 1, "id": "<client string>", "op": "<op>", "args": {...}}`. Response: `{"v": 1, "id": "<same>", "ok": true, "result": {...}}` or `{"v": 1, "id": "<same>", "ok": false, "error": {"code": <exit code>, "message": "...", "fix": "..."}}`. The open-file limit, the connection cap and the busy answer are C-16.6 and C-16.7; which pool runs each op is C-16.5.
- **C-16.2** Ops: `submit`, `list`, `show`, `wait`, `kill`, `lanes`, `readings`, `why`, `notice.pending`, `notice.ack`, `notice.mark`, `notice.list`, `notice.withdraw` (C-15.8), `ping`, `daemon.status`, `gate.start`, `gate.poll`, `gate.continue`, `sessions`, `pick`, `operations`, and the section 25 ops. Argument and result shapes are the dataclasses in `subfleet/protocol.py`, except the section 25 ops', which are design §5's (C-25.2); unknown fields are ignored, missing required fields are exit 2.
- **C-16.3** `submit` returns `{job_id, request_id, created: bool}` where `created: false` means an existing job matched the request id. `wait` returns the terminal states of the requested jobs or `{"timeout": true}` after the deadline. A client that connected and sent a request but read no complete, decodable response line (a timeout while sending or reading, a reset or broken pipe, a close with no line, a line past the size cap or one that does not decode) does not know the request's outcome and never reports it as not done: the daemon may have committed it and answered after the client stopped reading, and even a failed send may have delivered the whole line. A refused connection is the one failure that proves nothing was sent (exit 69, C-17.3). `submit` and `kill` are idempotent (C-6.2, C-7.1), so the CLI and the sessions kit send the identical request once more, the same arguments under the same request id, with a 60 s deadline, and its answer is the outcome: `created: false` there is the job the first, unanswered request created, which a request id the CLI minted makes this invocation's own (`created: true` in its output), and a kill answered "already finished" is followed by `show`, because the first kill may be what finished the job, and the CLI prints the job's state and rc. Only if the re-send is also unanswered is the outcome reported as unknown (exit 1, C-17.3), naming the request id and the command that settles it: the same command with `--request-id <id>`, which never creates a second job, or `subfleet kill <job>` again, which is safe; stdout then carries no job id, and a job whose kill is unknown is not waited on. A re-sent submit that is refused does not prove the first created nothing, because submit validates before it looks up the request id and a moved HEAD is a different payload (C-6.2), so the client looks the request id up: `list` accepts `request_id`, and the client filters the rows itself as well, for a daemon that ignores the field (C-16.2). A job found is the answer when the CLI minted the id and is named in the refusal otherwise; none found, the refusal stands; a lookup that fails leaves the outcome unknown. The different-payload refusal names the job that holds the request id. A daemon gone before the re-send leaves a submit's outcome unknown and sends a kill to offline mode (C-17.5). A re-send answered only `busy` (exit 69, C-16.7) was never read and settles nothing, since the first request may still be committing: a submit is still looked up by request id, and a job found is the answer when the CLI minted the id; otherwise, and for a kill, and when no job is found, the outcome is reported as unknown, never as busy (exit 69 would read as nothing sent) and never as refused (merge review 2026-09-25). The message also names `subfleet runs --request-id <id>`, which finds the job whoever its caller session is (a revive's job belongs to the session it continues, so `runs --mine` would miss it); every id in a printed command is shell-quoted, so pasting it cannot change the id. A quarantine resolution (`--confirm-dead`, `--force-release`) whose outcome is unknown is to be repeated with its own flag and note, not as a plain `kill`, which would answer "already finished" and resolve nothing. `revive` and `handoff` tell the settle whether their request id was minted for the call, as `run` does; the CLI mints it before calling them, because the staged prompt is named after it, so it says so explicitly, and only `handoff --request-id` is the operator's. A handoff's brief is rebuilt from a transcript that keeps growing, so for it the lookup is named first and a re-run is said to risk a refusal as a different payload, which names the job and creates none. The unknown entry of a batch names the lookup as well. A dry run creates nothing and is not re-sent (incident: 2026-09-24, a `subfleet run --batch` entry printed "no response from the daemon within 15s" and "NOT submitted" while the daemon had committed it at 11:47:04Z, about a second before the client's 15 s deadline (event 581051); a manual `subfleet run` retry minted a fresh request id and created a duplicate job at 11:50:38Z (event 581065); a kill reported failed three times, with "no response from the daemon within 15s" and then "daemon closed the connection without a response", had committed its cancel at 11:59:01Z (event 581283)).
- **C-16.4** The daemon serves requests on a thread pool; a request handler never blocks on a provider process, a probe, or `ps`. Long-running work is queued to workers with deadlines.
- **C-16.5** A liveness question and a hook's reads never queue behind slow requests. `ping` without `text` (none, or only whitespace, C-15.8) reads nothing and is answered on the connection's own thread. `list`, `show` and `notice.pending` (`LOOKUP_OPS`) run on their own 8-thread pool, `subfleet-read`; `wait` on the waiters' pool; `submit` and `gate.*` on the workers; conversation ops on the conversation pools (the long polls `conversation.events` and `conversation.watch` on their own, `subfleet-poll`, C-29.9; the file work on `subfleet-files`, C-25.3); every other op on the 16-thread request pool, where view builds and writes that wait for the store lock stay. With C-3.7 none of the lookups waits for a transaction either, nor longer than `READ_WAIT_S` for a read connection, and snapshots cannot take the connections kept for them. `wait` and the two conversation long polls, which hold a thread for up to 60 s and 50 s, have a thread for every connection the daemon may hold (C-16.7), so, when every held connection carries one request at a time, as the CLI's, the hooks' and the app's do, no long poll queues behind the others past its deadline. Pipelining breaks that for everyone: one client that sends more waits than the cap on its connections can hold every waiter's thread, and another client's wait, however well behaved, then queues behind them (reviews of 3c8fe55 and 02c6320). Incident: 2026-09-25, all 16 request threads were busy and a `ping` took up to 90 s.
- **C-16.6** The daemon does not exit because `accept` failed for want of a resource. `EMFILE`, `ENFILE`, `ENOBUFS`, `ENOMEM`, `ECONNABORTED`, `EINTR` and `EAGAIN` are waited out (50 ms, doubling to 2 s, reset by the next connection accepted; by sleeping, never by waiting on the stop event, whose lock the signal handler that sets it would meet held on the same thread) and logged on the 1st, 2nd, 4th, 8th consecutive failure with the count of open connections and descriptors; any other `accept` error still ends `serve_forever`. A stop is at most one pause late (review of the descriptor hotfix, F5). Failing without a break for 300 s (`ACCEPT_GIVE_UP_S`), the daemon ends `serve_forever` and exits, so launchd starts a fresh one: with client connections capped (C-16.7), a run that long is a leak outside them, and a daemon alive but deaf would keep the lock. On macOS the kernel drops the connection whose `accept` failed, so its client reads end of stream ("closed the connection without a response"): waiting out protects the process and the next client, and C-16.7 is what keeps a client from meeting `EMFILE` at all. At start, before anything else, the daemon raises its soft `RLIMIT_NOFILE` to 65536 (`subfleet/descriptors.py`), never above the hard limit and never lowering it, trying 32768, 16384 ... 1024 in turn if `setrlimit` refuses, and logs the effective soft and hard limits and what the raise achieved. `daemon install` writes `SoftResourceLimits.NumberOfFiles` (65536, or `kern.maxfilesperproc` if lower) into the launchd plist, so even a daemon that cannot raise its own limit does not start at launchd's 256. `daemon.status` reports `descriptors`: the soft and hard limits (`null` is unlimited), the descriptors open now, and the C-16.7 connection counts. (Incident: `~/.subfleet/daemon.log` holds 14 tracebacks of `OSError: [Errno 24] Too many open files` from `self._socket.accept()` in `serve_forever`, 2 on 2026-09-24 and 12 on 2026-09-25, some a few log lines apart as launchd restarted a daemon that failed again at once. Each ended the process, and every client waiting on a reply lost it. launchd had started the daemon at a soft limit of 256, and every client connection held a descriptor.)
- **C-16.7** Client connections are bounded and never wait for a reader. The daemon holds at most `max(4, min(512, (soft limit - 64 - 2 × turns) // 2))` client connections at once, derived at each `accept`: 64 descriptors stay reserved for its own work, including the store, the log, the lock, the listener and its children's pipes; each conversation turn runner still going holds two more of its own, its relay connection to the guardian, open for the turn's whole life (C-26.4), and its stdout while it reads a chunk; and each connection is allowed two. A cap that falls as turns start closes no connection already held. Each connection it holds has a reader thread of its own, started at `accept`, so no connection waits for a reader; requests can still wait for a thread of the request pools, and when every held connection carries one request at a time those waits are bounded by the cap; one client that pipelines can make every other client's requests wait too. A request whose pool cannot start a thread is counted (`unscheduled`, as on main) and its reader goes on: the standard pool has queued the call before it tried, so it may still run and answer. `close()` waits at most 2 s in all for readers to return, then closes every connection it still holds. One accepted past the cap, or one for which no reader thread can start, gets `{"ok": false, "error": {"code": 69, "message": "the daemon is busy: ...", "fix": "try again shortly; ..."}}` at once, before its request is read, and is closed; nothing else the daemon sends over the socket carries 69. The client reads that answer even if its request met the closed socket, and because nothing was read, `Client.call` sends the same request again after 50 ms doubling to 1 s (less up to half at random), but only while less than half its deadline has passed, so a retry the daemon does read still has at least half the deadline to answer and a clean busy never becomes an unknown outcome (C-16.3); after that it reports busy. A retry whose connect is then refused (ECONNREFUSED: the busy daemon's full listen backlog) while `daemon.lock` names a living daemon reports busy as well, so no caller falls back to offline mode behind a busy one; a socket gone (a stopped daemon unlinks it), a lock holder that is dead, or any other failure is an absent daemon (`client.refused_while_busy`); a lock that cannot say whether its holder lives (no lock, no usable pid, `ps` blocked by a sandbox) counts as busy inside a call and in the hook, both bounded by their deadlines, and for at most 60 s (`REFUSED_UNVERIFIED_MAX_S`) in a CLI wait loop with no `--timeout`, which has none. A long poll that loops (`subfleet wait`, `kill --wait`, `run --wait`, the PostToolUse hook) does not retry inside the call: it takes busy as an empty poll and asks again, so each poll has its whole deadline, and it takes a connect refused after a busy answer as busy too, by the same rule, so a daemon that stops or dies after a busy answer ends the loop instead of holding it for ever. The first try has the whole deadline, and a lost answer's message names the caller's deadline. A caller with a faster answer than waiting takes busy at once (`Client(retry_busy=False)`): the prompt hooks read the store offline instead. So a re-sent `submit` or `kill` answered only busy settles as C-16.3 says; `subfleet gate`'s poll is one call with a 180 s deadline; and `subfleet wait`, `kill --wait` and `run --wait` go on asking a busy daemon, backing off, until `--timeout`. `subfleet daemon status` reports a daemon that answered, busy or not, as running (exit 0). Reads time out: a connection with no request outstanding that has sent nothing and received no reply for 60 s is closed at its next read timeout (so after 60 to 120 s of silence), and the same 60 s bounds sending one reply, so a client that stops reading frees its thread; after a reply that failed part way nothing more is sent on that connection: the daemon shuts it down while it still holds the connection's write lock, so a reply waiting for the lock meets the closed stream instead of joining the broken line (review of 3c8fe55). A request line (newline included) over 1 MiB is answered once with exit 2 and the rest of that line is discarded, and one that nests too deeply to parse or to describe, carries an integer past Python's digit limit, or is not UTF-8 is answered exit 2 too; framing does not depend on how the bytes arrive. A read-only request (`list`, `show`, `wait`, `readings`, `why`, `pick`, `daemon.status`, `notice.pending`, `ping` without text, and `lanes` listing, with no action or `list`; and the conversation ops that only read: `capabilities`, `models.list`, `conversation.list`, `conversation.open` naming a conversation (opened by native session it may create one), `conversation.history`, `conversation.events`, `conversation.watch`, `conversation.runs`, `message.status`, `approval.list`, `approval.get`, `turn.diff`, `conversation.diff` and `workspace.check` (create's folder checks as a preview, making no folder); they may record observations such as the desktop identity or a diff's tree objects, nothing a caller relies on) whose client has closed its whole socket is not run: when the reader sees the client leave, such requests not yet started are cancelled, so the connection and its place under the cap go at once, and one a thread reaches later is dropped. A stream the daemon ended itself after a reply that failed part way is treated the same way, without being counted as a client leaving. A long poll whose client leaves ends at its next look, its answer unsent and counted as dropped: a `wait` within 0.25 s, a `conversation.events` or `conversation.watch` within 0.5 s (their store's wait looks that often); the conversation polls also end when the daemon stops. The app sends one request per connection and a long poll waits at most 50 s, so its pending poll keeps the connection from the idle close, which the reply then ends. The conversation ops' replies are written as the others are: after one that failed part way nothing more is sent. On macOS `getpeername` tells a closed client apart from one that shut down only its write half, which still gets its reply. A request that writes still runs after its client leaves, because a client disconnect cannot cancel its durable job (C-5.2). The listen backlog is `socket.SOMAXCONN` (128, macOS's default `kern.ipc.somaxconn`), since a connect refused on a full backlog reads to the CLI as no daemon at all. `daemon.status` `descriptors` also reports `live_turns`, the `reserve` they make and `unscheduled`. Refusals, idle closes and dropped requests are counted in `daemon.status` and logged on the 1st, 2nd, 4th, 8th occurrence; accept failures are counted there too and logged per consecutive run as C-16.6 says. (Before this clause every connection held one of 32 reader threads for its whole life, so 40 clients in `wait` left no reader for anyone else; `tests/unit/test_daemon_connections.py` holds that load and requires a ping answered within 2 s.)

## 17. CLI

- **C-17.1** Verbs (v1 spellings permanent, plan amendment 1): `subfleet` and `subfleet status` (table), `run`, `runs [--mine] [--running] [--last N] [--kind K | --include-turns] [--request-id ID] [--json]` (turn jobs only when asked, C-26.12; `--request-id` names the job carrying that request id, whoever submitted it and whatever its kind, C-16.3), `runs show <id> [--out|--err|--json]`, `runs reap` (reconcile now), `wait <id>... | --mine | --last [--timeout S]`, `kill <id> [--wait] [--confirm-dead|--force-release]`, `resume <id> [PROMPT]`, `lanes [list|probe|enroll <credential>|hold <lane> --until|release <lane>|transfer <lane> --to v1|v2]`, `why <id> | --task T --tier X`, `daemon [start|stop|status|logs|install]`, `doctor [--live]`, `ping [--session ID] TEXT`, `notices [--session ID] [--all] [--ack|--withdraw]` (C-15.8), `retention archives | restore <job> [--to DIR] [--repository R] [--check] | survey [--sample N]` (offline; C-8.4, d635). Aliases: `jobs` for `runs`, `show` for `runs show`, `capacity` for `status`, `notify` for `ping`, `resume-codex` for `resume`. Also first-class and permanent, from v1: `gate [pr|plan|continue]` with its 0 to 5 exit codes, `sessions [list|continue|revive|mirror]`, and `handoff <session> --to <model>`; they dispatch to the `subfleet-gate` and `subfleet-sessions` entry points. `doctor --guard-check <payload>` runs the never-rules check as a dry run that writes no denial log (C-23.24). The bare `runs show <id>` keeps v1's output shape, which agents parse today: the metadata object, then a `--- out.md ---` line and the deliverable; `--out`, `--err`, and `--json` are v2's single-artifact forms and print one thing each.
- **C-17.2** `run` flags: `--task`, `--tier`, `-m`, `-a EMAIL` (a Claude account), `-H CODEX_HOME` (a Codex home; either may name a lane id instead, and the flag's provider narrows a name, C-11.2), `-C DIR`, `-p PROMPTFILE`, `-o OUT`, `-n NAME`, `-s SANDBOX`, `-x EMAIL` (repeatable), `--mcp NAME` (repeatable; C-12.9), `--allow-desktop`, `--allow-tmp`, `--in-place`, `--independent`, `--parent JOB`, `--request-id ID`, `--wait`/`--attach`, `-d`/`--detach` (default inside a Claude session), `--batch FILE` (C-17.7), `--json`, `--dry-run`, `--why`, `--no-preamble`, `-I` (isolated review; requires `-s read-only` and `-D`; C-23.2), `-D REVIEW_ROOT`. Deprecated but accepted through milestone 8 with a stderr note: `-t CLASS`, `--overflow`, `-m sol` (remapped to `astra`).
- **C-17.3** Exit codes, one meaning each across every verb: 0 ok · 1 operational error · 2 invalid input · 3 no lane: no attempt could be admitted (message names the earliest reset; for a job whose pinned lane could never admit it, the lane and why, C-11.8) · 4 hard limit: the job's final attempt was `limited` and no candidate or attempt remained, whether or not a lane was pinned (message names the closure's clock) · 5 auth dead · 6 provider CLI too old · 7 refused (message names the rule and the fix) · 69 daemon unavailable · 75 queued (only with `--json` and `--no-wait-queue`) · 124 wait timeout · 125 job lost · 130 cancelled. `wait` and `--wait` return the job's rc when it is one of these; a provider rc outside the table maps to 1 with the raw rc in the message. Exit 1 also covers an outcome the CLI could not learn (C-16.3): the message says the outcome is unknown and names the request id, and such an outcome is never reported as refused, failed, or not submitted.
