- **C-17.4** Stdout carries the contract (job id on `run`, the table on `runs`, the deliverable on `runs show --out`); human progress goes to stderr; `--json` emits one JSON object per line and no prose.
- **C-17.5** Offline mode: when the daemon is unavailable, `runs`, `runs show`, `status`, and `kill` work by reading the store read-only and the receipts, and `kill` signals the recorded pgid only after C-5.3 identity checks; everything else exits 69 and prints `subfleet daemon start`.
- **C-17.6** `run` inside a Claude Code session (detected as v1 does, by the session registry and env) defaults to detached and prints the four-line hint v1 prints (out path, log path, wait command, status command).
- **C-17.7** `run --batch FILE` submits every job in a manifest in one call and labels them as one batch. The manifest is TOML when the file ends `.toml` and JSON otherwise (both in the standard library; the package takes no dependency): an optional `label` (default: the file's stem), an optional `defaults` table, and `jobs`, a non-empty list of at most 256 tables; a bare JSON list is `jobs`. A table says nothing `run` cannot. Its keys are `task`, `tier`, `model` (`-m`), `account` (`-a`), `codex_home` (`-H`), `workdir` (`-C`), `prompt` (`-p`), `prompt_text`, `out` (`-o`), `name`, `sandbox`, `exclude`, `allow_desktop`, `allow_tmp`, `in_place`, `independent`, `parent`, `no_preamble`, `mcp` (a list of server names; C-12.9), and `allow_unmeasured_reserve`; an unknown key is exit 2. Precedence is the entry, then `defaults`, then the other flags on the same command line. Relative `workdir`, `prompt`, `out`, and `codex_home` paths resolve against the manifest's directory, so a folder of briefs moves whole. A job with no `name` is named after its prompt file. Every entry passes `run`'s own client-side checks before the first submit; if one fails, nothing is submitted and the message names the entry (exit 2, or 7 for a client-side refusal). After that the entries are independent: a submission the daemon refuses is reported (`NOT submitted`, which names only an entry the daemon refused with an error response) and the rest still go, because a caller handing off K threads wants the K-1 that can run to run; the exit code is the first failure's, else 0. An entry whose answer is still lost after its re-send (C-16.3) is reported with its outcome unknown and its request id, and it ends the batch, because a daemon that answered neither is not answering: every later entry is reported as not sent and is not sent, the exit code is 1 unless an earlier failure's code stands, and stderr prints `subfleet run --batch <file> --request-id <batch id>`, which settles every entry and creates none twice. A daemon that refuses the connection ends the batch the same way, with exit 69, the count already submitted, and the same command. A batch that stopped waits for nothing. Stdout carries the job ids, one per line in manifest order, and with `--json` one object per entry (`batch`, `label`, `index`, `name`, `workdir`, `request_id`, `job_id`, `rc`, `outcome` (`created`, `existing`, `refused`, `unknown`, or `not-sent`), and `error` and `fix` for an entry that was not submitted, where the `fix` of an unknown or unsent entry is the command that settles it) (C-17.4); stderr carries the table and the `wait` command for the whole batch. `--wait` and a synchronous `run` wait for every submitted job. `--dry-run`, `--why`, `-p`, and a positional prompt describe one job and are exit 2 with `--batch`. The batch id is `ID` with `--request-id ID` and a fresh UUID4 without it, and entry n is always `<batch id>-n`, which must fit C-1.5's 128 characters (exit 2 before anything is sent), so submitting the same manifest again with `--request-id <batch id>` after a partial failure or a lost answer adds nothing twice (C-6.2) (incident: 2026-09-24, entries of a batch without `--request-id` each had an unrelated fresh request id, so no re-run could name them, C-16.3); an in-place entry whose checkout has since moved is reported as a different payload rather than resubmitted, because the digest covers the git head. The daemon records `{id, label, index, size}` in the `job.submitted` event and in `manifest.json`, returns it as `batch` on `show` and on each labelled row of `list`, and refuses a malformed one with exit 2. It is a label for people and the menu bar app: it is not a column, not part of the payload digest, and never an input to routing; a daemon that predates it ignores the field (C-16.2).

## 18. Timers (milestone 5, interface only here)

- **C-18.1** Probe cycles wait `probe_interval_s` after the preceding cycle completes (default 60 seconds). Every cycle, including an immediately requested repeat, attempts a usage read for every enabled, canonical v2, non-desktop Claude and Codex lane whether idle or busy. Existing operator-hold, auth-dead and revoked-credential latches, the server's `Retry-After`, Claude sensor failures still within the existing probe interval (durable across requested repeats and restarts), and a preceding read still behind its per-lane timeout fence remain exceptions (C-9.9, C-23.44, C-23.47). A fresh attempt reading never skips the usage read. Idle homes finish their read/heal phase, publish and release `slot:0` before busy reads start or consume Claude pacing, so busy reads never extend an idle lane's unavailability. An idle lane's probe holds `slot:0` from reservation through that idle publication; a busy lane uses the provider's no-turn usage sensor beside its attempts, with no slot lease or heal turn (C-18.3). A failed read preserves previous provider readings and their observation times; their age continues to increase. A cycle releases every timer hold it took, whatever raised (review of PR #72: a lane whose probe raised left every other lane's `slot:0` held until a restart). The default leaves refresh headroom within the 120-second reading TTL; configured intervals remain authoritative, and delayed lanes or failed reads still display stale readings honestly. Keepalive is `admission-observed` evidence every 5 h 05 m; reset-credit policy is an action (C-19) with v1's rule set; alerts fire on transition and at most every 6 h while persisting, as a notice for `alerts.operator_session` when one is set (C-15.8), and every alert in force is shown by `status`, `status.json` and the menu whether or not one is (C-18.4); retention runs every hour; `status.json` is published for the menu bar app every probe cycle. `status.json` judges a lane whose slot a probe holds (C-11.4's admission probe, a timer's turn, a re-enrolment's turn; quarantined or not, C-5.7a) as admission does: not dispatchable for as long as the lease is held, so it is not counted in `dispatchable_now` nor named `best_home`, and every probe lease counts toward `max_active_attempts`, when the policy sets one (C-6.4), as an active attempt does. The leases and their holders' records are read in the committed state the snapshot's rows come from (C-3.7), as admission's view reads them. Each lane row carries `probe_state`, the state of its holder's newest `probe.state` record (null when no probe holds the slot), and `probe_holder`. A lease no record names reads `uncertain`: a timer's usage read writes no record, and a timer's turn writes its first only after it took the lease. A turn's lane reads `completed` from its last record until the timer releases the lease. `codex.fleet` and `claude.lanes` carry `probe_held`, how many of their lanes a probe holds. These are additions: no field the menu bar app decodes changes name, type or meaning otherwise. The leases are laid over the cycle's snapshot after its reset-credit policy (C-19) and its alerts have judged it, so the slot an admission probe or a keepalive holds for its seconds neither triggers a credit nor raises an alert (2026-09-27: the timer's snapshot read no leases, so a lane held by a quarantined probe read as dispatchable, counted in `dispatchable_now` and could be `best_home` while admission refused it).
- **C-18.2** `status.json` also carries `jobs`, so the menu bar app shows work as well as quota: `live` (every `running`, then `waiting`, then `queued` job, oldest first within a state), `recent` (the 8 most recently finished jobs), and `counts` by live state, all three of detached work only: a turn job (kind `turn`, C-26.1) is its conversation's (C-26.12) and is shown under `conversations` (C-29.6). Each row has `job_id`, `kind`, `name`, `state`, `wait_reason` and `next_check_at` (only while `waiting`, so a finished job never shows a stale reason), `sandbox`, `workdir` (the allocated worktree once there is one), `model` and `lane_id` from the job's latest attempt (the pinned model before any attempt), `attempts`, the three timestamps, `rc`, and `batch` (C-17.7) or null. Batch labels are read from `job.submitted` events for the displayed jobs only. The section is always present, empty when nothing is running. The app keeps a batch's jobs together under one heading (`<label> · <size> jobs`) where the first of them appears, shows a waiting job's reason (`waiting: workspace`; any reason other than `capacity` is a warning), shows a failed job's exit code, and decodes a snapshot that has no `jobs` key, so an app newer than its daemon still works. The section is as fresh as the snapshot, one probe cycle (C-18.1); `subfleet runs` remains the live ledger.
- **C-18.3** A busy Claude or Codex lane is read beside its work with that provider's existing no-turn usage sensor (Claude `api/oauth/usage`, source `oauth-usage`; Codex's usage GET, source `wham`). Claude reads share the existing pacing with idle reads. A lane is busy when it has an attempt `reserved`, `starting`, `running` or `finalizing`, or any `lane:<lane id>:` lease; one transaction decides whether the lane is never read (disabled, not v2's, the desktop's, or under an open `operator-hold` or `auth-dead` closure, checked first), idle, or busy. A busy lane's read is the usage GET alone: it takes no lease and writes no reservation, so admission never sees it as a slot block or a reserved probe (C-6.10) and the next attempt on the lane takes the slot it would have taken without it; it runs no heal turn, because the lane's attempts renew its token (C-23.47); and it passes the per-lane read fence an idle read passes (one read per lane at a time, four at once, the 15 s deadline, a late answer dropped). Its result is judged after the busy read phase completes, with idle publication and lease release already finished, and it is judged and published in one store transaction: no slot keeps the lane's attempts away, so the store's write lock does, and a publication that raises rolls back whole, the settlement with it, leaving the store as it stood when the publication began, and the lane's previous verdict is put back under that lock; when it is the commit that fails, it is put back only if the publication's own verdict is still in place, checked and replaced as one step, so it never replaces one an attempt has recorded since. An `ok` or `limited` answer for the lane's own account (or a credential that names no account) publishes what an idle read of the same answer publishes: the readings, a `provider-limit` closure or its release, the reset-credit settlement that ends a confirmed override (C-23.17), and the verdict. A read whose credential names another account (the home's `auth.json`, whatever the request returned) publishes the identity mismatch as an idle read does (no provider readings, no settlement, no closure change, the lane disabled in the same transaction); no lease guards it, and a job admitted between the read and that commit runs as it would have had nothing been read; a half-written `auth.json` reads as no credential and is withheld. No other busy reading or admission verdict is published: not `auth-dead`, `revoked`, `expired-token` or `no-auth` (the lane's attempts are renewing its token as it is read, and a published `auth-dead` disables the lane until it is re-enrolled; an attempt that ends `auth-dead` disables the lane and records that verdict itself, C-23.44, and a credential failure the attempts do not classify as dead waits for the lane's next idle read, as before), and not a failed read. Failed Claude sensor reads record a separate `timer.usage-backoff` cooldown for at least the existing probe interval, extended by Retry-After, without refreshing provider timestamps, changing admission metadata, releasing closures or settling credits; requested cycles and restarts respect it. A Claude HTTP 429 carrying Retry-After is the sole exception: it publishes its cooldown verdict while preserving prior provider readings and observation times, releasing no closure and settling no reset credit. Whatever the read itself raises (its mark's query, its adapter, its request, an answer that is not a mapping) is a failed read, never a failed cycle. Nothing at all is published for a lane the timer would no longer read: one disabled, transferred, made the desktop's, held or closed `auth-dead` since its read. Every closure report leaves a `closure.recorded` event for its lane, including one that ends no later than the lane's open closure and so changes nothing in that row (`Store.put_closure` rewrites the row as it is); a busy read notes the store's newest event as it starts, and when such an event for the lane is newer than that mark at publication (a closure recorded, extended, or reported again, by an attempt, a conversation turn, an admission probe or an operator), the read publishes its readings and verdict but releases no closure and settles no reset credit, so an older answer never undoes a newer limit, whatever retention has pruned since; the next cycle reads again. Every read in a cycle, busy or idle, published or not, counts toward whether the cycle was offline, and the cycle's `timer.cycle` event names the busy lanes it read, the status of each busy read it did not publish, and, under `fenced`, each lane whose read was published while a limit was reported on it during the read. Both providers are read on every probe cycle while busy (C-18.1) (incident: 2026-09-30, codex-4 went from an operator hold straight to work with a fresh week from a reset credit; no cycle read it for 78 minutes, the credit's override stood, and the lane stayed unmeasured at one attempt while every other Codex lane was at its floor; `docs/decisions/2026-09-30-busy-lane-usage-read.md`, MaxGhenis/subfleet-v2#73).
- **C-18.4** (the alerts in force are shown) An alert is in force while its latch is active (C-23.52): from the cycle that sees its condition come back, which is the cycle it fires, except a daily alert seen again within a day of its last firing, which comes into force without firing (C-18.1), to the cycle that sees it gone. An offline cycle neither raises nor clears one (C-23.27). An alert whose notice could not be written does not latch, is not in force, and is tried again next cycle (C-18.1). With no operator session there is no recovery notice either: a recovery is the alert leaving the list. `status.json` always carries `alerts`: every alert in force, most severe first (`critical`, `warn`, `info`; an unknown severity sorts as `warn`), then the longest in force first, each with `key`, `severity`, `subject`, `body`, `since` (when it last came into force; null for one in force since before latches recorded it) and `last_sent`. The words are the latest cycle's when that cycle saw the condition, else the ones it last fired with, which its latch keeps; a latch that has none, from before alerts kept their words, shows its key. `subfleet status` prints the same list first, the body under each subject (a credential alert's body names the command to run, C-23.52; a limit's says when it resets), from `daemon.status` or, with no daemon, from the latches in the store. The menu shows them above the jobs, the body selectable so the command can be copied, and any alert above `info` marks the menu bar icon; a snapshot with no `alerts` key, from an older daemon, still decodes. Every publication of `status.json` carries the alerts in force, the probe cycle's (C-18.1) and any other, such as an operator's reset-credit pass, so an alert reaches the menu within one cycle and leaves it within one cycle of clearing, and no publication between cycles empties the list.

## 19. Actions

- **C-19.1** `actions` rows have kind (`merge`, `reset-credit`, later `send`), `op_key` (unique; for a merge the head sha plus PR, for a reset the account key plus the credit id), subject, state `pending` → `executing` → `confirmed` | `failed` | `unknown`, request and result JSON. The row is `pending` before the remote call; `unknown` after a timeout until a read of the remote state settles it. `status`, `why`, and `--dry-run` never create or advance an action.

## 20. Tests and release gates

- **C-20.1** Layout: `tests/unit/` (store, digest, state machine, policy evaluation, classifiers on fixtures, salvage on a temp repo), `tests/fake/` (daemon against fake providers in a temp `SUBFLEET_HOME`), `tests/process/` (guardian, containment, kill, `setsid` escape), `tests/live/` (opt-in with `SUBFLEET_LIVE=1`; one Haiku probe, one wham probe, one real launch per adapter). `tests/frontend/` compiles the Swift app's model and view layers with `xcrun swiftc` against fixture JSON the Python code produces, and is skipped off macOS.
- **C-20.2** Time budgets: unit under 30 s total, fake under 60 s, process and git under 20 s, all on this Mac. `uv run pytest -q` runs everything but live.
- **C-20.3** Crash matrix: for each attempt boundary in C-4.2 and for the notice, export, and salvage steps, a test SIGKILLs the daemon at that point with a fake job running and asserts the recovery column. Disk-full is simulated by a write hook that raises `ENOSPC` at each publication step.
- **C-20.4** Release gates before the `run` cutover are plan amendment 10, each as a named test or a recorded measurement in `docs/release-gates.md`.
- **C-20.5** Every test names the clause it proves in its docstring, for example `"""C-5.6 kill quarantines when a setsid grandchild survives."""`.

## 21. Milestone acceptance

| Milestone | Accepted when these tests pass |
|---|---|
| 1 | `fake`: a Codex job survives the submitting process exiting; `kill` finalizes with a salvage ref after verified containment; daemon SIGKILL during `running` re-adopts; SIGKILL during `starting` with no receipt recovers per C-4.2; `nested-setsid` scenario quarantines; guard trust preflight blocks a mismatched hash; isolation matrix; `runs`, `runs show`, `wait`, `kill` offline and online; exit codes table |
| 2 | Claude adapter parses all experiment-0 payloads to `provider` readings; a rejected event closes the model scope with the reported clock; attestation `mismatch` on the model-downgrade fixture; no percentage rendered without a `provider` reading; a hard limit closes the lane and the next attempt carries exclusions |
| 3 | C-11.6 routes to Astra with the recorded reason; `why` prints the decision; pins never fall back; both comparators apply C-11.3's weekly reserves and binding reset; unmeasured lanes rank after measured within preceding keys and use configured slots; golden decision cases from `docs/reports/D-surface.md` and the 06:31 to 06:33 log |
| 9 | `fake`: a Claude and a Codex conversation through fake providers speaking the recorded protocols: submit, acknowledgement, events, approval allow and deny, interrupt, follow-up; a lost acknowledgement retried by UUID creates one message; daemon SIGKILL mid-turn replays with no duplicate event or frame; an exit without a terminal event reconciles and never resends. `process`: guardian relay across a daemon SIGKILL. `frontend`: the app decodes every result shape in design §5 from Python-produced fixtures. `live` (recorded in the release record): one real Claude and one real Codex conversation with a follow-up, an approval, and attested served models |

## 22. Open experiments

Numbered as in plan B rev 4: 1 (optional; Claude lane homes), 2 (usage-endpoint rate limits under 14-lane probing), 3 (long-poll `wait` under the harness), 4 (`asyncRewake` in desktop and terminal), 5 (rewritten by amendment 2: `setsid` escape quarantines), 6 (process-group semantics under launchd), 7 (Codex hooks trust hash drift). Each has a written result before the milestone that depends on it.

## 23. Carried-forward invariants

Version 2, 2026-09-05. These clauses close the 92 rows of the v1 invariant ledger that version 1 left uncovered (`docs/invariants.md`, `docs/invariant-gaps.md`, where each clause's ledger rows, rationale, and incident are recorded). A clause here binds like any other; the milestone names when it is first tested. Proposal numbers `P-23.n` in the gaps document are these clauses' `C-23.n`.

- **C-23.1** (a caller's files are read-only to subfleet) No component of subfleet deletes, truncates, or rewrites a file the caller supplied. A job runs from the daemon's own copy of the prompt at `jobs/<job id>/prompt.md` (C-2.3), and the caller's `-p` file, the contents of `-C` that the provider does not itself change, and any file named in the prompt are read only to the daemon, the guardian, and every adapter. [rows 8 (`replace`, safety-guard); milestone 1; owner fake; module `subfleet/daemon.py`]
- **C-23.2** (an isolated review inherits no context and holds no hosted capability) `run -I` (isolated review) requires `-s read-only` and a review root `-D`; the review root and any further sources are exposed only through the provider's read-only source flag (`--add-dir` for Claude), and the Claude launch's tool allowlist is `Read`, `Glob`, and `Grep`. A `read-only` Claude launch removes `CLAUDE_CODE_ADDITIONAL_DIRECTORIES_CLAUDE_MD`, `CLAUDE_MEMORY_STORES`, `CLAUDE_CODE_REMOTE_MEMORY_DIR`, and the `CLAUDE_COWORK_MEMORY_*` family from the child environment before the provider initialises. An isolated Codex review runs `--ephemeral --ignore-user-config --ignore-rules` and disables every hosted capability v1's `prepare_isolated_review` disables — apps, plugins and remote plugins, hooks, multi-agent, tool suggestion, computer and browser use, image generation, goals, memories, shell snapshots and profile sourcing, project docs, web search, and each MCP server in the home's inventory by name — with `history.persistence` set to `none`. [rows 29 (`keep`, safety-guard), 31 (`keep`, safety-guard), 45 (`keep`, safety-guard); milestone 1 for the CLI and Codex halves, 2 for the Claude environment; owner unit; module `subfleet/cli.py`, `subfleet/adapters/claude.py`, `subfleet/adapters/codex.py`]
- **C-23.3** (an isolated review is refused when operator policy could override it) An isolated review is refused with exit 7, naming the variable or layer, when the submission inherits `CLAUDE_CODE_MANAGED_SETTINGS_PATH`, `CLAUDE_CODE_REMOTE_SETTINGS_PATH`, or `CLAUDE_CODE_MOCK_REMOTE_SETTINGS`, or when the Codex home reports managed configuration requirements or a nonempty `system` or `project` configuration layer. Operator policy is never silently discarded to make an isolated review possible. [rows 30 (`keep`, safety-guard), 46 (`keep`, safety-guard); milestone 1; owner unit; module `subfleet/cli.py`, `subfleet/guard/preflight.py`]
- **C-23.4** (isolation is prepared per attempt) An adapter prepares isolation from the attempt's own lane on every launch: the configuration layers, the managed-requirements check, and the MCP inventory are re-read for that lane, and no isolation state computed for a previous lane or attempt is reused. [rows 47 (`keep`, safety-guard); milestone 1; owner unit; module `subfleet/adapters/codex.py`]
- **C-23.5** (how long a guard preflight verdict is good for) A guard preflight verdict is reused only while the installed Codex version, the lane home, the override string, and the fingerprint of the seeded configuration are all unchanged; any difference re-verifies, and a lane home the daemon has not verified under that key is verified before its first launch on that lane. A cached verdict older than 30 days is discarded. A re-picked lane whose preflight fails refuses that launch rather than launching unguarded. [rows 41 (`keep`, safety-guard), 52 (`keep`, safety-guard); milestone 1; owner fake; module `subfleet/guard/preflight.py`]
- **C-23.6** (the `write_stdin` hole is closable) A Codex launch reads `SUBFLEET_CODEX_UNIFIED_EXEC`; when it is `off` the launch carries `-c features.unified_exec=false`, leaving `shell_command` as the only shell tool. `doctor` reports which setting is in force. [rows 65 (`keep`, safety-guard); milestone 1; owner unit; module `subfleet/adapters/codex.py`]
- **C-23.7** (reset credits are gifts, not purchases) The reset-credit client lists and consumes only gifted entitlements. Its request URLs are an allowlist that contains no purchase, checkout, or add-credit endpoint, and no verb, action kind, or alert offers one. [rows 170 (`keep`, safety-guard); milestone 5; owner unit; module `subfleet/actions.py`]
- **C-23.8** (a gate approves an exact revision) An approval binds to a fingerprint the caller attests — `--expect-sha256` for a plan, `--expect-head` with `--expect-base` for a pull request — and the gate never infers approval from a fresh read of the artifact. The fingerprint is re-captured after each peer round returns, and a round whose artifact changed while the peer was reviewing blocks instead of counting. [rows 193 (`keep`, safety-guard), 196 (`keep`, safety-guard); milestone 7; owner unit; module `subfleet/gate.py`]
- **C-23.9** (what counts as a peer verdict) A peer verdict is exactly one sentinel-delimited JSON object naming the revision that was reviewed, with no text outside the sentinels; anything else is not a verdict and the round does not count. An `approve` verdict carries no findings and no notes, and a `changes-requested` verdict carries at least one finding. [rows 194 (`keep`, safety-guard), 195 (`keep`, safety-guard); milestone 7; owner unit; module `subfleet/gate.py`]
- **C-23.10** (where a peer round runs and what reserves it) Each peer round is reserved under a lease held for the round's lifetime; output from a round whose lease is no longer held is discarded and never counted as an approval. The peer runs `read-only` from a neutral directory the gate allocates under `$SUBFLEET_HOME`, never inside the repository under review. [rows 198 (`keep`, safety-guard), 199 (`keep`, safety-guard); milestone 7; owner fake; module `subfleet/gate.py`]
- **C-23.11** (what a merge requires before it is attempted) A merge action runs its preflight immediately before the remote call and proceeds only when the pull request is open and not a draft, its head and base are the approved ones, its mergeability is clean, and its checks have reached a terminal green state. The merge command pins `--match-head-commit` to the approved head. [rows 201 (`keep`, safety-guard), 202 (`keep`, safety-guard); milestone 7; owner unit; module `subfleet/gate.py`]
- **C-23.12** (how a landing is verified) The gate verifies a landing against the merge commit's own parents — the approved base, and for method `merge` also the approved head — never against the base branch's current tip. Merge methods are `merge` and `squash` only; a rebase landing is refused. A landing whose parents do not match is recorded as a mismatch on the action, and the gate neither retries the merge nor reverts it. [rows 203 (`keep`, safety-guard), 204 (`keep`, safety-guard), 207 (`keep`, safety-guard); milestone 7; owner unit; module `subfleet/gate.py`]
- **C-23.13** (only the holder publishes an action's result) An action moves to `executing` by recording the identity of the worker that holds it, and only that holder may write its result; a result offered by any other worker is discarded and recorded as an event. A `confirmed`, `failed`, or `unknown` action is never overwritten by a later result for the same `op_key`. [rows 206 (`keep`, safety-guard); milestone 5 for `reset-credit`, 7 for `merge`; owner unit; module `subfleet/actions.py`]
- **C-23.14** (what a handoff may carry) A handoff excerpt is scrubbed before it is written: private keys, JWTs, prefixed API tokens, and `Bearer` values are replaced, encoded binary is omitted, and the result of any tool call whose input matches a credential-reading pattern (`agent-secret get`, a keychain read, `env` or `printenv`, `auth.json`, `.env`, a credentials file) is omitted by pattern rather than redacted. Ordinary code, commands, and tool output are retained verbatim. Secret assignments and flags are scanned by identifier in linear time, with quoted spans and removed values scanned without repeated suffix searches. No rule but those for blocks that span lines runs over more than 262,144 characters at once. In a longer block, reminders, private keys and data URIs are first resolved over all of it, exactly as in a scrub of the whole block, by scans whose work is linear in its length; if it is still longer, every other rule runs only on a head excerpt (its whole lines within the first 131,072 characters) and a tail excerpt (its whole lines within the last 131,072), joined by a marker giving the characters left out, and the result is bounded like any other text. Those rules match within a line, except across a line break after a `:` or `=` that follows a key (an identifier with a sensitive ending, headers included), after a key or its closing quote when a `:` or `=` is the next visible character, or after `Bearer` when a token character is; an excerpt is cut only at a line break where none of these applies, so no match spans a cut, each excerpt scrubs exactly as the whole block does there, and the excerpts never show a value that a scrub of the whole block removes. An excerpt with no such line break within reach (a first or last line longer than an excerpt, say) keeps nothing. The assembled handoff brief is scrubbed whole, once more; C-23.36 keeps its sections within the budget. Credential-read suppression matches a tool input of any size in linear time (a wrapper's flags, as in `sudo -E env`, are walked once), so a large input is classified by what it holds, not by its size. [rows 208 (`keep`, safety-guard), 209 (`keep`, safety-guard); milestone 6; owner unit; module `subfleet/sessions/handoff.py`]
- **C-23.15** (transcript usage is summed once per message) Token usage read from a Claude transcript is summed once per `message.id`: a repeated assistant message replaces the record already held for that id and never adds to it. The count is a `readings` observation, not a job column. [rows 128 (`keep`, capacity-truth); milestone 2; owner unit; module `subfleet/adapters/claude.py`]
- **C-23.16** (when a reset credit may be spent) A reset-credit redemption is attempted only when the usage endpoint reports the account `limit_reached` and the entitlements list holds a concrete `available` credit of type `codex_rate_limits`; neither condition alone admits it. Each consume carries a fresh UUID4 `redeem_request_id` in the action's request JSON. Only a response with code `reset` and `windows_reset` greater than zero moves the action to `confirmed`. [rows 161 (`keep`, capacity-truth), 164 (`keep`, capacity-truth), 165 (`keep`, capacity-truth); milestone 5; owner unit; module `subfleet/actions.py`]
- **C-23.17** (a confirmed consume is the authority) A `confirmed` consume is the later `provider` reading C-9.6 requires: it releases the lane's weekly closure, sets the weekly reset clock to now plus seven days with `clock_source: guessed`, and leaves the lane dispatchable even while the usage endpoint still reports the old window. It also clears the lane's `local-backoff` dispatch closure. [rows 167 (`keep`, capacity-truth), 169 (`keep`, capacity-truth); milestone 5; owner unit; module `subfleet/actions.py`]
- **C-23.18** (an unreadable count is not a small count) A fleet-wide credit total is rendered only when every lane's count was readable; if any lane's count is unknown the total is `null` and is displayed as unknown, never as a sum over the readable lanes. [rows 168 (`keep`, capacity-truth); milestone 5; owner unit; module `subfleet/actions.py`]
- **C-23.19** (when a five-hour window is open) A keepalive reading's observed-at instant is the moment the provider request is sent, not when the worker was queued or the credential was read. A lane whose window was opened by any request inside the last five hours — a keepalive, a completed attempt, or an attempt still running — is skipped and recorded `skipped-open`. An attempt that ended rc 5 without a recorded provider session did not open the window. [rows 172 (`keep`, capacity-truth), 173 (`keep`, capacity-truth), 174 (`keep`, capacity-truth); milestone 5; owner unit; module `subfleet/keepalive.py`]
- **C-23.20** (revive measures the lane it is about to use) Revive admits a lane only on a `provider` reading taken in the same pass; a stored reading, a `stale-provider` reading, or the absence of a closure never qualifies a lane on its own. [rows 189 (`keep`, capacity-truth); milestone 6; owner fake; module `subfleet/sessions/tickle.py`]
- **C-23.21** (how a provider binary is found) A provider binary is resolved to an absolute path before launch, never left to `PATH` alone. Claude resolves `SUBFLEET_CLAUDE_BIN`, then `~/.local/bin/claude`, then `PATH`; Codex resolves `SUBFLEET_CODEX_BIN`, then `PATH`, then its known install locations. The resolved path goes into the launch argv, is recorded on the attempt, and is printed by `doctor`. [rows 26 (`keep`, ops-hygiene), 158 (`keep`, ops-hygiene); milestone 2; owner unit; module `subfleet/adapters/claude.py`, `subfleet/adapters/codex.py`]
- **C-23.22** (accounting never changes an outcome) Usage accounting runs synchronously inside finalization, before the notice is written, and never changes a job's class, rc, deliverable, or state. A hook failure or a parse failure records an `events` row and finalization continues, exactly as a failed export does under C-8.3. [rows 35 (`keep`, ops-hygiene), 129 (`keep`, ops-hygiene); milestone 1 for the finalization ordering, 2 for the parser; owner fake; module `subfleet/daemon.py`]
- **C-23.23** (how the guard preflight probes) The guard preflight never writes a lane's provider home: it seeds a scratch home under `$SUBFLEET_HOME` with copies of the lane home's `config.toml` and `hooks.json`, copies no credential or session store, and probes there. The probe carries a deadline: it fails at once if the app-server exits without answering, and at the deadline it SIGTERMs the probe's process group, waits, then SIGKILLs it. [rows 50 (`keep`, ops-hygiene), 53 (`keep`, ops-hygiene); milestone 1; owner process; module `subfleet/guard/preflight.py`]
- **C-23.24** (a guard check writes nothing) The guard check verb evaluates a payload against the copied hook and prints the verdict without writing the hook's denial log, the preflight's cache, or any other guard telemetry. [rows 56 (`keep`, ops-hygiene); milestone 8 (guard parity); owner unit; module `subfleet/guard/preflight.py`]
- **C-23.25** (installing a hook into a shared settings file) Before changing `~/.claude/settings.json` the installer writes a timestamped backup beside it, and the change preserves every key the installer does not own. A second install of the same version makes no further change and writes no further backup. [rows 70 (`keep`, ops-hygiene); milestone 4; owner unit; module `subfleet/hooks.py`]
- **C-23.26** (secondary records are pruned by age, never by a caller's window) The maintenance pass deletes notices in state `surfaced` or `acknowledged` whose row is older than 14 days, and never prunes a `pending` or `offered` notice by age. A `pending` or `offered` service notice leaves the store only by an operator's withdrawal (C-15.8), which records what it removed, or by C-11.8's withdrawal of a pin notice no one has been shown. A provider scan-cache entry is dropped only when its file no longer exists or its record is more than seven days old; a narrower scan window never evicts an entry. [rows 80 (`keep`, ops-hygiene), 141 (`keep`, ops-hygiene); milestone 2 for the scan cache, 4 for notices; owner unit; module `subfleet/retention.py`, `subfleet/adapters/codex.py`]
- **C-23.27** (one monitoring cycle, one verdict) A cycle heals before it persists: `status.json`, the readings the cycle writes, its alert conditions, and its history all see post-heal verdicts. At most one refresh probe runs per provider home per cycle, and two probes of the same home are at least twenty minutes apart. A cycle in which every provider probe failed with a network error is recorded `offline` and emits no alert of any kind, including scoped-limit conditions. [rows 152 (`keep`, ops-hygiene), 153 (`keep`, ops-hygiene), 154 (`keep`, ops-hygiene); milestone 5; owner fake; module `subfleet/timers.py`, `subfleet/alerts.py`]
- **C-23.28** (mirror health is a state file, not a quiet log; health is not reach) Mirror health is judged from the mirror's per-pass state sidecar, never from log recency: a pass the sidecar records as in flight is healthy until thirty minutes after its recorded start, and only then is the mirror `stalled`. Health is not reach: the desktop app lists a session folder into its sidebar only when it loads that folder (at launch, on an account or org switch, at the first login after a logout, and when a new app window builds its session API), so a record the mirror copies into the loaded folder afterwards is missing from the running app's sidebar until its next load, and a flag the mirror writes there is overwritten by the app's next save of that record. The mirror therefore puts each new session whose transcript exists into every folder within about `mirror_hot_interval_s` (2 s, a `policy.json` cap under C-6.4) of the write while no full pass holds its worker. Both passes sync changed sessions' archive, star, title and setting fields. A full pass keeps `mirror.lock` (flock `LOCK_EX|LOCK_NB`) and, once this process has completed its first inventory, synchronously services flag-only hot passes while it inventories, copies, restores or prunes, using a separate inventory snapshot: service is due after `mirror_hot_interval_s` from full-pass start or the previous service's finish, and begins at the next checkpoint (between entry reads, every 64 directory entries while listing, and between other work units). Zero disables this service as it disables the hot timer. A warm full inventory therefore cannot hold flag sync until its completion; cold startup completes one inventory before any hot snapshot is forked. This is a scheduling bound, not a fixed wall-clock completion promise: the current indivisible operation or flag transaction finishes first, the hot inventory and publication take their own time, and a cold process must discover all copies before it can decide flags. A stalled filesystem or unscheduled thread has no wall-clock bound. Embedded service never spreads, repairs, revives or prunes, so it cannot change the identities or liveness on which the full pass is acting; its projected records are immutable and its cache containers and payload reference counts are separate, and each known copy, flag write or prune invalidates both inventories even if the directory timestamp does not move. Only the flock holder writes, and a flag transaction (decision through matching base publication) never nests another one. After embedded service, the full pass refreshes its flag snapshot while continuing to service hot checkpoints; if a service leaves a copy write standing (including a failed put-back), cannot observe the outcome of a successful copy write, or publishes a merge base whose `(isArchived, isStarred, title)` differs for any session (including a new row), it invalidates that refresh. Merely considering or holding candidates, or changing transcript metadata alone, does not invalidate it. The full pass repeats an invalidated refresh, at most twice, then holds its decision for the next full pass if changes continue. It never commits a stale snapshot over a newer merge base. Hot progress records its own start, finish, error and held causes under `hot` in the sidecar and in `sessions mirror --status`; idle starts and finishes are sampled every 60 seconds, every recorded start is finished, and completed changes, errors and hold transitions are recorded immediately; it never changes the full pass's `pass`, `updated_at` or `last_ok_at`. The mirror decides each session's flags from every copy or not at all (a folder that fails to list is read by name from its last listing only while its directory is unchanged since, and otherwise holds every session; an account that fails to list keeps the org folders its last listing named only if each was listed itself, and otherwise holds every session, in the hot pass as in the full one; a copy that exists and cannot be read holds the session its folder's last listing saw in it, including when the hot pass rereads that copy; only sessions individually held by flag sync enter the hot retry set, blind holds wait for the 60-second full pass, and an identity with no copies left is dropped from retry; what the app cannot read either (permission denied, a directory, FIFO or device, or a symlink loop, where a record should be) is in no sidebar and holds nothing; a store that fails to list fails the pass; bases of sessions a pass did not see survive it; held sessions and their causes are recorded in `flags_held` and `held_by` and shown by `sessions mirror --status`), skips publication of an unchanged merge base, writes each session's flag changes whole or not at all, only over copies still as it read them (a write that finds its copy rewritten since the check puts back the copies the batch already wrote, and a pass cancelled before it publishes writes nothing; an app rename in the instant between the mirror's last signature check and its own rename is the one save the mirror can overwrite), and reports, from a journal of its own writes and the app's log, how many of its copies into the loaded folder postdate that load and still wait for a relaunch (`sessions mirror --status`, `sessions list`, `doctor`). The daemon runs the mirror on the timers' own worker, which `Daemon.close()` waits for (`Timers.stop()`), so the mirror opens what it reads outside its state root (the store's records, transcripts, archived transcripts, the app's log, `cc-mirror.json`), and its own lock, only as regular files and never blocks in `open()`: a FIFO, device or directory there reads as unreadable at once, and one where a transcript belongs is no transcript, which revival leaves in place; a transcript that is a symlink is checked again on every pass, since its target can be replaced without its project directory changing. What it writes there (a copy into the store, a flag write, a revived transcript) it makes as a new file under a name of its own (O_EXCL, O_NOFOLLOW), writes, stamps and fsyncs through that one descriptor, and puts in place only while the name still holds that file; it never opens a name that already stands. Revival puts its copy in place create-only (a hard link; on a filesystem without hard links, a rename after a fresh look, which leaves the one race `os.replace` had), so a transcript another writer made after the check is never replaced; and a sweep removes the mirror's temporaries that a killed pass left, beside the store's records, in the projects directory and among its own state files, once they are an hour old. Inventory reads use the opened regular file's descriptor metadata instead of a preliminary path stat, read in 64 KiB chunks, and still validate the path after reading before caching; warm sweeps reuse the listing's `DirEntry.stat`. The ten-minute stat sweep still detects in-place writes, and only a completed full sweep advances its clock. The flag protocol is specified in `docs/formal/MirrorFlags.tla`; its invariants (convergence, change wins both ways, idempotence, cancellation safety, no lost update, all or nothing, base agreement, intent wins) are checked over every reachable state of its executable twin `tests/mirror_flags_model.py` (TLC has not been run on the spec), and the mirror is held to that model on random interleavings, including writes between two of a publish's writes. They hold while the app saves only what it has seen, and while no copy the app could not read either becomes readable again with an old value; an app save from memory that predates the mirror's write, or such a copy, reads as the user's change and wins (`docs/reports/2026-09-25-mirror-consistency.md`). [rows 159 (`keep`, ops-hygiene); milestone 6; owner unit; module `subfleet/sessions/mirror.py`, `subfleet/sessions/desktop.py`]
- **C-23.29** (a keepalive pass is bounded) A keepalive pass runs at most `keepalive_workers` (4) lanes concurrently with a per-lane deadline of `keepalive_timeout_s` (60), both `policy.json` caps under C-6.4. A lane that exceeds its deadline is recorded as timed out and is not retried inside the same pass. [rows 177 (`keep`, ops-hygiene); milestone 5; owner fake; module `subfleet/keepalive.py`]
- **C-23.30** (which registry row speaks for a session) When the session registry holds more than one row for a session, the row used is the one whose recorded pid is live, then the one whose socket is present, then the newest by start time. [rows 72 (`keep`, session-continuity); milestone 4; owner unit; module `subfleet/notices.py`]
- **C-23.31** (a headless lane run is not a session) A headless lane run is never a `ping` or notice target, never appears in a session listing unless lanes are explicitly included, and is never revived or continued. A request naming one is refused with the reason. A session is a headless lane run when the daemon recorded it as one it launched (`lane_sessions`), or when its transcript says so. Claude Code stamps its entries with the `entrypoint` of the process that wrote them: a transcript is a lane run when an entry names `sdk-cli`, `sdk-ts` or `sdk-py` and no entry names another, so one entry the desktop app (`claude-desktop`), a terminal (`cli`) or any other process wrote makes it a session, whatever wrote after it. Only the transcript's first 5,000 lines and 64 MB are read, as before this change: an entry past them is not seen. Prompts that name no entrypoint (older Claude Code) are read as v1 read them: a typed prompt, one with no `promptSource`, or a third `sdk` prompt makes a session, and a transcript that names no entrypoint is a lane run only when it holds one or two text prompts, all `promptSource: sdk`. The desktop app sends its prompts as `sdk` too, so a prompt that names an entrypoint is never read that way. [rows 76 (`keep`, session-continuity), 187 (`keep`, session-continuity); milestone 4 for notices, 6 for the listing and revive; owner unit; module `subfleet/notices.py`, `subfleet/sessions/tickle.py`]
- **C-23.32** (the importer never rewrites what it imported) An imported v1 record is never rewritten. A legacy Codex run whose thread id was not recorded resolves its resume identity in memory, from the saved `err.log` and the rollout name, at the moment a resume needs it. [rows 95 (`keep`, session-continuity); milestone 8 (cutover); owner unit; module `subfleet/importer.py`]
- **C-23.33** (when a session may be nudged) A nudge is sent only for a `SessionStart` whose source is `startup` or `resume`, never `compact` or `clear`, and only when the interruption is younger than `nudge_max_age_s` (8 h). A session is nudged at most once per interruption point and no more often than `nudge_cooldown_s` allows; both are `policy.json` caps under C-6.4. [rows 179 (`keep`, session-continuity), 180 (`keep`, session-continuity), 181 (`keep`, session-continuity); milestone 6; owner unit; module `subfleet/sessions/tickle.py`]
- **C-23.34** (the worker decides against the transcript it can see) The hook records the wake and decides nothing: dedupe, cooldown, and eligibility are re-decided by the worker against the transcript as it reads after the nudge delay. A session whose last real turn changed during the delay is skipped, and a sweep started by hand requires a longer quiet window than a `SessionStart` wake. The transcript reader recognises the app's synthetic resume stub and judges the turn beneath it. Inside a process Subfleet launched the hook records nothing and spawns no worker (C-26.13). [rows 182 (`keep`, session-continuity), 183 (`keep`, session-continuity), 184 (`keep`, session-continuity), 185 (`keep`, session-continuity); milestone 6, with the hook half in milestone 4's `subfleet/hooks.py`; owner unit; module `subfleet/sessions/tickle.py`]
- **C-23.35** (which sessions revive admits) Revive admits only a session whose recorded permission mode is `bypassPermissions` and whose retirement flag is unset. Retirement is a durable session flag set by the operator; a retired session is absent from every session listing and is never a revive candidate until the operator clears it. Desktop metadata inspection, for revival and cold inspection alike, skips unreadable records, symlinks, non-regular files and records over 1 MiB through a bounded no-follow, nonblocking descriptor read, including records for unrelated sessions. A skipped record may be the session's own, and so may one in a directory of the store that could not be listed: when any was skipped and no readable record names the session, its ownership is unknown (`desktop_owned` null, `unreadable_records` naming what was skipped) and revive refuses automatic revival as for a desktop-owned session, naming the first of them (`--revive` or `sessions.auto_revive_desktop_owned` still admits it). A record naming the session without a cwd still makes it desktop-owned. [rows 188 (`keep`, session-continuity), 191 (`keep`, session-continuity); milestone 6; owner unit; module `subfleet/sessions/tickle.py`]
- **C-23.36** (a handoff is bounded and points at its source) Every section of a handoff is bounded by an explicit per-section character cap. The caps of the sections a brief carries (`original_task`, `recent`, `progress`, `repository`) may total at most 245,760 characters, the scrubber's 262,144-character budget less 16,384 for the brief's header, because the assembled brief is scrubbed whole (C-23.14); policy refuses caps that total more. The handoff records the absolute path of the source transcript, which stays the durable record. [rows 210 (`keep`, session-continuity); milestone 6; owner unit; module `subfleet/sessions/handoff.py`, `subfleet/policy.py`]
- **C-23.37** (stranded capacity is spent first) A lane under an unexpired closure scoped to a model above the one the job needs, with no `account` closure and a free slot, is model-stranded. The Claude comparator orders model-stranded lanes ahead of unstranded ones and then applies C-11.3's order within each group. [rows 108 (`keep`, routing-policy); milestone 3; owner unit; module `subfleet/policy.py`]
- **C-23.38** (which lane a reset credit is spent on) Reset-credit redemption orders candidate lanes by weekly reset furthest out first, then fewest in-flight attempts, then lowest lane number. This ordering governs redemption only; C-11.3 continues to order routing candidates. [rows 162 (`keep`, routing-policy); milestone 5; owner unit; module `subfleet/actions.py`]
- **C-23.39** (revive keeps the session's own tier) Revive launches on the tier recorded in the session's registry row. A different tier is used only when the operator passes `--model`, and the substitution is recorded. [rows 186 (`keep`, routing-policy); milestone 6; owner unit; module `subfleet/sessions/tickle.py`]
- **C-23.40** (finding the transcript attestation reads) The transcript for a session uuid is looked for at `<projects>/<session id>.jsonl` and `<projects>/*/<session id>.jsonl` and nowhere deeper; no search walks a workdir or a worktree. Attestation waits for a transcript that has not landed yet at most `transcript_wait_tries` (4) times with a one-second backoff, and then the verdict is `unattested`. [rows 19 (`keep`, provenance/attestation), 218 (`keep`, ops-hygiene); milestone 2; owner unit; module `subfleet/adapters/claude.py`]
- **C-23.41** (a nested submission mints its own identity) A submission made from inside a running attempt takes no identity from its environment: the daemon mints a fresh job id and, absent `--request-id`, a fresh request id. An inherited `SUBFLEET_JOB` is read only as the default `--parent`, and an inherited `SUBFLEET_ATTEMPT` — which C-5.1 and C-5.5 require the child to keep — is never an identity claim. [rows 32 (`replace`, provenance/attestation); milestone 1; owner fake; module `subfleet/daemon.py`]
- **C-23.42** (a notice says what the recipient may do) Every notice envelope declares the recipient session's own recorded permission class. `SUBFLEET_NOTIFY_MODE` in the recipient's environment overrides the recorded value, and the override is recorded on the notice. [rows 74 (`keep`, provenance/attestation); milestone 4; owner unit; module `subfleet/notices.py`]
- **C-23.43** (an unattested round is not a verdict) A Claude peer round counts only when its attestation is `attested` for the model the gate asked for, because the provider can silently serve another model. A Codex peer pins its model at launch and its CLI has no such fallback, so a Codex round counts when its attestation is `attested` or `unattested`, and the gate's round record notes which (the certificate keeps v1's exact content); installed Codex 0.153.3 persists no rollout for an isolated ephemeral run, so `unattested` is the common case there. A round whose attestation is `mismatch`, or which carries a downgrade record, is discarded and re-run on either provider; it is never counted as agreement or as changes requested. (Amended 2026-09-05 16:45 from the gates lane's finding.) [rows 197 (`keep`, provenance/attestation); milestone 7; owner unit; module `subfleet/gate.py`]
- **C-23.44** (what an auth-dead lane costs) An `auth-dead` result disables the lane at once. A disabled lane is not a routing candidate, not a keepalive target, and not probed — no request is sent to it — until `subfleet lanes enroll` rebinds its credential, which records a new lane id under C-1.3; the CLI exits 5 and names that command. The lane's auth-dead detail is logged at most once a day. One exception (2026-10-03): the `auth-dead` of a job that already moved on from one lane fault (C-4.5) does not disable its lane. That job ends `failed` with rc 5, the attempt records `auth_dead_again` (`lane_id`, `lane_left_enabled`), `daemon.log` and the notice say the lane was left enabled, and the next attempt there, another job's, decides. With the pilot hold on (C-6.14) that lane is one a model answered on within `admission.prove_idle_s`, or one Subfleet's probe just answered on, since a job that moved on reaches no other lane unprobed (C-4.5); with the hold off, a job that moved on can meet a second dead lane as its first attempt there, and then that lane stays enabled until another job's first `auth-dead` or a keepalive or heal turn finds it. The job whose attempt found a dead lane moves on to the next candidate when that attempt is a lane fault (C-4.5: no lane pin, no turn, its workspace as it found it, its first), without the attempt counting, and `daemon.log` says so; a pinned job, or one whose workspace changed, ends `failed` with rc 5 as before. The job's notice (C-15.1) names each lane it moved on from: ``attempt a<seq>: lane <lane id> went auth-dead (<detail>); it is disabled until `subfleet lanes enroll` rebinds its credential, and the job moved on to the next lane without counting the attempt``, and a second lane as ``attempt a<seq>: lane <lane id> answered auth-dead too; two lanes refusing one job points at the job, so <lane id> was left enabled``. Since C-6.14 a lane that has not answered for `admission.prove_idle_s` takes one detached attempt before any other, so a dead lane disables after one attempt, with the rest of a burst still queued. [rows 116 (`replace`, identity), 175 (`keep`, identity); milestone 2 for the disable, 5 for the keepalive and log cadence; owner fake; module `subfleet/credentials.py`]
- **C-23.45** (one account, one enabled lane) At most one lane is canonical for an account key. Enrolment and each probe cycle detect a second lane bound to an account key another lane already holds, mark the later binding non-canonical, and raise a critical alert naming both homes. [rows 136 (`keep`, identity); milestone 2 for detection, 5 for the alert; owner unit; module `subfleet/credentials.py`]
- **C-23.46** (what shadowing changes and what it does not) A lane whose account is a provider desktop app's current login is recorded as shadowed; that fact alone never changes its dispatch order. Reset-credit redemption excludes a shadowed lane while any unshadowed lane holds a concrete `available` credit, and redeems on a shadowed lane only when none does. [rows 138 (`keep`, identity), 163 (`keep`, identity); milestone 5; owner unit; module `subfleet/actions.py`]
- **C-23.47** (the auth store belongs to the provider CLI) subfleet never writes a provider auth store and never refreshes a token in process. A lane whose Codex token looks merely expired gets exactly one automatic heal — a minimal `codex exec` turn under that lane's home, so the CLI refreshes and persists its own `auth.json` — followed by a re-probe. A `refresh token was revoked` result latches the lane: it is probed no further until the credential epoch of C-10.1, the home's `auth.json` `last_refresh`, changes. [rows 139 (`keep`, identity), 150 (`keep`, identity), 151 (`keep`, identity); milestone 2 for the prohibition, 5 for the heal and the latch; owner fake; module `subfleet/credentials.py`, `subfleet/alerts.py`]
- **C-23.48** (what the session hook counts as a launch) The session hook blocks a provider CLI or a v1 runner only in command position; a mention inside a quoted string, a heredoc, or an inspection command such as `bash -n` is not a launch. A runner's own detach flag passes, and `SUBFLEET_ATTACHED_OK=1` in the tool call's environment is the explicit one-off override. [rows 68 (`keep`, UX-contract), 69 (`keep`, UX-contract); milestone 4; owner unit; module `subfleet/hooks.py`]
- **C-23.49** (one envelope per notice) A notice is delivered as exactly one envelope. Any sequence in the body that would close the envelope early is neutralised before the envelope is written. [rows 75 (`keep`, UX-contract); milestone 4; owner unit; module `subfleet/notices.py`]
- **C-23.50** (the push waits behind a live waiter) The best-effort socket push is skipped while the job has a `wait` or `--attach` waiter whose recorded identity is still live, and is attempted once that waiter is no longer live. The push is never the reason a caller learns about a job twice. [rows 79 (`keep`, UX-contract); milestone 4; owner fake; module `subfleet/notices.py`]
- **C-23.51** (a running job never falls off the list) `runs --last N` bounds terminal jobs only. Every job in a live state is listed whatever N is and however old it is, and `--json` output carries the same set. [rows 89 (`keep`, UX-contract); milestone 1; owner unit; module `subfleet/cli.py`]
- **C-23.52** (what an alert says and when a recovery is one) subfleet never performs a provider login; every alert about a credential names the exact command the operator must run. A recovery notice is emitted only when a condition has cleared and no other condition on the same home is active; a move from one condition to another is reported as the new condition, not as a recovery. [rows 149 (`keep`, UX-contract), 156 (`keep`, UX-contract); milestone 5; owner unit; module `subfleet/alerts.py`]
- **C-23.53** (a gate stops) A gate stops after `gate_max_rounds` (4) peer rounds without agreement and reports the blocker, naming the last verdict. The cap is a `policy.json` cap under C-6.4. [rows 200 (`keep`, UX-contract); milestone 7; owner unit; module `subfleet/gate.py`]
- **C-23.54** (one dispatch path) Every provider launch is a `subfleet run` submission, including subfleet's own — a handoff is dispatched detached through the ordinary submit path so it inherits routing, the guard, salvage, the ledger, and notices. The session hook blocks a provider CLI or a v1 runner invoked directly from a session's Bash tool and names `subfleet run` as the replacement. [rows 67 (`keep`, process-survival), 211 (`keep`, process-survival); milestone 4 for the hook, 6 for handoff; owner fake; module `subfleet/hooks.py`, `subfleet/sessions/handoff.py`]
- **C-23.55** (one live revive per session) A session has at most one live revive: the revive lease `session:<session id>:revive` is taken in the transaction that admits the attempt, and a session that already holds it is skipped rather than launched again. The census the sweep skips on is the lease rows, read inside the admitting transaction, not a snapshot taken at the start of the pass. [rows 192 (`keep`, process-survival); milestone 6; owner fake; module `subfleet/sessions/tickle.py`]

## 24. Conversations and messages

Milestone 9, the desktop workspace. Specification: `docs/desktop/design.md` revision 3 (cited as design §n or D-n). Every clause in sections 24 to 30 names its owner layer and module in the trailer, as section 23 does.

- **C-24.1** (a conversation is a daemon row) A conversation binds one provider, at most one native session id (unique per provider; recorded only once the provider holds that session: a Codex thread id its server returned, or a Claude session with a terminal event, an acknowledgement or a transcript on disk, so a session id Subfleet minted is never resumed before the provider created it), one workspace directory for its life, settings `{model, effort, fast, permission, auto_continue}`, and an origin (`new`, `native`, `handoff`, `legacy`); a Codex conversation also names the lane whose home holds its thread. Conversation state lives in `conversations.sqlite3`, written only by the daemon; `state.sqlite3` keeps schema 5. [milestone 9; owner unit; module `subfleet/conversations/store.py`; design D-4]
- **C-24.2** (a message is accepted once, in order) A message carries a client UUID, its client predecessor, and a digest over its conversation, text, attachment SHA-256s and settings. A repeated UUID with the same digest returns the stored receipt with `created: false`; a different digest is exit 2 `message-id-conflict`; a predecessor the daemon has not committed is exit 2 `out-of-order`. Neither refusal changes anything. [milestone 9; owner unit; module `subfleet/conversations/store.py`; design D-22]
- **C-24.3** (intent before dispatch) A message's text is published under `conversations/<id>/messages/` (C-8.1) and its row committed before `message.submit` returns, and before any job exists for it. A receipt is local acknowledgement only. The conversation store publishes a text, like an approval's request (C-27.1), only while it is open, and makes the directories below the state root (each 0700) but never the root itself: once closed it refuses (`store-closed`, exit 1), and with the root gone it fails `state-root-gone` (exit 1). A text published just before the store closed stays with no row, as after a crash between the two. Reading accepted text uses a bounded, nonblocking, no-follow descriptor for a private regular file owned by the daemon user with no second hard link, verifies the message digest over its original text, attachments and settings, and only then normalizes newlines; changed text is refused. [milestone 9; owner fake; module `subfleet/conversations/service.py`, `subfleet/conversations/store.py`]
- **C-24.4** (states are the provider's facts) Message states are those of design D-12. `running` requires the provider's acknowledgement of that message (Claude `command_lifecycle started` or the replayed user message carrying its UUID; Codex a `turn/start` response carrying a turn id). A provider-reported success is `complete` even when a stop was requested. A message in `waiting` always has a non-empty `state_reason` (2026-09-29, I3; the store refuses `waiting` without one), `<kind>: <detail>` or one of the reasons the dispatcher writes before its turn job exists: `dispatching` (its claim, C-24.7), `deferred: <why>` (C-26.1), `external-writer: pid <n>` (C-26.3) and `readmit:<why>` (C-24.6). Once the job exists it is `admission: sent to the daemon, which has not placed it yet` until admission's turn pass looks at the job, and after every turn pass it says why that pass left the job unplaced (`subfleet/conversations/waits.py`, from C-6.11's hold; written only when it changes, and looked at again at least every 30 s while one hold stands, since a lease's holder or a title may change under it; two workers run turn passes, and the notes of a pass older than the last one noted are dropped, never written over a newer pass's; a note that found its message not yet bound to the job is tried again a second later): `lease: <who>` naming the job that holds what it needs and, for a turn, that conversation's title; `capacity: <detail>` only when some lane would take it once room frees (its slots busy, its usage at the floor or kept for the reserved model) or a turn cap holds it; `closed: <detail>` when every lane that could take it is closed, naming the first reset; `usage-unknown: <detail>` when no lane has a fresh usage reading to take it on; `no-lane: <detail>` for refusals no wait ends (disabled, signed in to another account, a probe a turn never waits for), the lanes' reasons counted in every lane case, and for the lane a Codex conversation keeps (C-26.2) when it refuses the turn so (C-11.8's `pin-unadmittable`), that lane and its refusals named; `blocked`, `workspace`, `route` and `admission` for the other holds; and `placed: starting the provider` once placed, until its runner starts. Incident: on 2026-09-28 four messages waited 29 minutes to 13 hours for another conversation's turn in their folder with no `state_reason`, and the app, which fell back to "Waiting for capacity", said capacity while lanes were free. [milestone 9; owner unit; module `subfleet/conversations/claude_turn.py`, `codex_turn.py`, `subfleet/conversations/waits.py`]
- **C-24.5** (one turn at a time per conversation) A conversation's next message gets a turn job only when the previous turn job is terminal and has released its `conversation:` and `native:` leases, and the conversation is not blocked. A conversation is blocked while its `blocked_by` (the service's own: `unfinished-turn`, `delivery-unknown`, `quarantined-turn`) or its `legacy_hold` (the legacy import's, C-30.4) is set. While it is, no message is dispatched or re-admitted, and a turn job created before the block places nothing at admission (hold `conversation-blocked`, C-6.11). Different conversations run concurrently under admission, also when they share a folder (one write target, C-6.5): each writable turn holds a row of its own on the folder, `worktree-turn:<target>:<job id>`, and none holds `worktree:<target>`, so no conversation waits for another's turn there (the owner's ruling of 2026-09-28, "nothing should be queued"; the Claude app lets several sessions share a folder). A read-only turn holds `worktree-read:<folder>:<job id>` (its checkout's top, or the directory outside git), which excludes no writer. Two turns of one conversation, or on one native session, still never run at once (`conversation:` and `native:`, C-26.3). A turn and a detached writer in one folder still exclude each other: a writable turn waits `lease-held` on `worktree:<target>` while a detached writer (or retention's fence, C-8.4) holds it, a detached writer waits at admission while any `worktree-turn:` row is on its folder, and one submitted then is refused (C-6.5). Why they still exclude each other: C-6.5 promises a detached in-place job that it writes its checkout alone, so the salvage ref taken at its end (C-13.1) is its own work, and salvage never snapshots a folder while a conversation writes in it (I5); detached writers normally run in worktrees of their own (C-6.6), so this meets a conversation only for an explicit `--in-place` job or one whose worktree a conversation continues, and the wait names the job that holds the folder (C-24.4). What another conversation changed in the folder during a turn is labelled on that turn's changes (C-26.14). A detached writer's edits are not: none is made from the turn's reservation to its end, but admission takes the turn's start snapshot before its reserving transaction, so a detached writer that held the folder after that snapshot began and released it before the reservation can leave edits in the turn's diff that nothing names (review of 5e9f2fbd, P3-1; a known limit). `steering` is live and holds the queue until its host settles; `steered` is terminal and never gets its own job. A missed steer keeps its original sequence and runs next: the dispatcher takes it ahead of every queued message that was not steered, behind only a repair message, and in sequence among missed steers (C-24.9); its reason keeps the `steer-missed:` mark the queue orders by when a submit of its turn is deferred, and when a handoff that cancelled its turn job is rolled back (C-30.3). Handoff refuses a live steer. [milestone 9; owner fake; module `subfleet/conversations/dispatch.py`; design §4]
- **C-24.6** (no re-send after ambiguity) A message whose delivery is uncertain is never sent to a provider again automatically. Every turn that ends without the provider's terminal event (Claude `result`; Codex `turn/completed` or its answer to `turn/start`), whether stdout ended or the driver's own check ended it, is reconciled as design D-14: `delivered` when the attempt's stdout acknowledged the message (C-24.4) or the native record holds it (Claude: a `user` record with the message id as `uuid` in the session transcript after the attempt's `transcript_offset`; Codex: a rollout record whose `client_id` is the message id); `not-delivered` only with the process verified gone (the attempt's exit receipt), the native record read without it (or no native session exists for the attempt), and a relay log that was read whole and consistent and holds no record of the message frame. Otherwise the message is `delivery-unknown` and blocks its conversation until a person's `message.resolve` with the JSON literal `confirm: true`, which records who and when and never resends. A person's Stop does not count as delivery evidence: it preserves a genuinely unknown delivery until resolved; once resolved, a recorded personal stop settles as `interrupted` with reason `stopped`, without an unfinished-turn block. A driver's refusal before sending that another admission may pass (`external-writer`, `fast-unavailable`, `provider-init-failed`, `guard-refused`; review IR-23) re-admits the same message as a new turn job only when reconciled `not-delivered` and the person has not stopped it, at most 3 times (counted by the message's other turn jobs a provider reached, so a handoff's rolled-back cancels count for nothing, less those whose `turn.json` shows a wait for the session's other writer; one whose outcome cannot be read counts), and then fails it `not-delivered`. A wait for the session's other writer (`external-writer`, and the legacy hold's `legacy-owner`, C-30.4) is re-admitted however often it happens and uses up none of those 3, then or later. Each steer settles before its host: consumed/delivered becomes `steered`, an echoed Codex steer with no following agent output becomes `steered-unanswered:<host>`, proven unsent/refused/cancelled returns to `queued`, and a written or ambiguous relay intent with no provider fate becomes `delivery-unknown`. Positive consumption evidence takes precedence over a subsequent cancellation. Resolving one unknown does not unblock other unknown messages. [milestone 9; owner fake; module `subfleet/conversations/reconcile.py`]
- **C-24.7** (stop is scoped and ends the turn) `message.cancel` withdraws a message only before its job has an attempt, checked in the transaction that records the cancel, except a message with no job bound, which it withdraws even if a job the dispatcher made for it meanwhile has been given an attempt: the stop recorded with that withdrawal keeps the attempt from ever writing the message (below). Withdrawing an id the daemon never received (naming its conversation) records a tombstone that is cancelled in the transaction that creates it; a later `message.submit` of that id returns the tombstone and nothing is dispatched (review IR-7). The dispatcher claims a message (`waiting`, reason `dispatching`) in the conversation store before it creates the message's job, so a handoff that sees the claim refuses rather than racing the submit (IR-28); `message.cancel` still withdraws a message with no job bound, claimed or not, and the job the dispatcher then creates finds it withdrawn and is cancelled before any attempt (IR-2); `turn.interrupt` of a waiting message with no job bound withdraws it too. A cancel records the person's stop in the transaction that withdraws the message, so a runner admission started for that job meanwhile never writes it: a turn whose message has a person's stop recorded before the relay log shows the message handed over is stopped before it is ever written (a message frame whose send was already under way when the stop was recorded, its answer lost, counts as handed over when the relay's log, read after a fresh `status` answer, holds it). A person's stop is recorded, and a runner hands the message frame to the relay, under one lock per message, and the runner reads the stored stop again under it just before the handover, so a stop recorded after the runner started (while the provider answers `initialize`, say) still keeps the message unwritten, and one made during the handover is recorded after it and stops the turn as below; a stop the runner was asked for before the handover (the daemon's wall limit or kill) keeps it unwritten too. A relay slow to take one message's frame holds up the stops and cancels of that message alone. The withdrawal also cancels such a job while it has no attempt, and admission cancels any turn job whose message has settled (hold `message-settled`, C-6.11). A cancel refused (too late, or the message being handed to its job) records no stop, so nothing it did stops the turn it lost to, and clears none; a person's cancels and stops of one message are taken one at a time. A claim is released (a message that was queued, or whose claim a crash interrupted, goes back to `queued`) when its submit is deferred. `turn.interrupt` targets only that message's attempt and escalates as design D-13: the provider's interrupt, SIGINT to the provider child through its guardian's relay, closing stdin, and only then C-5.6 containment. Each later step is taken only while the turn has no terminal event, at policy `conversations.stop_sigint_after_s`, `stop_close_after_s` and `stop_contain_after_s` seconds after the stop was requested (defaults 10, 20, 30); the policy loader refuses a value that is not a positive finite number and an order other than SIGINT before closing before containment. No other conversation, job, or process is signalled. A steer cancellation succeeds only before its relay handover; afterwards it refuses `too-late`. Steer frames take both host and message handover locks, so neither a recorded host stop nor its own cancellation permits a late write. Stop applies to the entire host: `turn.interrupt` of a `steering` message stops its host (a steer has no attempt of its own), and the steers the provider shows it had not read (never handed over, or shown not taken: Claude's `cancel_queued` receipt or a cancelled or refused lifecycle; Codex's refusal, or an accepted steer never echoed before a proven interrupted end) return to the queue and run next (C-24.5); a written steer the provider reports nothing about settles `delivery-unknown` (C-24.6, C-26.5). Stop never pulls them back into the composer. Claude requests `cancel_queued: true` when supported and the turn has steers (a turn with none sends the interrupt it always sent), and uses the receipt to settle pending steers. A recorded personal stop settles a turn without a terminal event as `interrupted` with reason `stopped` once delivery is known, whether delivered or not delivered, and never sets `unfinished-turn` or re-admits that message. Settlement and the person's stop are serialized per message. Waiting messages remain queued and run next after the stopped attempt releases its leases; a provider success still wins (C-24.4), and genuinely unknown delivery still blocks (C-24.6). [milestone 9; owner fake; module `subfleet/conversations/service.py`]
- **C-24.8** (an unfinished Claude turn blocks) A Claude attempt that ends without `result` after its message was delivered (C-24.6: acknowledged, or in the session transcript; review IR-5), including a system stop that got no `result` and a driver's own stop (a model mismatch) that no `result` followed, sets the conversation `blocked_by: "unfinished-turn"`; a written message frame whose delivery is unknown blocks it as `delivery-unknown` (C-24.6), and a person's `message.resolve` of a Claude message as `delivered` turns that block into `unfinished-turn`. A recorded personal stop is the exception: it settles without `unfinished-turn`, including after resolving an unknown delivery (C-24.7). For an unfinished turn, the next message waits for a person's `conversation.unblock` (`continue` or `leave`), recorded. `leave` sends a one-line note ahead of any queued message, so the next turn does not resume the stopped work; `continue` sends nothing. [milestone 9; owner fake; module `subfleet/conversations/reconcile.py`, `subfleet/conversations/service.py`]
- **C-24.9** (steer) A person may steer any queued person message into the conversation’s running or approval-needed host with a steerable runner (the messages queued for later keep their order), except while a repair message (an unblock note or a failover continuation) is queued, which refuses `not-next`. It runs under the host’s settings, and a message whose permission is narrower than the host’s refuses (`settings-narrower`). A `/` command or `!` shell input is never steered: the app queues it (C-29.7) and the daemon refuses it (`not-steerable`); leading Unicode White_Space is skipped, as Swift’s `Character.isWhitespace` does. A request that names the host it was meant for (`into`) is refused `no-live-turn` once another turn is the live one, so a late or retried steer never joins a different turn; a live turn whose runner the daemon has not taken back yet after a restart (or that is settling) refuses `not-steerable`, which the app asks again about (C-29.7). The service atomically claims the message as `steering`, reason `steer:<host>`, before enqueueing the runner command. Delivery becomes `steered`, reason `steered:<host>` or `steered-unanswered:<host>`; a proven miss returns to `queued`, reason `steer-missed: <why>`, keeping its sequence, and runs next (C-24.5). A steer whose input cannot be built (an image gone or changed, a driver failure of any kind) returns to the queue the same way and never ends its host’s runner; its images resolve by digest (C-28.1), not through the host’s. No steer is both delivered and requeued, lost, written after close/stop/cancel, or sent twice on replay.
