A turn with no steers retains its existing delivery/outcome rules: for every recorded history with no steer (`tests/unit/no_steer_histories.py`, 172: both providers, acknowledged or not, allowing, denying or stopping the turn from an approval, stopped or not, a model mismatch, an initialize or `turn/start` refused, ending with success, an error or stdout's end), every frame, event, approval and outcome its driver produces are those of the pre-steer code (commit 52ffde17), checked against a recording of it (`tests/fixtures/steer/no_steer_presteer.json`); for the 31 of them a provider writes alone (no person's answer or stop mid-turn), so is what the runner loop writes and the service settles. Outcomes and `turn.json` carry an empty `steers`, and anything in it fails the check. The one deliberate difference: once a Codex turn's id is known, a notification naming another turn is ignored (C-26.6); a recorded history checks that it differs there alone (the pre-steer code showed another turn's text as this turn's). Invariants 1 (exactly once), 4 (restart) and 5 have generated or differential coverage through the runner's own loop, the real drivers, store and settlement; invariant 2 (queue order) through the real store and settlement; invariant 3 (no write after close, stop or cancel) through the real drivers and the runner's handover checks against a relay journal. Driver, runner, service and fake-provider end-to-end tests exercise the boundary races. [milestone 9; owner unit/fake; module `subfleet/conversations/turn.py`, `subfleet/conversations/store.py`, `subfleet/conversations/service.py`, `subfleet/conversations/runner.py`, `subfleet/conversations/claude_turn.py`, `subfleet/conversations/codex_turn.py`]

- **C-24.10** (durable unattended wakes) The daemon batches terminal outcomes of runs dispatched by a conversation turn into one message with origin `wake`, displayed as Subfleet and beginning `[Subfleet]`; each run says `<id> finished: <state>; deliverable <path>`. The turn job is the run's parent; older jobs are associated by the caller session used by `runs --mine` and notices, within a recorded turn's lifetime. Automatic completion discovery starts at the persisted activation timestamp, so upgrade does not announce historical runs. A one-time snapshot excludes jobs already completed at activation; the time floor is rounded to the stored second precision and comparisons normalize ISO timestamps. A run started earlier can still wake when it finishes after activation, including that same second; legacy completions without finished_at use created_at. Explicit requests may still name older runs. Completions already surfaced by a hook or acknowledged are not offered again automatically. Runs covered by a pending all-of request are gathered into its single wake; an already-delivered runs kind is satisfied without sending a message or charging the throttle, while that request's timer and PR alternatives stay pending. A request is never satisfied silently as a whole until every kind is resolved; a fired request names its accepted wake message. A registered run whose row was pruned is finished with state `pruned`. A live turn finishes before a wake is submitted: C-24.9's steer admits person messages only. Accepting the message, consuming requests and run ids, and incrementing the unattended counter share one durable transaction. Completion notices become surfaced with transport `conversation` afterwards, repaired from a durable queue in batches of at most 500 after a crash. Historical receipts are backfilled once on upgrade; completed repairs do no further writes. Notice lookup by job is indexed. Automatic discovery and request evaluation run at most once per second on the control loop, eligibility uses a read, and target reads are batched. An idle evaluation opens no write transaction and notifies no readers. `subfleet wake --runs ID... --pr OWNER/REPO#N... --at ISO --note TEXT [--request-id UUID]` records against the calling turn or its bound session (`conversation.wake`, capability `wake.v1`). A run trigger waits for all named runs; a PR trigger waits for any watched PR to merge, close, finish checks, or gain a submitted review; a time trigger waits until its instant. Kinds within a request are alternatives: the first ready kind consumes the request once. At most one pending request per kind (`runs`, `pr`, `time`) exists per conversation; a new request supersedes the old request of that kind. Each kind has at most 16 targets, each at most 200 characters, and notes at most 4,000 characters. `at` requires a timezone and must be at least five minutes away when first accepted; an identical request-id retry remains accepted afterwards. PR polling uses one batched `gh api graphql` query across conversations per cycle, a persisted timestamp at least 60 seconds apart, a 20-second timeout, and durable baselines and ready events. A transport failure consumes nothing. Alias-specific errors retain valid data for other PRs and produce one explicit refusal wake for the affected watch. Refused targets persist per conversation; re-arming one is refused visibly without another wake. Re-arms carry the last fired request's observed snapshot forward; an observation never delivered cannot become a new baseline. A first observation without a previous snapshot fires only for events timestamped strictly after registration, or after turn-job creation for a final-text request (message creation if no job exists), so results arriving during that turn remain visible. A partial check set cannot prove completion. The bounded PR worker does not block control-loop dispatch and is joined before store shutdown. A wake waits while blocked (including legacy holds), archived, while any person message waits, while a turn runs or is queued, or while its conversation lease is held. At eight wakes without a person message it is throttled to one every 30 minutes: eight permits several build/review rounds, and the cooldown bounds check-back loops while permitting unattended progress. Person acceptance resets the counter. A wake waiting at capacity yields its unstarted job and retains its message behind personal input; wakes never change the personal predecessor chain or reorder personal messages. The final text may end with consecutive `WAKE-ME:` lines: `WAKE-ME: runs=ID[,ID...] prs=OWNER/REPO#N[,OWNER/REPO#N...] at=ISO note="TEXT"`. Fields are optional, unique, whitespace-separated with shell quoting; at least one trigger is required. Only top-level trailing lines are parsed, allowing bullets, bold markup and one standard DONE, WAITING ON MAX or HANDED TO close-out. Empty target fields are ignored when another trigger exists. Fences, quoted lines and indented code cannot register requests. Each line is independently accepted or refused, with stable replay ids. Final-text timers validate the five-minute floor against both now and turn-job creation (message creation if there is no job); past or nearer timers are refused. Identical request-id replays retain their original acceptance. Invalid requests are logged and displayed as a refusal notice without failing a successful turn. [owner fake; module `subfleet/conversations/wakes.py`, `store.py`, `service.py`, `subfleet/cli.py`; design continuation amendment]
- **C-24.11** (a superseded block clears) A service block records `blocked_at` independently of subsequent queued messages. At most once per five seconds per unchanged block, a native Claude transcript's catalog mtime strictly later than that timestamp makes `unfinished-turn`, `delivery-unknown`, or `quarantined-turn` moot once admission's fresh C-26.3 `external_writers` check finds no outside writer, no Subfleet turn remains live or queued, and no conversation/native lease is held. Clear only the observed block and timestamp, preserving legacy holds and handoff fences, and record `conversation.unblocked` with reason `continued-elsewhere`. An unknown message superseded this way settles `failed: continued-elsewhere` without claiming delivery or resending it (an exception to C-24.6's personal resolution requirement). An older store recovers the block timestamp from its last failed/unknown/interrupted message, before later queued input. [owner unit; module `subfleet/conversations/service.py`, `store.py`, `catalog.py`; design continuation amendment]
- **C-24.12** (one real native history) `conversation.open` includes a bounded native-history page; it and `conversation.history` run on a separate bounded history read pool and use the catalog's resolved transcript paths, including bound sessions excluded from the sidebar. Before a catalog record exists, a recorded attempt path or exact Claude workspace/session path is used. Neither scans native trees. Claude prompt UUIDs and Codex turn-context ids classify native turns as Subfleet or `other-app`; interrupts, compaction summaries, tool results and task notifications within a Claude turn do not start new prompt groups; partial groups whose ownership cannot be read within the cap retain the conservative timestamp filter. The frontend removes Subfleet duplicates, labels other-app turns "Made in another app", and merges them chronologically with Subfleet events, including after binding and after event replay. Opening or switching conversations scrolls to the newest row at the bottom. [owner frontend; module `subfleet/conversations/history.py`, `catalog.py`, `app/Sources/Timeline.swift`, `ConversationStore.swift`, `UIWindow.swift`; design continuation amendment]

## 25. Protocol additions

- **C-25.1** (discovery before mutation) `capabilities` returns protocol version 1, the daemon version, `conversation_schema` 1 (the version of the conversation ops' argument and result shapes, C-25.2, which is not the conversation store's schema, C-26.14), capability names and limits. A client sends no conversation op to a daemon that answers "unknown op", and sends a field whose silent omission would change an outcome only after the daemon advertised it. `PROTOCOL_VERSION` stays 1. [milestone 9; owner unit; module `subfleet/protocol.py`]
- **C-25.2** (the ops) The conversation ops are `capabilities`, `conversation.list`, `conversation.open`, `conversation.create`, `conversation.settings`, `conversation.unblock`, `conversation.history`, `conversation.events`, `conversation.watch`, `message.submit`, `message.status`, `message.cancel`, `message.steer`, `turn.interrupt`, `message.resolve`, `approval.list`, `approval.get`, `approval.respond`, `attachment.add`, `catalog.refresh`, `models.list`, `conversation.runs`, `turn.diff`, `conversation.diff` and `workspace.check`, listed in `subfleet/protocol.py` (`CONVERSATION_OPS`) and answered by `subfleet/conversations/service.py`, with the arguments and results of design §5. Receipts from `conversation.open` and `message.status` carry the message's `text` (at most 20,000 characters, `text_truncated` when cut), so a client that did not send a message can show it. Errors are `ok: false` with an `Exit` code. An op added to this list is advertised by a `capabilities` name (`diff.v1` for the two diff ops, `runs.v1` for `conversation.runs`), not by a new `conversation_schema`. `steer.v1` advertises `message.steer {message_id, into?}` and `steer_providers: ["claude", "codex"]`; `into`, when sent, is the host the steer was meant for. Its person-only result is a Receipt, idempotent for `steering`/`steered`; refusal codes are `not-queued`, `not-next` (a repair message is queued), `no-live-turn` (no live host, or not the one `into` names), `settings-narrower`, `not-steerable` (the host's runner cannot take it now: not taken back yet or still catching up after a restart, the provider not ready, the turn stopping or ended; or it is a `/` command or `!` shell input). Receipts, message status and change rows include nullable `steered_into`, derived from `state_reason`, with store schema still 2. [milestone 9; owner unit; module `subfleet/protocol.py`]
- **C-25.3** (handlers never wait) A conversation op handler validates, writes at most one transaction per store (a worktree conversation's create writes its row, then its worktree), wakes the control loop, and returns. It never waits on a provider, a probe, `ps`, git, or a catalog scan (C-16.4), with two exceptions: `turn.diff` and `conversation.diff` run git (C-26.14), and `conversation.create` of a worktree conversation runs git to cut its worktree (C-26.10, design D-16). Those three ops (`conversation.create` whatever its workspace kind), `workspace.check` (the same bounded folder and git admission checks), file copies and transcript reads run on a dedicated bounded pool (the file pool), never on the pool the other ops and the session hooks use. The conversation service's `close()` lets the file ops already running finish before it returns, while the state root and the conversation store are still there, drops the ones not yet started, and the pool accepts none after. A file op writes into the state root as it stands and never creates it: with the root gone it fails `state-root-gone` (exit 1). The conversation service (its ops, its control-loop steps and its turn runners) opens every file it reads only as a regular file, and never blocks in `open()`: what it reads of a native session (a Claude transcript, a Codex rollout, Codex's `session_index.jsonl`, a Claude session-registry file), a workspace's `PROGRESS.md`, the real `.git/index` a diff copies, and in the state root an admission job's `manifest.json`, a message's text, an attachment's stored copy, an approval's request, `catalog.json`, `catalog.lock`, `conversations/models.json` and an attempt's files (`stdout`, `stdin.jsonl`, `turn.json`, the JSON records). Admission reads a manifest through `O_NONBLOCK|O_NOFOLLOW`, checks its descriptor with `fstat`, and reads at most 16 MiB; an unreadable manifest holds that job as `conversation-blocked` with its manifest error, without delaying later admission. A FIFO, device or directory there reads as unreadable at once; a stored attachment or a message's text is read only up to its limit (20 MiB, 1 MiB). Contents of the listed service-owned state files are read through a separate no-follow regular-file reader, including attempt stdout and relay logs; `catalog.lock` is opened only for locking, with its existing nonblocking regular-file check. Native transcript readers retain their existing symlink support. A file it writes it makes as a new file of its own (O_EXCL, O_NOFOLLOW) and puts in place by rename, never opening one that stands there, and an attachment's stored copy that does not hash to its name (a FIFO, a partial copy) is replaced by the new copy. Some of what they read of a native session is capped too: a scan for a first task or a session's prompts reads at most the first 64 MiB; a reader that reads lines forward skips a line longer than 64 MiB rather than hold it, so a page of history reads its budget (C-29.8) and, for results past its cursor, the rows that start within its window, each at most 64 MiB, and looks no further back than its budget for a row's start; a registry file is read up to 1 MiB and Codex's index up to 64 MiB, of which a lookup of one thread parses and scrubs only the rows that name it. Settling a turn scans its session's file from the turn's start to its end, and a diff copies the whole of `.git/index`: those are bounded by the file's size alone. So `close()`'s wait for the file ops already running is bounded by those caps and sizes, git's time limits, and the walks of a provider's session trees that find a rollout, which have no budget of their own; and only on a filesystem that answers: `O_NONBLOCK` bounds a FIFO's open, not a read from a stalled mount. A FIFO where a rollout belonged had held a file op, and with it `close()`, until a writer came. The catalog run (C-30.1), a process of its own, opens those files plainly: a FIFO holds that run until its service stops it, never the daemon. `close()` also stops every turn runner and waits, at most 5 s in all, for each to finish the iteration it is in, so what a runner was writing (its events, an approval, `turn.json`, the model catalog in `conversations/models.json`) lands before `close()` returns; it logs each runner still going when the wait ends. The control loop adopts no runner once `close()` has begun, and keeps none whose thread could not start (a thread limit): its next pass adopts that turn again, and a step `close()` overtook is logged as stopped, not failed. After `close()` the conversation store refuses every read and write (`store-closed`, exit 1), and an op that meets the refusal fails with it (`message.status` answers `unknown` only for an id the store holds no message by, and a cancel makes a tombstone only for such an id); a runner's containment write to the main store (D-13) is refused the same way; and a runner's files, the model catalog and message texts (C-24.3) are written only while the store is open, which `close()` waits for; none of them creates the state root. A runner whose last writes are refused still ends, closes its relay and is marked finished, and `close()` logs one it stopped with its outcome unreported; a later daemon replays the attempt from its stdout (C-26.6), and settles a message whose attempt has ended with no runner of its own by replaying that attempt once, so a turn whose report was cut short is settled whichever of finalization and adoption comes first. A settlement writes the conversation's block before the message's state, so one cut short leaves the message live for that replay. `Daemon.close()` waits for its request pool after the service has closed, so an op still running then finishes before the daemon's `close()` returns, its store work refused. The daemon's other work that `Daemon.close()` waits for (the timers' workers through `Timers.stop()`, and its requests and workers pools: `submit`, `gate.*`, `lanes`, an attempt's finalization) reads a file only as a regular file too, never waiting in `open()`: a lane's or the desktop's login (`auth.json`, `.credentials.json`, `.claude.json`), the guard's TRUST, hook, seed files and cached verdicts, `conversations.sqlite3` for `status.json` (checked by `stat` before SQLite opens it by name: a swap between the two is not covered), an attempt's records and artifacts, a Codex rollout a turn attests, a job's prompt, a gate's brief, plan and state, and v1's rosters, plists and run records (a roster that is not a regular file is refused, never read as an empty one an edit would replace); and a file it writes beside one it does not own (`lanes transfer`'s roster and its backup, a raw stream copied from stdout) is a new file of its own (O_EXCL, O_NOFOLLOW). [milestone 9; owner fake; module `subfleet/daemon.py`, `subfleet/conversations/service.py`, `subfleet/conversations/runner.py`, `subfleet/conversations/store.py`, `subfleet/sessions/transcripts.py`]
- **C-25.4** (a bounded cursor, batched writes) `conversation.events` returns events after a sequence number, long-polls at most 50 s, cuts a page at 256 KiB, and says `reset: true` exactly when the cursor is below the conversation's compaction floor (a cursor of 0 included), the highest sequence number compaction removed; each page carries the floor. Compaction removes an attempt's `text.delta` and `thinking.delta` rows, only once the job store has ended the attempt (not `quarantined`), no runner reads it, and its message has been terminal for `conversations.compact_after_s` (default 300 s), at most `conversations.compact_per_tick` (20) attempts per control-loop pass; a replay never stores a removed delta again (review IR-6). Driver output is written at most once per attempt per 250 ms or 64 KiB. It is polling with a wake-up, and no document calls it streaming. [milestone 9; owner fake; module `subfleet/conversations/events.py`]
- **C-25.5** (events and results carry only what a person may see) Events and op results hold user-visible text, provider-displayed reasoning, tool names, scrubbed input summaries of at most 500 characters and scrubbed result previews of at most 2 KiB; a credential-reading call is shown as hidden. Codex raw reasoning, Claude redacted thinking and signatures, and credentials never appear in them. Unscrubbed provider content at rest (attempt stdout, `stdin.jsonl`, prompts, message text) is 0600 in 0700 directories, retained under C-28.2, and never exported or put in a notice. Text scrubbing follows C-23.14's linear scans and character budget before the display cap is applied: a text or thinking block, a result preview or an input summary over the budget keeps its scrubbed head and tail excerpts within its cap (60,000 characters for a text or thinking block), with the marker between them. Approvals are shown in full with token-shaped values masked in place (C-27.1). [milestone 9; owner unit; module `subfleet/conversations/redact.py`]
- **C-25.6** (person-only operations) `approval.respond`, `approval.get`, `message.resolve`, `message.steer`, `conversation.unblock`, and any settings change that widens the permission policy or sets `allow_main` are refused (exit 7) when the caller (by `LOCAL_PEERPID`) or an ancestor is a live guardian's descendant or carries the C-5.1 markers, and accepted only from the installed app's executable (the kernel's path for the process, `proc_pidpath`, never a split command line) or a process with a controlling terminal; a daemon on any state root other than `~/.subfleet` also accepts the development build that `SUBFLEET_DEV_APP_EXECUTABLE` named when it started (design D-21). Approval nonces appear only in person-only results. [milestone 9; owner process; module `subfleet/conversations/peers.py`; design D-8]

## 26. Turns under admission

- **C-26.1** (a turn is a submission the daemon makes) Each turn is a job of kind `turn` with request id `turn:<message id>:<n>`, created only by the daemon's dispatcher through the ordinary submit validation, with `max_attempts` 1, no parent, `in_place` in the conversation's workspace, and the message digest as its payload digest. It is admitted by `evaluate` inside the reserving transaction like any job (C-6.3; C-23.54's one dispatch path). The socket `submit` op refuses `kind: "turn"` and request ids beginning `turn:`. A turn submit the daemon refuses before the job exists sent nothing: a refusal of the message as it is (exit 2 or 7, such as a checkout on `main` without `allow_main`, a missing attachment, or a model the fleet does not route) fails it `not-delivered: <reason>`; any other refusal (exit 1, such as git timing out while inspecting the workspace, or a store or file error) leaves it in its state with `state_reason` `deferred: <why>`, submitted again after 2 s, then 4 s, doubling to at most 300 s, never on every pass. A message withdrawn while its job is being submitted takes that job with it (C-24.7). [milestone 9; owner fake; module `subfleet/conversations/dispatch.py`, `subfleet/conversations/service.py`, `subfleet/daemon.py`]
- **C-26.2** (routing by provider) A Claude turn routes by model across Claude lanes whose credential resolves to the default config directory (`keychain-token`, `env`); a `home`-kind Claude lane is not a candidate (`config-dir`). A Claude turn prefers the lane that served the conversation's previous turn while that lane stays eligible, and each move is recorded with its reason. A Codex turn is pinned to the lane whose home holds its thread; a new Codex conversation's first turn routes by model and pins the conversation to the lane it ran on. [milestone 9; owner fake; module `subfleet/scheduler.py`]
- **C-26.3** (one writer per native session) A turn holds `conversation:<id>` and `native:<provider>:<native session id>` from reservation through finalization or quarantine. Resume, revive and handoff reservations wait while either is held, and `resume` refuses a `turn` source. A live process outside Subfleet holding the session (Claude: a pid in `~/.claude/sessions/*.json` naming it, in whichever case either spells its UUID, without Subfleet markers and not an owned identity; Codex: the thread reports an active turn) makes the turn wait `external-writer`, shown to the person, not a refusal: a Claude message is not dispatched while the process lives (a dispatch wait, with no job made), and an attempt that finds either writer when it starts (the Claude app may take the session while a job waits for admission) ends before its message is written and re-admits it, however often (C-24.6). Admission holds nothing for a writer. Conversation-bound sessions are never listed, nudged, revived, cold-swept or handed off by the sessions kit (C-26.13), whichever case a UUID is spelled in, and the daemon refuses to record a nudge of one. Native UUIDs compare in canonical lowercase form in conflict checks and all native-session lease keys, including old raw keys; opaque native ids and display spellings remain as supplied. [milestone 9; owner fake; module `subfleet/conversations/dispatch.py`, `subfleet/daemon.py`, `subfleet/sessions/`]
- **C-26.4** (the guardian relays, it does not interpret) A turn's guardian owns the provider's stdin as a pipe and accepts connections on `run/<16 hex>.sock` (0600 in a 0700 directory) only from the process `daemon.lock` names (pid, boot id, start time). Frames carry a number, a tag and the SHA-256 of their line; each is logged as an intent with fsync before the pipe write and then as `written` or `failed`. A resent number is acknowledged as a duplicate only when its hash matches a written frame; other content is a conflict; a failed or unfinished write is never reported as applied and ends relaying. It never parses provider messages. The relay advertises its version and frame cap in `start.json` and answers `status`, which is not a frame (no number, never logged, answered only after any frame being applied is logged), with the frames applied, whether stdin is closed, whether the child runs, and the cap. A runner asks for it before it sends or replays anything and then sends nothing on a guess: a relay whose count differs from its log, or whose log shows a frame not written, is not written to; a relay that answers `status` as a malformed frame (version 1) is read from its log as before. `message.submit` refuses a Claude message whose text and images cannot fit one frame (`capabilities` limit `relay_frame_bytes`); a frame over the cap is never sent, stdin is closed instead and the turn is reconciled (C-24.6). An unanswered frame or `status` is retried at most 5 times after doubling pauses; then, as after `conflict` or `failed`, nothing more is written and a turn without a terminal event is stopped as C-24.7 escalates, skipping the steps that need the relay. stdout and stderr remain files; `exit.json` still ends the attempt (C-5.1). [milestone 9; owner process; module `subfleet/relay.py`, `subfleet/guardian.py`, `subfleet/conversations/runner.py`]
- **C-26.5** (a turn's end) The provider's terminal event (Claude `result`; Codex `turn/completed`) ends the turn, after which the daemon closes stdin. Output after it is attached to the same message and never changes the outcome. A Claude turn launches with `CLAUDE_CODE_PRINT_BG_WAIT_CEILING_MS=120000` and without `Monitor`, `CronCreate`, `ScheduleWakeup` and `RemoteTrigger`; a process alive policy `conversations.after_result_s` seconds after its terminal event (default 135: the ceiling plus 15 s) is stopped as C-24.7 escalates. Claude steer sends a UUID-bound stream-json user row with `priority: "next"` only with `msg_lifecycle_v1`. It handles both folding at a tool boundary and a separate queued turn, tracking lifecycle, replay rows and result UUID lists. Pending steers keep stdin open across intermediate results. While the host’s result is held for steers the CLI has not shown taking, and no steer runs as its own turn, a 15 s watchdog asks the CLI to cancel those unseen steers (`cancel_async_message`); 15 s later, with no receipt or a `cancelled: false` one, it gives up those steers alone as unknown (delivery-unknown rather than risking duplication) and the turn ends with the held result. A held result whose `queued_turn_count` is positive with no steer pending is bounded the same way, with nothing to cancel. The watchdog never runs during a steer’s own turn, never cancels or ends a delivered steer, and refuses new steers only while its round is open. A cancel receipt refreshes it. A steer the CLI starts after the held result runs as its own turn until the next result, even one a stop’s receipt or lifecycle then calls cancelled; a result ends every turn a started steer is part of, whether or not its bounded `user_message_uuids` lists it. A result held only for a steer that is then not written (its cancel or a stop won the handover, its frame over the relay cap, its input unreadable) ends the turn at once. stdout ending while the host’s result is held ends the turn with that result; only a steer’s own turn cut short leaves the outcome to reconciliation. [milestone 9; owner fake; module `subfleet/conversations/runner.py`]
- **C-26.6** (drivers replay) Turn drivers are pure: the daemon rebuilds one after a restart by replaying the attempt's stdout; a runner that finds the provider gone reads all of stdout before it ends the turn, and ends a Codex turn whose thread went idle as complete (C-26.5); a later runner restores a recorded no-send execution boundary before starting or replaying its driver, keeps the outcome an earlier runner for the attempt recorded in `turn.json` (it knew what a replay cannot: a legacy withhold, a stop's reason, an idle settle), and writes no new provider work for a recorded terminal turn (neither the user frame nor a person's approval answer), adding only what stdout said after it; an interrupt is not new provider work, so the rebuilt driver's interrupt (a model mismatch's, C-26.8), the close and the stop's signals are still sent; a frame whose tag the relay log shows as written is not sent again; replay reconstructs the state and events of a handed-over user frame without rebuilding its image payload, even if attachments have disappeared or the state root moved, and an interrupt it shows written is one the rebuilt driver asked for; a person's or legacy hold's stop recorded after the message was handed over is the rebuilt turn's stop too, its interrupt sent (unless written) once the replay has read the stdout there is, so the turn settles as stopped, as it would have without the restart; events are unique by `(attempt, source, position, ordinal)` with no NULL part and are not stored at or below the attempt's watermark. Codex steer sends `turn/steer` with threadId, expectedTurnId, input and clientUserMessageId, waiting for the turn id when necessary. A matching userMessage.clientId establishes delivery, later agent output establishes an answer, and late responses/items remain readable after turn completion. Once the turn's id is known, a notification whose `turnId` names another turn is ignored; before it is known, none is dropped (a steer is sent only once it is known). Both drivers expose steer(message_id, text, images) and Outcome.steers. The runner tags frames `steer:<mid>`, records written/unsent/refused and fate in turn.json, replays written intents without resending, and reissues unwritten claims only after stdout replay catches up while still steerable. Oversized steer frames are refused without closing host stdin. A steer’s images resolve by digest as the host’s do (C-28.1): the Claude driver reads verified bytes and the Codex driver publishes a sha-keyed copy into the attempt directory; any failure building or taking a steer requeues it (`steer-missed: input-unavailable: …`) and the runner goes on. [milestone 9; owner unit; module `subfleet/conversations/claude_turn.py`, `codex_turn.py`, `runner.py`]
- **C-26.7** (limits end the turn; continuation is new) A limit or credit refusal after a message reached the provider ends the turn `failed` (`limited`); its closure is recorded at finalization (C-9.6) before anything else is submitted. With the conversation's `auto_continue` on, a Claude conversation gets a continuation message (new id, `origin: failover`, fixed text, excluding the limited lane), one per limited message and at most two per original message, written before the limited message shows `failed` (it cannot run while that message is live), ahead of any queued person message; the original message is never sent again. A Codex conversation waits for its lane. [milestone 9; owner fake; module `subfleet/conversations/dispatch.py`, `subfleet/conversations/service.py`]
- **C-26.8** (served facts, not requested ones) Each turn records the lane, account label, served model, effort, and Fast state the provider reported. Settings are validated against the provider's own catalog for that account before the message is sent (design D-19): the lane's claimed account is its email label (C-1.4), compared with the email Claude's `initialize` reports; a Claude model is a catalog value (`opus[1m]`) or a model id, resolved through the entry with that value or else the entry that resolves to the model admission routed to; a Codex turn always asks for the routed model id; a served model that differs from the request (after removing `[1m]`, ignoring `<synthetic>`) ends the turn `failed` (`model-mismatch`). A turn attempt is classified from structured provider evidence only (design §7), never from prose. `models.list` reports what each provider's catalog offers; a value not yet observed is checked by the driver before the message is sent. Claude Fast is shown as billing usage credits and needs a per-conversation confirmation; a served Fast state that differs from the request is a visible warning. [milestone 9; owner fake; module `subfleet/conversations/claude_turn.py`, `codex_turn.py`, `classify.py`] Claude's ultracode (xhigh effort plus standing dynamic-workflow orchestration) is an effort a conversation may name wherever the model's catalog offers xhigh, and `models.list` offers it there: the turn runs with `--effort xhigh` and `{"ultracode": true}` in its command-line settings (the settings key 2.1.280 documents; its undocumented `--effort ultracode` alias is not used), or with the xhigh effort alone when read-only, whose tools include no Workflow tool. The effort a Claude turn records as served is the provider's own report: right after the message the driver asks `get_settings`, and its `applied` answer gives `ultracode` when that flag is on, else the effort applied (`none` when the provider applied none); what the command line asked for is kept as `effort_requested`. A CLI without `get_settings` leaves the served effort unrecorded, and a runner that finds the message already sent by an earlier runner (a replay, including an attempt from before this rule) does not ask. A message that names no effort runs at the policy's `conversations.default_effort` for its provider (ultracode for Claude, the provider's own for Codex), resolved per turn and only where the catalog a turn last reported for the model offers it; `default_effort: null` turns it off for every provider. A default is never refused before sending: where the account's own catalog lacks it the turn still goes ahead and the provider applies what the model allows (2.1.280 drops xhigh and ultracode for Haiku), which the served effort then shows; an effort the person named is refused as before. The stored settings keep `effort: null`, the turn records `effort_default`, and `models.list` names the default (`conversation_default_effort`); the composer shows the efforts and default of the model it has picked, saved or not.
- **C-26.9** (attended turns: first, not reserved) Turn jobs have an admission pass of their own. `_admit` runs it before the pass over detached jobs (unless the turn worker is in one), and the control loop also runs it alone, beside the detached pass, on its own worker (`admission:turns`), so a turn submitted while a detached pass works through a backlog, prepares a workspace or waits on a probe is placed by the next turn pass, not after the detached pass; a turn half that raises does not stop the detached half, and its error is raised after it (C-6.12) (incident: 2026-09-26, at load 110 to 170 with 48 jobs queued, a turn waited about 725 s to be placed and ran in about 20 s; `why` said no pass had reached it 12 minutes in). The two passes never look at the same job, each keeps its own holds and freed-lease record, placements are noted for C-6.11 as each is made, and C-6.3's reservation finds its job no attempt in flight. With no turn queued and none held by the last turn pass, the turn pass is one indexed statement, so offering it every tick costs a starved daemon nothing it needs. Within a pass, turn jobs sort ahead of detached jobs of the same tier; a turn that is not placed never enters the detached jobs' waiters, holds none of them `behind-older-job`, and causes no `slot-kept`. Among turns, C-6.9's hold applies only while the policy sets a turn cap: with neither cap set (the default), an unplaced turn holds no later turn `behind-older-job` or `slot-kept`, since no turn slot exists for a later turn to take (review of 5549d87d: an older turn waiting on its closed pinned lane, or on a checkout another turn held, kept a third conversation's turn waiting while other lanes were free). What an older turn waits for is kept for it lease by lease instead: a later turn that needs a lease an older turn of the pass is queued for is held `lease-held`, the lease under `queued` and the older turn under `queued_behind`, capped or not, and one that needs none of them is not held. A key a turn needs free but never takes (`worktree:<target>`, held by a detached writer or retention, C-24.5) is not queued for: every turn waiting on it may start when it is released. A turn skipped on its clock keeps its place for the keys its last hold named under `leases` or `queued`. Turns trigger no admission probe; a lane whose verdict would require one is not a candidate for a turn (`probe-required`). Turns have their own capacity, counted apart from detached jobs: at most `conversations.max_active_turns` turns at once across the fleet and `conversations.turn_slots_per_lane` on one lane, each null by default, which is no cap (2026-09-27; until then 3 and 1), and a positive whole number when the policy sets one; detached jobs count only detached attempts against `max_active_attempts` and `max_in_flight_per_lane`, and a full pool holds back only its own kind, so an attended turn never waits for a background job's slot (the installed daemon's first turn, 2026-09-24, waited on `no-slot` on every open Claude lane while detached jobs held them). The slot leases are numbered apart too: a turn takes the lowest free `lane:<lane id>:slot:turn-<n>`, a detached attempt `lane:<lane id>:slot:<n>` from n = 1 (C-6.9), so no attempt holds `lane:<lane id>:slot:0`, the lease a detached job's admission probe takes (C-11.4). With one numbering, the turn pass, which runs first, gave each new turn `slot:0`, and an older writable job waiting on a probe of that lane stayed `probe-pending` through ten turns in a row with no probe (review of d04b8b3; e053b2c had done the same to a job of the turn's own tier). A probe may therefore run beside a live turn on its lane, as it already could whenever the turn held another slot; while it runs it keeps every job off the lane, a turn too. A turn held off a lane by a detached job's admission probe waits for exactly that lease, whenever the probe began: the turn pass counts admission probes' leases (never timer probes', which come and go every cycle) among those whose release is freed capacity (C-6.10), adding the probes a held turn's decision names, so the next turn pass looks at the turn as soon as the probe ends, not on its backed-off clock (review of 5d14f98). The single pass of e053b2c ended every probe before a turn behind it was looked at. Turns are the `attended` class (C-6.9), the first placed, and the machine guard never holds one (C-6.13). Tool approvals wait without a time limit by default: policy `conversations.approval_wait_s` defaults to null (no limit), including when omitted. A positive finite number of seconds remains supported; a turn waiting longer on a tool approval is then stopped with reason `approval-timeout`, never answered automatically. The former 3600-second default protected turn capacity that became uncapped by default on 2026-09-27. A question (approval kind `question`, Claude's AskUserQuestion) always waits for its answer with no limit, even when a tool-approval limit is set (2026-09-28: an agent's question left overnight stopped its turn at the hour and blocked the conversation behind an unfinished-turn banner, with the person's later messages queued behind it). [milestone 9; owner fake; module `subfleet/scheduler.py`, `subfleet/daemon.py`]
- **C-26.10** (attended workspaces) A conversation's workspace may be outside git. A checkout on `main` or `master` requires the conversation's `allow_main`, set by a person (C-25.6). A writable turn (Claude `accept-edits` or `bypass`, a writable Codex turn) is refused (exit 7) when its workspace equals or contains the state root, `~/.claude`, `~/.codex`, an enrolled lane home, or the guard hook's directory, each side compared in its one spelling (`folders.canonical`, C-6.5), so a workspace or a `HOME` typed in another case than the volume stores is still seen to contain it (review of 5e9f2fbd, P3-4). Turn jobs write no salvage ref, at finalization or when a quarantined turn is released (C-5.7); the receipt records HEAD before and after, and a writable turn's working tree is written as tree objects at its start and end, with no ref (C-26.14). C-13.1 and C-13.2 keep binding every job that is not a turn. A worktree conversation (design D-16) is cut by `conversation.create` on branch `subfleet/<conversation id>` at `<state root>/worktrees/conversation-<conversation id>`; the store's update records the worktree on its row (path, branch, source directory, repository, base commit), every conversation view returns it, and the conversation's workspace is the worktree (the same subdirectory when the person picked one). Repeating the create with the same request id finishes a cut that failed or was interrupted, adopting a worktree already on that branch; until then the conversation refuses `message.submit` (`worktree-missing`) and never dispatches in the checkout it was to be cut from. [milestone 9; owner fake; module `subfleet/conversations/dispatch.py`, `subfleet/conversations/service.py`]
- **C-26.11** (the never-rules hook applies to turns) Every Claude turn outside `read-only` passes `--settings` with `"disableAllHooks": false`. Every Codex turn runs only after the C-14.2 preflight and the same-server `hooks/list` check of design D-10, and carries every guard flag an exec launch carries (C-23.6). Codex turns are `read-only` until a live test shows the hook denying a forbidden command and an `apply_patch` in an app-server turn. The Codex thread is opened read-only and a turn leaves no project-trust entry in a lane's `config.toml`. [milestone 9; owner live; module `subfleet/conversations/claude_turn.py`, `codex_turn.py`]
- **C-26.12** (turn jobs are the conversation's) A turn job creates no caller notice, exports no deliverable, and is retained under its own budget (policy `retention.turn_jobs`, default 2,000 jobs, and `retention.turn_bytes`, 4 GiB), separate from C-8.4's, so neither kind of job is pruned to make room for the other: pinned while its message is non-terminal, its conversation blocked, or a live runner reads its attempt (the conversation service answers, and retention asks again inside each delete transaction), and for `retention.turn_keep_days` (14) after it ends (review IR-17). It is not detached work: `list` leaves turn jobs out unless the request names a `kind` (`turn` lists only turns) or sets `include_turns`, and refuses a malformed filter (exit 2), so `runs`, `wait --mine` and `wait --last` never resolve to one; an explicit job id still reaches it, and `runs reap` still reconciles it. `status` shows live turns under their own line and heading, never as running or waiting jobs, and `why` names a turn as its conversation's. The daemon advertises the filter as the capability `jobs.kind.v1` (C-25.1), and `runs` sends `kind` and `include_turns` only after `capabilities` listed it. Against a daemon that does not (one older than `capabilities` answers "unknown op"), `runs --kind` exits 69 naming the restart without asking for the list, `runs --include-turns` sends neither field because such a daemon lists every kind, and `runs` drops the turn rows it returns (every row carries `kind`) and says on stderr that the list may be short when `--last` was applied first. `runs` also drops any row whose `kind` contradicts the request, whatever the daemon advertised. [milestone 9; owner unit; module `subfleet/retention.py`, `subfleet/conversations/service.py`, `subfleet/daemon.py`, `subfleet/cli.py`, `subfleet/offline.py`, `subfleet/render.py`]
- **C-26.13** (a conversation's session is not the sessions kit's) Inside a process Subfleet launched, one whose environment carries a C-5.1 marker (`SUBFLEET_ATTEMPT` or `SUBFLEET_JOB`), `subfleet hook SessionStart` hands no wake to the sessions kit, and `SessionStart` and `UserPromptSubmit` surface and mark `surfaced` (C-15.3) only the notices that name a job (C-15.1); a notice that names no job (a `ping`, a sessions-kit nudge, a timer alert, an imported v1 continuation) is neither printed nor marked there and stays pending. Every launch path sets `SUBFLEET_ATTEMPT`; the guardian passes the markers to the provider, and Claude Code passes them to its command hooks (observed with 2.1.280, new and resumed sessions, `-p` and stream-json input). A job that `subfleet run` submits from a turn's Bash tool names the session in `CLAUDE_CODE_SESSION_ID` as its caller (`cli.session_id`), the conversation's, so when it ends after the turn its notice reaches the conversation's next turn by this layer: `PostToolUse` (C-15.2 layer 2), which runs there unchanged, as does `PreToolUse`'s attached-runner guard (C-23.54), waits only on jobs still running, and a turn's process does not outlive its result by more than C-26.5 allows. The `sessions` op's `state` reports `conversation_sessions`: every native session id a conversation binds, archived or not, and every one a turn attempt ran; `lane_sessions` (C-23.31) still leaves turns out, because a turn may resume a session Subfleet did not create and is no lane run. A `state` reply without `conversation_sessions` comes from a daemon older than this clause, which may already run conversations (`PROTOCOL_VERSION` stays 1, C-25.1): every sessions-kit verb that reads it exits 69 with the fix `subfleet daemon stop && subfleet daemon start`, as for an op the daemon does not know, and never reads the silence as an empty list. The sessions kit never lists, nudges, revives, cold-sweeps or hands off one of these sessions, whatever `--all`, `--revive`, `--force` or `--dry-run` say. A request that names only such sessions, or only such sessions and headless lane runs (C-23.31), is refused (exit 7, under `--json` too) by `sessions continue` in every scope (v1's `tickle`, `muster` and `revive` spellings included), by `sessions revive` and by `handoff`, with the reason, checked before C-23.31's lane check, and the fix to continue it in the Subfleet app; a request that also names other sessions acts on those and reports each refused one with its reason. A cold handoff checks each canonical session id against the daemon's lists again at handoff, and a refusal there holds only that session. The daemon refuses the same continuations whatever client asks: a `resume` whose source job is a turn or whose native session is one of these, and a `revive` of one, are refused at submit (exit 7) with that fix and nothing stored, and one accepted before its session became a conversation's is failed with exit 7 by the admission pass that would have launched it, before any lease or attempt; nor does it record a nudge of one of these sessions. Every one of these checks, the kit's and the daemon's, compares a UUID whatever its case on either side: the daemon's list, a registry row, a transcript's name, a conversation's binding, a turn attempt's record and the request may each spell it in upper or lower case. A retry of a request the daemon already accepted is answered as C-6.2 answers any retry (its job id, exit 0; exit 2 for another payload), not refused: the refusal at submit applies only to a request id no job holds. Session ownership uses that same canonical native identity, including brace, urn:uuid: and 32-hex UUID spellings, and is re-read inside the reservation transaction after preparation, refusing a newly bound resume or revive before leases or attempts. A public native open that creates a binding refuses a session whose native: lease is held by a live job (including a quarantined attempt after its job becomes lost); its SQL check and binding serialize with main-store reservation, closing the reservation-to-launch window. [milestone 9; owner fake; module `subfleet/hooks.py`, `subfleet/sessions/`, `subfleet/daemon.py`]
- **C-26.14** (a turn's changes) A writable turn (any permission but `read-only`) whose workspace is a git checkout with a commit has two snapshots of its working tree, each a tree object written through a temporary index by the C-6.8 snapshot, with no ref and with HEAD, the real index and the files untouched: the start is the one admission takes (the attempt's `baseline_tree`); the end is taken at finalization before the turn's leases are released, or when a quarantined turn is confirmed dead, and a forced release with writers still live records that it has none. Both are kept per attempt in the conversation store (schema 2; an older store is migrated in place, a newer one refused) and in the attempt's receipt, which also lists the nested repositories with no commit that the end snapshot left out (C-13.1); recording the end writes one `conversation.watch` row for the message with `state` null, which, as for an approval asked or answered, tells a client to fetch the message again. While git fails transiently the end is tried at most three times in one daemon run (a restart counts again), and once when a quarantined turn is released (an operator's request is not offered again); then the failure is recorded and finalization goes on without an end snapshot. `turn.diff` compares a message's latest turn attempt's start and end snapshots (the attempt recorded last: C-24.5 runs one turn of a conversation at a time) or, before the end exists, its start with the working tree now, saying so (`to.live`); `conversation.diff` compares the start of the conversation's first writable turn (the first recorded) with the working tree now. A `to` that is the working tree now also lists the nested repositories with no commit that its snapshot left out (`to.skipped`, C-13.1), which the diff therefore does not show; a stored end's list is in the turn's receipt and evidence. Both run on the file pool (C-25.3), use git's plumbing with no external diff driver or textconv filter, list at most 1,000 changed files with status and line counts (the stats count every changed file, not only those listed; if git's listing passes 4 MiB they count the files it listed, say `complete: false`, and `files_truncated` is true) and at most 512 KiB of unified diff (the UTF-8 text returned, measured after decoding and scrubbing) cut at a line with `truncated: true`, scrub the diff text (C-25.5) one line at a time, a hunk's line without its `+`, `-` or space prefix, including private-key lines that a hunk's edge, the cut, or a hunk header's function context separates from the armour line at either end, each replaced in its line so every hunk keeps its line counts, and take `path` only relative to the checkout's top level, naming a file or a directory (then every changed file under it) (exit 2 otherwise). With nothing to compare they answer `available: false` in the same shape with a reason (`no-turn`, `read-only-turn`, `no-snapshot`, `snapshot-failed`, `snapshot-pruned`, or `workspace-gone` when git can no longer open the workspace as a checkout, which is never reported as a pruned snapshot). Neither commits, stages, checks out, or writes a ref. Neither claims that one conversation made what it shows (2026-09-29; conversations share folders, C-24.5): a turn's window runs from just before admission began its start snapshot (the attempt's `baseline_at`) to its recorded end (open until then; an attempt that ended with no end recorded, such as one never launched after its runner recorded the start, or a row an earlier build left open, is closed by the service's next tick at the attempt's `finished_at`, a whole-second stamp taken to that second's last millisecond, and the marks its closed window no longer meets are dropped on both sides, each message whose marks changed getting a `conversation.watch` row with `state` null; a quarantined attempt has not ended, since its writers may still be live and it still holds its folder, so its window stays open until its release records the end; a row whose job retention has removed is closed when found, keeping its marks; review of 5e9f2fbd, P3-5), in its folder (C-6.5's write target, the attempt's `folder`), both kept per attempt (`turn_trees` columns `window_start` and `target`, additive, with no schema step; a row written before them keeps its workspace as its folder). Whenever a writable turn's snapshots are recorded, in the same transaction, it and every writable turn of another conversation in that folder whose window meets its own (closed windows; an open end reaches on) are marked as sharing it, each in the other's `shared_json`, and a turn marked later (one whose window began before an ended turn's end but was recorded after) gets a `conversation.watch` row with `state` null. So a turn is marked exactly when another conversation's writable turn's window met its own, whatever order the starts and ends are recorded in (I4, tested as a property over generated schedules in `tests/unit/test_turn_overlap.py`); two turns of one conversation never overlap (I2) and never mark each other. `turn.diff` carries `shared`: one entry per such conversation, `{conversation_id, title, message_ids, from, to}` (its title now; `to` null while one of its turns still runs); `conversation.diff` carries the other conversations whose writable turns in the folder reached into the time since its first writable turn's window began. The app says so above that diff (C-29.10). `shared` names conversations only: no detached writer holds the folder while a turn does, but one that released it between the turn's start snapshot and its reservation can leave edits in the diff that it does not name (C-24.5). [milestone 9; owner fake; module `subfleet/conversations/diff.py`, `subfleet/daemon.py`, `subfleet/conversations/service.py`; design D-25]

## 27. Approvals

- **C-27.1** (real requests, bound replies) A provider permission or question request becomes one approval bound to its attempt and the provider's request id, with the exact request stored (0600) and its SHA-256, a random nonce, and options from design §8; the message becomes `approval-needed`. The approval, its `approval.requested` event and that move commit in one transaction, so no reader sees the event while `approval.list` lacks the approval. The display includes every field that changes what is granted. Approval views expose `provider_request_id`, and the same id as `request_id` (C-27.5), so event cards join by message and exact provider request, never by kind or display alone. A view from a daemon older than both fields gets an independent actionable card keyed by immutable approval id; unmatched event cards have no answer controls, and the daemon's whole pending list withdraws them rather than transferring their drafts (C-27.5; `docs/desktop/app-needs.md` 5). `approval.respond` requires the approval id, the nonce, the request SHA-256 the person saw, a live attempt, and a decision among the options; a reply to an answered, withdrawn, or mismatched approval is exit 2, and a duplicate of the recorded reply returns it unchanged. Before `approval.get` returns a request, it reads a private regular file through a no-follow descriptor, bounds it by the relay frame cap, and verifies the returned bytes against the recorded request SHA-256; changed bytes or an unsafe file are refused. [milestone 9; owner fake; module `subfleet/conversations/approvals.py`]
- **C-27.2** (never granted by Subfleet) No approval is answered without a person's `approval.respond` (C-25.6). No answer grants more than the provider asked: no permission updates, execpolicy or network amendments, and no one-tap allow where the provider requires interaction. The only change Subfleet makes to a request's input is adding `answers` to a question. Tool approvals offer Allow and Deny on the inline conversation card, with details and an optional note available on request; masked values retain the explicit review guard. AskUserQuestion stays inline, with option buttons and number keys 1–9 while choices are focused, multiple selections when `multiSelect` is true, Other free text, and Skip. Multiple questions step forward and back through preserved drafts, then send one combined `answers` map keyed by question text; multiple choices are joined with comma and space, skipped keys are omitted, and skipping every question denies the request without stopping the turn. Sending a composer message leaves a pending question and its draft answers intact. [milestone 9; owner unit; module `subfleet/conversations/claude_turn.py`, `codex_turn.py`]
- **C-27.3** (approvals outlive the daemon) After a daemon restart pending approvals are re-derived by replay and remain answerable; a request the provider re-announces is matched by its id. A stopped, ended, or timed-out turn withdraws its pending approvals. [milestone 9; owner process; module `subfleet/conversations/approvals.py`]
- **C-27.4** (unsupported requests are refused, visibly) Provider requests Subfleet does not support get the refusal of design §8 or JSON-RPC -32601 and an `error` event; credential requests are never answered with a credential. [milestone 9; owner unit; module `subfleet/conversations/claude_turn.py`, `codex_turn.py`]
- **C-27.5** (a waiting approval is within reach) While a card in the focused conversation is pending, the app shows the strip pinned above the composer, and the strip carries a Review button that opens the oldest pending card (by when it was asked), labelled with the count when several wait; with none pending there is no Review. A question is answered on its inline card (C-27.2), so for a question Review brings that card into view rather than opening the request sheet, which has no form for its answers. Answering the card it opens moves Review to the next, so every pending card is reachable from the strip without scrolling. A card is joined to its approval by the provider's request id, which `approval.list` and `conversation.open` carry as `request_id`, so Review opens the approval of the card it shows or says it is no longer pending. A card is not pending once its message has ended or its delivery is unknown (C-27.3), or once the daemon's whole pending set no longer lists its approval or cannot identify its event card. Legacy views remain reachable on independent approval-id cards (C-27.1). Once the conversation has read its event log to the end after opening, it scrolls each new pending card into view once, when it appears, wherever the person was reading; it brings the oldest into view when the conversation is opened, when the person asks (the strip's Review, or the sidebar's hand badge, which opens its conversation), and once more when the first page of older history that opening loads adds rows above it. The timeline shows turns in the order they began and every message still queued below them, in the order the daemon sends the queue (C-24.8, C-26.7), and follows the newest row of the turn that began last. [milestone 9; owner frontend; module `app/Sources/Timeline.swift`, `app/Sources/UIWindow.swift`, `subfleet/conversations/service.py`]

## 28. Attachments and drafts

- **C-28.1** (attachments by content) `attachment.add` copies a regular file owned by the daemon's user, opened without following symlinks, at most 20 MiB, with PNG, JPEG, GIF, or WebP magic bytes, into `attachments/<sha256>.<ext>` (0600, fsync) through a temporary file of its own (a random name created exclusively, drawn again if taken), which it renames into place or, when the copy fails, removes if the directory still lets it, and verifies the copy's hash. It copies whenever that name does not hold the daemon's own copy, a regular file of that size with those bytes, owned by the daemon's user, with no second link and no group or other mode bits (so none, other bytes, one it cannot read, a symlink, which it does not follow, a FIFO, a hard link another name writes through, or a copy others can read), so adding an image again repairs a changed stored copy, and the row names the copy where it now is; a copy gone when it is verified is `copy-mismatch`, to try again. It fsyncs `attachments/` after the rename and before the row, as C-8.1 publishes, also when it finds the copy already in place. Adds of the same bytes at once each return the same receipt and leave one copy. A message names at most 8 attachments by hash; when a turn is submitted, the driver re-checks each stored copy the same way, reading at most one byte past its size, never following a symlink and never blocking on a FIFO. At final frame construction, an undelivered image is resolved by digest in the current state root, and its expected size, SHA-256, owner, private mode and link count are verified on the same descriptor whose bytes are used. Claude receives those verified bytes; Codex receives a separate 0400 attempt-local copy published from those bytes. A missing or invalid image ends the driver explicitly as `attachment-missing` and is reconciled, never left running by a payload-read exception. [milestone 9; owner unit; module `subfleet/conversations/attachments.py`]
- **C-28.2** (retention) `message.submit` updates each attachment's last use. An attachment is deleted only in a transaction that re-checks that no non-terminal message references it and it is 30 days unused, then unlinked. Copies inside attempt directories follow turn-job retention (C-26.12). [milestone 9; owner unit; module `subfleet/retention.py`]
- **C-28.3** (drafts and sends survive the app) The app journals drafts and every mutating op with its idempotency key under its Application Support directory (0600) before acting on it, keeps at most one `message.submit` outstanding per conversation in journal order, and resends unacknowledged ops with their original keys after a restart. [milestone 9; owner frontend; module `app/Sources/ConversationStore.swift`]

## 29. The desktop client

- **C-29.1** (an explicit endpoint) The app connects only to `<SUBFLEET_HOME or ~/.subfleet>/daemon.sock` and reads only `status.json` and `catalog.json` there. It never runs a v1 executable, never reads the v1 state directory, never starts a broker, and never falls back to either. [milestone 9; owner frontend; module `app/Sources/DaemonClient.swift`]
- **C-29.2** (unavailable is a state) A daemon that is down, lacks `capabilities`, or reports another conversation schema shows an actionable status; drafts stay editable and journaled ops stay queued. A daemon that answers busy (exit 69, C-16.7) is shown as busy, never as incompatible, and a send it answered busy stays queued and goes again under its key. The first `conversation.watch` answered after one failed checks `capabilities` again, so a check answered busy never leaves the app waiting. Reloading `status.json` is labelled as reloading a snapshot, never as a new observation (C-18.1). [milestone 9; owner frontend; module `app/Sources/`]
- **C-29.3** (a normal app) The app has a main window with standard window controls, a Dock icon, the standard menus, reopens its window from the Dock, and keeps the menu bar quota panel with an always-present "Open Subfleet" action. [milestone 9; owner frontend; module `app/Sources/SubfleetApp.swift`]
- **C-29.4** (a separate development identity) A development build uses bundle id `org.maxghenis.subfleet.dev` and refuses to run against `~/.subfleet`; `app/build.sh` never writes into `/Applications`. [milestone 9; owner frontend; module `app/build.sh`]
- **C-29.5** (`sessions continue` keeps its meaning) C-17.1's `sessions continue` remains interruption recovery; sending a message is `message.submit`. [milestone 9; owner unit; module `subfleet/sessions/cli.py`]
- **C-29.6** (per-account windows, conversations apart) `status.json` publishes, per Claude account, the five-hour, weekly and every model-scoped weekly window (including Fable) with percent, reset time and evidence label, from `provider` and `stale-provider` readings only, and the app shows them. The windows are one list keyed by (`scope`, `window`), the key readings are stored under (C-9.8, C-9.9), so a model-scoped window never replaces the account window of the same name; the older per-account `probe` and `live` fields read scope `account` only. `claude.earliest_reset` is the soonest future reset of an account window on a lane admission could use (enabled, `owner: v2`, not the desktop login, its identity not mismatched: C-10.6). Every job row carries `kind`; turn jobs are left out of `jobs` (C-18.2) and shown in a `conversations` section: counts of active conversations (a message queued or live), conversations needing approval and blocked conversations; live turn jobs by state; and at most 20 conversations that are active, need approval or are blocked, each with id, provider, title, state, pending approvals and its live turn job. The section is read from `conversations.sqlite3` on the timer thread that publishes the file, through a read-only connection of its own and never the store's lock, a request thread or the control loop; a store that could not be read (a SQLite or an OS error, a newer schema) or a reader that failed is `available: false` with the error's name and no counts, never zeros, and never stops the file being written (C-18.1). The menu groups turn work by conversation. Shape: design §12. [milestone 9; owner frontend; module `subfleet/status_json.py`, `subfleet/conversations/store.py`, `subfleet/timers.py`, `app/Sources/`]
- **C-29.7** (the composer) Return sends; Shift-Return and Option-Return insert a newline; IME composition is respected; pasted and dropped images become attachments; the composer stays editable while a turn runs. While a live turn takes steers (`steer.v1` for the provider; the turn running or asking, not stopping, its outcome not yet known; the conversation’s permission not narrower than the turn’s), Return steers the message into that turn and ⌘Return queues it for later, as Claude Code does; the send button is then a split button, primary “Steer” with “Queue for later” and “Stop” in its menu, and when the composer’s picks differ from the turn’s a one-line hint says a steer runs under the turn’s settings. `/` commands and `!` shell input always queue. There is no Tab action and no action on a queued bubble. A steered message is a sent bubble with a read state from the turn it joins: “Unread until Claude’s next step.” while the model works, “Unread until the current step finishes.” while a tool runs, “Unread. Claude needs your approval first.”, and “Unread until the current turn ends.” once it missed the turn (it runs next); at its host’s `steer.delivered` it is drawn there, once, and reads “Read”; a Codex steer recorded after the model’s last step reads “Read after the turn’s last step; ask again for a reply” A refused steer stays queued and its status line says why; one the daemon answers `not-steerable` (the turn's runner not taken back yet or still catching up after a restart, the provider not ready) is asked again briefly first when it names its turn (`into`), at 0.5 s doubling within the same five tries a lost answer gets, holding that conversation's later sends meanwhile (at most 7.5 s), since `into` has the daemon refuse any try that comes after that turn ended. Esc while a turn runs takes back the latest unread steer, withdrawn from the outbox while the daemon may not have it, else with `message.cancel` (never `turn.interrupt`): its words and images return to the composer and to the conversation’s draft, so they survive leaving the conversation or quitting. A steer the daemon answers `too-late` for is passed over only while the app still sees it where it was then (steering in that turn, or shown just as it was); the words say whether the next Esc takes back an older or a newer steer or stops the turn. With no steer to take back, Esc stops the turn. Stop keeps unread steers and queued messages: they run next. A Stop of a queued message whose withdrawal the daemon answers `too-late` (it left the queue meanwhile) reads its status before sending `turn.interrupt`, and interrupts only a message it reads as its own turn’s (waiting, starting, running or asking): a steering or steered message is not interrupted (an interrupt of a steering message stops its host, C-24.7), one queued again or settled is left alone, and a status it cannot read is reported, with nothing interrupted. A journaled steer names the turn it was sent to (`into`). [milestone 9; owner frontend; module `app/Sources/UIComposer.swift`, `app/Sources/ConversationStore.swift`, `app/Sources/Timeline.swift`, `app/Sources/UIModel.swift`]
- **C-29.8** (bounded rendering) Conversation Markdown renders with bounded cost: long messages and code blocks are truncated with a control to expand, and history loads in pages. A page ends after a whole transcript row, so a row's blocks are never split; its cursor is that row's byte offset from the start of the file, which later turns never move, and each page's cursor is below the last. A page reads at most 4 MiB of rows below its cursor (or the one row there, up to 68 MiB, the cap plus 64 MiB, when that row is larger) and up to 8 MiB past the cursor for its calls' results; a page that reaches that cap, or a row too large to read, hands on a cursor; only the file's start ends the history, and the page that reaches the file's first row (after any blank lines) ends it. A page that adds nothing to the timeline but hands on a cursor is followed at once, up to 16 pages per "Load earlier"; opening a conversation reads one page and follows none. [milestone 9; owner frontend; module `app/Sources/Markdown.swift`, `app/Sources/UIModel.swift`, `subfleet/conversations/history.py`]
- **C-29.9** (one feed and notifications) The app long-polls one `conversation.watch` and `conversation.events` only for the focused conversation; both run on a dedicated daemon pool with at most one of each per client, never on the pool `message.submit` uses. A turn that completes, fails, needs approval, or becomes `delivery-unknown` while unfocused posts a notification; the Dock badge counts pending approvals. An Allow once notification action is offered only for one identified, pending, unmasked tool request with an `allow` option; responding rechecks its message, conversation, approval id, request hash, state and masking. A stale or masked request opens the conversation for review. [milestone 9; owner fake; module `subfleet/conversations/events.py`, `app/Sources/`]
- **C-29.10** (changes and detached work) The app shows each turn's and each conversation's changes (`turn.diff`, `conversation.diff`); above a diff whose `shared` names other conversations it says that the folder was also changed by them during the turn (or since the conversation's first turn began), each by its title (one line, at most 60 characters, cut with an ellipsis; untitled when blank) and whether it still runs, and that the diff may include their edits (C-26.14; `diffSharedWords`). It never commits, pushes or merges on its own, and keeps a Compose view for detached jobs that previews with `submit {dry_run: true}` and dispatches with `submit`. [milestone 9; owner frontend; module `app/Sources/`]
- **C-29.11** (launch at login and honest states) "Start at login" uses `SMAppService`. Capacity waiting, fleet-full, external writer, authentication failure, stale usage, an unsupported setting, and a model mismatch each have their own visible state, distinct from completion; a waiting message's status is its `state_reason` (C-24.4) in words (`TurnTimeline.waitingWords`), its kind read from the reason's start (an external writer is `external-writer: …` or `readmit:external-writer`), never from text inside it, which may quote a conversation's title (review of 5e9f2fbd, P2-1), only a `capacity:` reason reads "Waiting for capacity", and a waiting message whose daemon gave no reason reads as such, never as capacity (2026-09-29); a capability the daemon does not offer is disabled with its reason. [milestone 9; owner frontend; module `app/Sources/`]
- **C-29.12** (search from the keyboard) ⌘K, and File > Search…, open a palette over the main window (⌘K again closes it) that finds conversations and native sessions whatever the sidebar's search text and provider filter. A candidate matches when every word of the query appears, ignoring case, diacritics and width, in its title, its workspace as the sidebar shows it, its provider, or its messages' text: the person's messages, the answers and the transcript rows of every conversation whose timeline the app holds (those opened since it started; not thoughts, tool calls, errors or notices), and each session's first prompt. After a pause in typing, `conversation.list` with `query` (the query's longest word) adds sessions from the whole catalog, past the page the sidebar loaded, which the daemon matches by title, folder and first prompt; sessions a conversation already continues are left out. Results rank the exact title first (white space aside), then titles holding every word, then titles, workspaces and providers holding them, then message matches, then fuzzy titles: a word found nowhere, of two or more letters, has them in the title in order within three times its length ("sbflt" in "Subfleet"), as Claude Code's palette matches titles, and the query's other words are found as above; within each, the most recent activity first, undated last, then title and id, so the order never depends on the order candidates arrive in. Matching folds text (case folding, canonical decomposition with the marks removed, and width folding) once, keeping unchanged messages' folds between openings, so a word matches part of a character folding makes several of ("s" in "ß"). Former titles are not kept by the daemon or the app, so a renamed conversation is found by its current title only. Matched words (a fuzzy title's letters) are highlighted, by the same folding, and a message match shows who wrote the message holding the most of its message-only words (the newest such), on one line, cut around the first match with "…" where it was cut; opening it scrolls the conversation to that message. An empty query lists everything by recent activity. After the results the palette offers to start a new conversation (the sheet File > New conversation opens), selected when nothing matches. Up and Down (Control-P and Control-N, Tab and Shift-Tab) move the selection, Page Up and Page Down by eight, Return (or ⌘↩) opens it, and the composer takes the keys once the conversation shows (a locked session has none); Escape or a click outside closes the palette and gives the keys back to where they were. While it is open, the window behind it takes no clicks or shortcuts; the menus still work, and File > New conversation closes it to open its sheet. At most 50 results are listed and the rest counted; words past twelve are counted and not searched. [milestone 9; owner frontend; module `app/Sources/SearchPalette.swift`, `app/Sources/UISearchPalette.swift`]
- **C-29.13** (readable text) Conversation text (the timeline, its Markdown and code blocks, the composer, the sidebar's rows and provider badges, the conversation header, status lines and served chips, tool rows and approval cards and sheets, the Changes pane, and the search palette) is set in reading sizes larger than the system's, except three small controls other changes were rewriting (the Stop links beside a turn's status and on the live strip, and the sidebar's approval count), which stay at the system's caption size until those land: at actual size body 16 pt (Claude Code's Large; the system's body is 13), headings 24, 20 and 17, secondary prose 15, code 14.5, metadata 13 and 12. macOS's text styles do not follow Dynamic Type, so the sizes are points times one scale. View > Bigger (⌘+, and ⌘= in the window), Smaller (⌘−) and Actual size (⌘0) step it as Claude Code's zoom does, half a zoom level (×1.2^½) at a time, from 1.2^−1 (83 %) to 1.2^5 (249 %); it persists in UserDefaults under `conversationTextScale`, where a value outside that range reads as its nearest bound and one that is not a number as actual size. The conversation column's widest (56 times the body size, 896 pt at actual size) and a message bubble's (40 times) grow with it. At every scale no size is below 10 pt, metadata stays smaller than body, and code lies between them. [milestone 9; owner frontend; module `app/Sources/TextScale.swift`, `app/Sources/UIReading.swift`]

## 30. Catalog and legacy continuity

- **C-30.1** (catalog out of process) Discovery of existing native sessions runs in a separate process with a 20 s wall-clock cap per run, writes `catalog.json` atomically with titles and prompts scrubbed, re-reads only files whose size or mtime changed, indexes every session (exclusions of lane runs and Codex exec or subagent rollouts come before any limit), and never writes a native store. The daemon never scans the Claude projects tree or Codex session trees on a request or control thread. The conversation service starts a run every `conversations.catalog_interval_s` (default 60 s; 0 means only on `catalog.refresh`) and on request, never waits for one, and stops a run still alive after 60 s by its process group. Before each file it publishes, a run checks that the service that started it is still open and that the state root it locked is still there: it inherits the read end of a pipe (the fence, above descriptor 2) whose only write end the service holds, and reads end-of-file there once the service closes or dies; and the lock it holds must still be `<root>/catalog.lock`. It publishes by path and never creates a root, so a root removed or replaced between a check and the write after it can fail that write (the run then exits 1) or receive it. A run that finds the service gone or the root removed or replaced at a check publishes nothing more and exits 3; one whose fence descriptor is not a pipe writes nothing and exits 4; one that finds another run holding the lock exits 0. A probe of the lock (`conversation.list`'s `refreshing`, the check before a run starts) takes it shared, and a run tries for it for up to a second before it leaves the catalog to the run holding it, so a probe is never taken for a run.
